Anna heard, with her suddenly awakened mind’s ear, her own boisterous voice crying, “Oh, Mrs. Butters, most cheerfully I accept.﻿ ﻿…” Too soon﻿—too soon﻿—and now too late remembered. To remember the sound of her own voice was almost always, for poor Anna, to hear a sort of bugle call calling to retreat﻿—and retreat was always, alas, by then impossible. Every battle was always fought and lost by the time she heard that dreaded call.

“Take the creature,” she said to Seryozha in a broken voice. “Let it die if you like, or take it back to the mission.” She went out, loaded with sadness, to cook the supper.

===Chinese===

“你这缝纫，一个子儿也不值，”老谢尔盖说，“更别说一只小羊了。传教士们给你钱，纯粹是慈善——他们知道我瞎了，养不活老婆孩子，不像从前了。”
谢廖沙发出一声粗鲁的怪响。
“那么为什么，”老谢尔盖不依不饶——“传教士们凭什么蠢到两日元之外还搭一只小羊？我就不信他们给了。”
“咦，小羊这不是在这儿。摸摸，”安娜说。“依你看，它是怎么来的？难道是我偷的？”
“天知道它怎么来的。天知道你下一步还要干什么。我怎么知道你不是偷的？咱们败落到这步田地，什么也不叫我惊奇了。要不，是巴特斯太太说着玩儿，根本没打算给你，你呢，照例闹了个误会，就把畜生拎回来了。她凭什么给咱们一只臊羊？咱们不要羊；没跟谁讨过羊；它还要把屋子糟蹋脏。什么地方——绅士的起居室里养羊！还有，不用说，我和谢廖沙的晚饭是没影儿的。…哦，不必了！偷来的羊，当然得先喂——先喂你们那偷来的羊，轮不着丈夫和儿子——”
“闭嘴，你这可恶的老头子！”安娜喊，此刻满腔是火，又掺着心虚的慌乱——因为她那个敏感的良心承认：巴特斯太太许下那只小羊，也许本没有让她这样照字面、这样当场兑现的意思。安娜用蓦然惊醒的心之耳，听见了自己那副大嗓门：“哦，巴特斯太太，我欢天喜地收下了。…”太快了——太快了——如今想起，已经太迟。对可怜的安娜来说，记起自己的嗓音，几乎永远等于听见一声撤退的号角——可叹的是，听到那声可怕的号角时，撤退永远已经不可能了。每一仗，总是在她听见号声之前，就打完、打输了。
“拿去，”她哑着嗓子对谢廖沙说。“随它死，随你送回传教士那儿去。”她满怀悲伤地出去做晚饭了。

===Original===

Seryozha took the kid and the cup of milk out into the yard and sat crooning into its winking ear as he held it in his arms and dipped and redipped the rag. “Yoodle-doodle-doo﻿ ﻿… yoodle-doodle-dido,” he sang in a small falsetto voice which the kid seemed to like. The breast of Seryozha’s blouse was soon soaked in milk. The kid’s yellow cynical eyes, slotted with vertical irises, were fixed on him as though it were trying to persuade itself that this was some eccentric relation of its late mother’s.

Old Sergei sat alone in the living-room, his trembling veined old hands clasping and unclasping limply between his knees. Anna came to the door, mixing some egg and flour in a bowl, and said: “You horrid old man﻿ ﻿… you wearisome old man﻿ ﻿… running about honoring strangers and then coming home to break my heart. That kid was given to me﻿—it was.﻿ ﻿…”

“God knows whether it was or not,” said Old Sergei, but under his breath, thus satisfying his honor as a Husband with a Righteous Grievance without speaking loud enough to provoke the violence of his wife. Anna sighed petulantly and noisily and returned to the stove. Old Sergei sat drooping, opening and shutting his blind eyes to remind himself that he could not see. He laid his hand on his throbbing throat, for he craved to feel life always under his hand in order to titillate his fancy about death. He swallowed; his Adam’s apple moved under his hand, and his vague thoughts floated round and round the strangeness of life and death.

He was old, he thought; he was not loved. He loved no one. He felt the breath climbing foolishly up and down the unsteady shaft that was his body, like an imprisoned bird never losing hope of escape. Some day the prisoner would find the loophole and fly from his lips. The sooner the better, thought Old Sergei﻿—or rather he thought that he thought so; life was a curse without serenity. Who could be serene by Anna’s side? He felt as homesick for serenity as though he had once enjoyed it. He believed he had left it behind him in old lost Russia. He believed he would find it again in heaven, which was the only province of lost Russia left to him to visit now.

===Chinese===

谢廖沙抱着小羊和那杯奶到院子里，把它搂在怀里，对着它眨动的耳朵哼哼，把布片蘸了又蘸。“呜嘟——嘟嘟——都…呜嘟——嘟嘟——嘀嘟，”他用小小的假嗓唱着，小羊似乎爱听。谢廖沙衬衫的前襟很快让奶浸透了。小羊那双黄色的、冷眼看世界的眼睛——竖缝的瞳仁——定定望着他，仿佛正努力说服自己：这是它亡母的一门古怪亲戚。
老谢尔盖独自坐在起居室里，青筋毕露的老手哆嗦着，在两膝间松松地握了又松、松了又握。安娜端着一只碗，搅着鸡蛋和面，来到门口，说：“你这可恶的老头子…你这熬人的老头子…满世界跑着敬重外人，回家来伤我的心。那只羊是给我的——就是给我的。…”
“天知道是不是，”老谢尔盖说——可这是屏着气说的；这样，既全了他“怀冤丈夫”的名节，又不至于声音大到招来妻子的武力。安娜气呼呼、响亮地叹口气，回炉子边去了。老谢尔盖菷蔫地坐着，一睁一闭他那双瞎眼，好提醒自己看不见了。他一只手按在自己突突跳的喉咙上；他永远渴望手底下压着一条命，好逗弄他那关于死的想象。他咽了口唾沫；喉结在手掌底下滚动；他那些漫漶的念头，绕着生与死的怪异，一圈一圈打转。
他老了，他想；没人爱他。他谁也不爱。他觉出那口气，顺着身子这根不牢靠的立柱，傻乎乎地爬上爬下，像一只关着的、从不死心的鸟。总有一天，囚徒会寻着那处孔隙，从他唇间飞走。越快越好，老谢尔盖想——或者说，他以为自己这么想；没有了安宁，活着就是一场咒。在安娜身边，谁能安宁？他渴念安宁，像渴念一样曾经拥有过的东西。他相信自己把它丢在了失落的旧俄罗斯。他相信到了天堂还能找着它——如今失落的俄罗斯诸省里，剩给他去走一走的，也只有那一省了。

===Original===

“To die﻿—to die﻿—to die﻿ ﻿…” whispered Old Sergei, enjoying the feeling of tears brimming over the quivering skin of his eye-sockets. He stroked and stroked his too living throat; in his sightless eyes he saw the sad picture of his lovelessness. Clasping his hands together, he laid this sad picture before his God, hoping to soften God’s heart by a prostrate-spirited humility. “I have sinned, O God,” he thought, for his vanity felt defiled by Anna’s reproaches. “I have sinned, I am reproached, so let me die. I am cursed; I am found out﻿ ﻿… all life is too difficult for me, for I belong to a cursed race﻿ ﻿… wretched Russia﻿—exiled and despoiled﻿—a dying race﻿—a reproach to all the nations of the earth among whom we are dispersed.﻿ ﻿… Here am I, a wretched sinner, a reproached sinner, member of a wretched and reproached race. O God, let me die. It’s the only way to make people sorry for me instead of angry with me.﻿ ﻿…” He thought of oblivion as a revenge; death, not penitence, seemed to him the apposite answer to a justified reproach; he had no courage for penitence. He would refuse to be anything more responsible than a pitiful memory in Anna’s mind, and in God’s. “Let my spirit be taken from me﻿ ﻿… let me be dissolved and become earth.﻿ ﻿… Let me go into the everlasting place,” he implored of his God, as a man faced by the irritation of shaving on a cold morning almost decides to go back to bed and sleep the day out.

He was interrupted in this limp ecstasy by hearing Seryozha’s peaceful “Yoodle-doodle-doo,” outside in the yard. He had heard it for some time, but it had not come to the forefront of his attention till now, suddenly, in a pause in Anna’s kitchen clatter.

“Seryozha,” called Old Sergei, “bring me that kid.”

Seryozha, whistling very softly between his teeth, brought the kid, more than half asleep, huddled in a length of sacking, and put it on the floor between his father’s feet. Old Sergei’s hands﻿—tense, as though they expected to find something new﻿—stroked the kid’s hard little brow, the thin ridge of its neck, the harsh hair on its narrow shoulders, the heaving bulging ribs, the upturned hoofs tipping the awkwardly kneeling legs.﻿ ﻿… Old Sergei’s fingers ran up and down the sleepy little animal’s backbone, as though it were an ecstatic instrument.

===Chinese===

“死——死——死…”老谢尔盖低语，享受着泪水漫过眼眶颤抖的皮肤的感觉。他摩挲又摩挲自己那条过分鲜活的喉咙；在失明的眼睛里，他看见自己无人爱的一幅惨画。他双手合十，把这幅惨画陈在他上帝面前，指望拿一副五体投地的谦卑，软化上帝的心。“我犯了罪，上帝啊，”他想——安娜的数落把他的体面弄脏了。“我犯了罪，我挨了骂，那么让我死吧。我受了咒；我被看穿了…活着对我来说太难了，因为我属于一个受咒的种族…可怜的俄罗斯——流亡，劫掠——垂死的种族——散在天下万国之中，做万国的笑柄。…我在这儿，一个可怜的罪人，一个挨骂的罪人，一个可怜而挨骂的种族的一员。上帝啊，让我死吧。要叫人们怜惜我、而不是对我动怒，只有这一条路。…”他把遗忘当报复；面对一场有理的数落，他觉得对题的答话是死，不是悔改；悔改，他没有那份勇气。在安娜心里、在上帝心里，他只肯做一段可怜的回忆，再不承担更多。“求你收回我的魂…求你叫我消解，化为泥土。…求你领我进入那永恒的居所，”他这样恳求他的上帝，像一个人大冷早晨对着刮脸的烦恼，几乎打定主意回床上去、把这一天睡过去。
这份瘫软的狂喜，被院子里谢廖沙平平和和的一声“呜嘟——嘟嘟——都”打断了。那声音他听了一会儿了，只是直到安娜那边锅碗声一歇，才猛地升到注意的前排。
“谢廖沙，”老谢尔盖喊，“把那只羊给我抱来。”
谢廖沙牙齿间轻轻吹着口哨，把小羊抱了来——它已经睡了大半，裹在一截麻袋里缩成一团——放在他父亲两脚之间的地板上。老谢尔盖那双手——紧张地，仿佛指望着摸出什么新东西——掠过小羊硬硬的小额，脖颈细细的棱，窄肩上粗硬的毛，起伏鼓胀的肋，还有翘着的蹄、衬着笨拙跪姿的腿。…老谢尔盖的手指在那睡意沉沉的小牲口的脊梁上上下下跑，仿佛那是一件心醉神迷的乐器。

===Original===

“Seryozha,” said Old Sergei, turning his face down toward the kid as though he could see it, “if I should die﻿ ﻿…”

Emotion checked his speech, and so long was the pause that Seryozha, who was tapping with his foot the already flat corpse of a cockroach in cold abstraction, as though it deserved to die a hundred deaths, was obliged to say, “Oh, nonsense, papa! There’s no reason why you should die﻿ ﻿…”

“There is every reason,” said Old Sergei, feeling a little baffled as Seryozha began again whistling, almost in a whisper, through his teeth﻿—an unsuitable obbligato to a talk on death. “No one values my presence here﻿—still less do I value it myself. I am a weariness to those around me and to myself.﻿ ﻿…”

“You don’t really think that, papa,” said Seryozha. His father, with some surprise, took this as an affectionate filial disclaimer of his proposition. Really Seryozha meant his remark quite literally. He knew that his father did not mean his statement that the necessity for him to remain in the world was now at an end. “Nobody believes that,” thought Seryozha, “however much they may say so. Papa’s world wouldn’t be there if he weren’t there. My world wouldn’t if I weren’t. This cockroach’s isn’t, now it’s dead. So none of us really thinks our world can do without us. I’m sure it had never before occurred to this cockroach that its world could do without it﻿—that anybody could wish it dead. Its vanity was all comfortable inside itself﻿—it felt valuable. When it saw my foot coming, it thought﻿—‘an unnatural accident is happening to a noble and unreplaceable cockroach﻿—*me!*’ That’s what papa thinks.” Seryozha scanned his father’s rather tiresome face, his leaking eyes, nose, mouth, dispassionately. “Poor old ass,” thought Seryozha. “His vanity’s a bit uncomfortable, inside him﻿ ﻿… hungry, perhaps.﻿ ﻿…”

“Just now I have been praying to die,” said Old Sergei. (“Pretty safe,” thought his son, arrogantly, “as experience must have shown him. Funny how old people don’t learn by experience. Only we young people do that.”)

===Chinese===

“谢廖沙，”老谢尔盖说，把脸朝小羊低下去，仿佛看得见它，“我要是死了…”
激动嫌住了他的话，停顿那么长，害得谢廖沙——他正冷冷出神地用脚尖点着一只早已扁了的蟑螂尸首，仿佛它该死一百回——只好接话：“哦，瞎说，爸爸！您没有道理死…”
“道理有的是，”老谢尔盖说，随即有点狼狈——谢廖沙又吹起口哨来，从牙缝里，几乎耳语——给一场谈死的谈话配这么一支伴奏，不合适。“没有一个人珍重我在这儿——我自己更不珍重。我是我四周人的累赘，也是我自己的累赘。…”
“您心里并不真这么想吧，爸爸，”谢廖沙说。他父亲有点惊讶地把这话当作孝子的深情驳斥。其实谢廖沙这话是照字面说的。他知道父亲说“他留在世上的必要已经到头了”，心里并不真这么想。“没有人真信这个，”谢廖沙想，“嘴上说得再凶也不信。他要是不在了，他的世界也就不在了。我要是不在了，我的世界也不在。这只蟑螂死了，它的世界就没了。所以我们谁也不真信自己的世界离得开自己。我拿准，这只蟑螂从前也从没想过它的世界离得开它——会有人盼它死。它的体面在自己壳里舒舒服服——它觉得自己要紧。它看见我的脚落下来的时候，想的是——‘一桩反常的意外，落在一只好样的、无可顶替的蟑螂头上——我！’爸爸就是这么想的。”谢廖沙冷眼打量父亲那张相当烦人的脸——那双漏水的眼睛、鼻子、嘴。“可怜的老驴，”他想，“他那份体面在他肚子里有点不舒坦…兴许是饿了。…”
“方才我一直在祷告求死，”老谢尔盖说。（“倒挺稳当，”他儿子傲慢地想，“经验总该教过他了。怪就怪在，老年人不肯从经验里学。只有我们年轻人才学。”）

===Original===

“﻿—and as I prayed I remembered how penniless and friendless you and your mother would be were I to leave you. The shop needs a business head, and if I were not here to talk things over with your cousin Andryusha, God knows what would happen. You and your mother do not realize the value of a business head quietly yet actively in the background﻿—an asset quite as necessary to a family’s prosperity, I assure you, as all this cadging of goats and hacking of logs. However, these things are not appreciated until one is dead, and, as I told you, I have been praying for death.”

In order to prove to his wife and son the value of a business head, he had prayed to have it chopped off. Old Sergei had a different vanity every day. Sometimes he changed twice or thrice a day. When he got up in the morning, Anna and Seryozha usually gleaned﻿—though too often in a rebellious spirit﻿—what fancy aspect of his nature he was displaying for their admiration for the next few hours. Since four o’clock today he had been, as Seryozha saw, a Business Man, afflicted with blindness, to be sure, but quietly effective nevertheless.

“And as I prayed﻿—since even in meditation and worship my business sense is awake﻿—I remembered that two hundred rubles I left with Gavril Ilitch Isaev at Seoul, to invest in his hotel, many years ago, at a time when I feared for the safety of Chinese banks and decided that Seoul was safer. He banked the money in his own name, but I have the receipt. I wrote it and he signed it. I think it is in that volume of Pushkin’s poems that props up the short leg of our bed. You see what it is, Seryozha, to have an orderly business mind. You would probably never have remembered that two hundred dollars.”

“I couldn’t have remembered it, since I never heard of it before,” said Seryozha. “And I don’t know now whether you mean dollars or rubles. You say both.”

===Chinese===

“——我祷告着，忽然想起，我一走，你和你母亲该多么无钱无靠。铺子需要一个生意头脑；我要是不在了，没人同你堂兄安德留沙商量，天知道要出什么乱子。你和你母亲不懂得，一个生意头脑，安安静静待在幕后、却始终运转着，是什么价值——我向你担保，这份家当，同你们满街讨山羊、满山砍柴火一样，是发家的必需品。可惜这些东西，人不死总不识货；而我，方才也告诉过你，一直在祷告求死。”
为了向妻儿证明一个生意头脑的价值，他祷告着叫人把它砍掉。老谢尔盖一天一个虚荣。有时一天换两回三回。早上他一起床，安娜和谢廖沙多半就能（尽管十有八九憋着一股反劲）猜出，接下来几个钟头，他要把自己天性里的哪一副花样摆出来供他们叹赏。打今天下午四点起，他——如谢廖沙看出的——一直是一位生意人，诚然瞎着，可照样在暗地里悄悄奏效。
“我祷告着——连冥想和敬拜的时候，我的生意头脑也醒着——忽然想起我那二百卢布，许多年前放在汉城加夫里尔·伊里奇·伊萨耶夫那儿，投在他的旅馆里；那时我怕中国的银行不稳，认定汉城稳当。钱存在他自己名下，可收据在我手里。我写的，他签的字。我想，它就夹在顶咱们床那条短腿的那本普希金诗集里。你瞧瞧，谢廖沙，这就叫有个有条有理的生意头脑。换了你，那二百块钱多半永远想不起来。”
“我想得起来才怪，我从没听说过，”谢廖沙说。“这会儿我也不知道您说的是美元还是卢布。两个您都说。”

===Original===

“I mean neither,” said Old Sergei. “Really, Seryozha, you are not using your mind. What is the currency of Korea? Yen, of course. Should I be likely to have my savings put away in a bank in a currency not native to the country in which the bank does business? Yours is the kind of question which shows me how ill able to look after your mother you would be were I to leave you. You should certainly cultivate a business sense. Now my idea is that you should go on foot over the mountains to Seoul and fetch that money from Gavril Ilitch.”

“Why don’t you write and ask him to send it?”

“I did, of course, write to him, some time ago, in the summer of 1924, I think it was. Isaev did not answer. He is by no means a business man and I should say hardly knows how to put pen to paper except just to sign his name. He is a peasant﻿—was my brother’s gardener before the revolution, in Vladivostok. A devoted creature, but evidently deficient in business methods. Since I wrote to him my mind has been occupied with other matters and I only thought of the money just now, when praying to die. But, having once thought of it, I have no difficulty at all in recollecting every detail of the transaction, and in deciding on the best and most businesslike solution of the problem﻿—which is that you should walk to Seoul and﻿—”

“Why in the world did you give your two hundred yen to a peasant who could neither read nor write? That wasn’t very businesslike, it seems to me.”

“Seryozha, you are not using your mind. Surely you cannot expect me to explain all my business dealings to a raw lad like you, without financial experience of any kind. As a matter of fact, it was the best thing to do; I was in Seoul buying stock for the shop, and found that, since the goods I expected could not be delivered, I had two hundred yen too much﻿—more than was safe to carry across the robber-infested Manchurian border. Isaev had had a good position as coachman to the Japanese bank-manager and was thinking of starting a hotel. His savings were banked in the Chosen Bank. Naturally I gave him my savings, too, to invest with his own until I should ask for them. I dare say the poor fellow is wondering every day why I do not return to claim my money.”

===Chinese===

“哪个都不是，”老谢尔盖说。“真的，谢廖沙，你不动脑子。朝鲜的货币是什么？日元，当然。我的存款，难道会存进一家银行、用的却不是银行所在地本国的货币？你问的这种问题，正告诉我：我一走，你多没本事照看你母亲。你确实该培养培养生意头脑。我的主意是：你该徒步翻山去汉城，把钱从加夫里尔·伊里奇那儿取回来。”
“您怎么不写信叫寄来？”
“我当然写过信，有些日子了，我想是一九二四年夏天。伊萨耶夫没回。他根本不是生意人，我看他除了签自己的名，几乎不知道怎么拿笔。他是个庄稼汉——革命前在我弟弟家当园丁，在海参崴。一条忠心的性命，可生意上显然一窍不通。写了那封信以后，我的心叫别的事占着，直到方才祷告求死，才想起这笔钱。不过，一旦想起，交易的每一处细节我毫不费力全记起来了，而且立刻定下了解决问题的最好、最合生意经的路子——就是你，走着去汉城，然后——”
“您到底为什么把二百日元交给一个不识字的庄稼汉？依我看，这可不大合生意经。”
“谢廖沙，你不动脑子。总不能指望我把我的生意往来，对向你这么个生瓜蛋子、一个铜板的财务经验都没有的人，一一解释清楚吧。实际上，那是最妥当的一步：我当时在汉城给铺子办货，发觉要的货送不来，手头多出二百日元——比带者横穿满是胡匪的满洲边境能保险的数目多。伊萨耶夫那时有份好差事，给日本银行经理当车夫，正琢磨开一家旅馆。他的积蓄存在朝鲜银行。我自然把我的积蓄也交给他，同他自己的放在一起，等我来取。我敢说，那可怜人天天纳闷，我怎么还不回去取钱。”

===Original===

“After ten years of wondering every day I should have thought he might have got a friend who could write to try and get in touch with you,” said Seryozha, sulkily, but his mind was already, as it were, packing its wits for the journey; his toes were already throbbing with the starting fever. Every day in his unpromising life he woke up feeling “perhaps something great will happen today,” and here was something great﻿—a lonely, dignified journey, without any father and mother to be ashamed of at every turn.

Old Sergei straightened his back, and in doing so awoke the kid, which, after innocently making a little mess on the floor, tottered on unsteady legs toward Seryozha, who, it seemed to the kid, gave forth an inviting smell of milk and mother. Old Sergei did not notice the departure of his toy, he was so much interested in the deathbed advice he was determined to give his son before his fount of tears and high principles should be dried up by the arid discomforts of an actual deathbed.

“I have a feeling,” he said, his words dipping under his shaking upper lip like chickens escaping under a rabbit-wire fence, “I have a feeling that this is the last talk we shall have together, Seryozha.﻿ ﻿… If, when you return, I should be already no more, I entreat you, my boy, to be good to your mother. Remember the dangers she went through for your sake in bearing you﻿—”

“That wasn’t for my sake, papa﻿—she didn’t know me then﻿—it was for her pleasure and yours that she bore me,” said Seryozha.

“Let her lie by my side in the grave,” said Old Sergei, trying to ignore the possibility of an interval of healthy widowhood for poor Anna, “as she has lain so many nights by my side in the big bed.”

“Oh, don’t worry, papa,” said Seryozha. “You’ll both of you live till ninety, I’m sure.” And he began to whistle softly through his teeth again.

Tears squeezed between Old Sergei’s eyelids as he half-realized his impotence in imposing his posthumous pathos on the living. How could he force his wife and son to regret him all their lives? There was no way. There was no love or loyalty in the world.

===Chinese===

“天天纳闷了十年，我倒想，他总该找个会写字的朋友，想法子同您联系上了吧，”谢廖沙悻悻地说；可他的心已经——好比说——在给这趟远行收拾行装了；他的脚趾头已经随着出发的热病突突直跳。在他那没指望的日子里，他每天醒来都觉着“今天兴许要出件大事”；这可就是大事——一趟孤孤单单、体体面面的远行，一路再没有走到哪儿都叫他害臊的爹和娘。
老谢尔盖挺直了腰，这一挺惊醒了小羊。小羊先是无辜地在地板上撒了一小泡，随即踩着不稳的腿，摇摇晃晃朝谢廖沙挪去——在小羊看来，谢廖沙身上散发着牛奶和母亲般诱人的气味。老谢尔盖没留意他的玩具离了岗；他一心惦着要赶在自己那眼泪与高义的泉眼、被真实临终的干涩苦楚榨干之前，把临终的嘱咐交代给儿子。
“我有一种预感，”他说，字字从他那哆嗦的上唇底下溜下去，像小鸡从防兔的铁丝网底下逃生，“我有种预感，这是咱们爷儿俩最后一场谈话了，谢廖沙。…万一你回来时我已经不在了，我求你，孩子，待你母亲好些。记着当年为了生你，她冒过多少险——”
“那不是为了我，爸爸——她那时候还不认得我呢——她生我，是为她自个儿的快活，也是您的，”谢廖沙说。
“让她在坟里躺在我身边，”老谢尔盖说，竭力不去想可怜的安娜或许还有一段健健康康守寡的岁月横在中间，“就像这么多个夜晚，她在我身边躺在大床上一样。”
“哦，放心吧，爸爸，”谢廖沙说。“你们俩准都活到九十。”他又牙齿间轻轻吹起口哨来。
泪水从老谢尔盖的眼缝里挤出来：他半觉出，自己这份“身后之悲”，在活人身上强加不动。他怎么能逼妻儿悔他一辈子？没有办法。世上没有爱，没有忠心。

===Original===

“Supper is ready,” said Anna, and stood in the doorway, suddenly thinking of something else, her eyes fixed on a fly on the wall. She was trying to think what had irritated and hurt her just before she began cooking the fish pie. Somehow she craved to identify that scar on her temper. But she could not trace any thought to its source because the tiresome wilderness of her old husband’s presence kept on blossoming into silly words that distracted her attention. To stop him talking she said again, “Supper is ready.” But Old Sergei went on talking. Anna went on thinking. Seryozha went on whistling. Anna caught words.

“… and giving to those who are poorer than yourself. God remembers it even if the ungrateful forget it. He will repay.﻿ ﻿…”

“An investment only﻿—not a gift,” thought Anna, and stood in abstraction, scratching her head, till the next words intruded:

“… sleeping with women, Seryozha. Remember your Russian blood is a pure sacred inheritance﻿ ﻿… an insult to the land of your fathers to mix your blood with﻿ ﻿…”

“For poor Seryozha,” thought Anna, “Russia is unluckily becoming nothing but that﻿—the land of his fathers﻿—‘father’﻿—‘Russia’﻿—boring peevish words. And yet the high Russian fields﻿ ﻿…”

“Supper is ready,” she said aloud. Seryozha, goaded by his empty stomach, got up so abruptly that he knocked his chair over. But still Old Sergei went on talking﻿—commanding them to stay, with his weak, blind, upraised face.

“… worth your while to earn, as I have earned, the reputation of a sound man﻿—a man with a stake in the community﻿—one who pays what he owes, no more, no less.﻿ ﻿…”

Anna’s thought ran off to the little shop on the other side of the matchwood partition she leaned against﻿—a place of business closed since the Chinese soldiers’ raid, but still containing a tall pile of unwanted tins of Milkex, two dozen fancy diaries, four or five dozen celluloid hand mirrors in pastel shades, a case of comic can-openers in the form of bulldogs, a hundred or so silk-padded coat-hangers, and a few other temptations that could not even tempt thieves. These goods, Anna felt, shone in an idealized form in her old husband’s imagination, and gave him the right, in his own eyes, to claim a stake in the community.

===Chinese===

“晚饭好了，”安娜说，站在门口，忽然又想起别的，眼睛盯着墙上的一只苍蝇。她想想起炸鱼馅饼之前，是什么惹恼了她、伤了她。不知怎么，她渴望认出脾气上这道疤。可她追不出任何念头的来路，因为丈夫那片恼人的荒原，不住地开出些蠢话的花，搅散她的注意。为叫他住口，她又说一遍：“晚饭好了。”可老谢尔盖只管说。安娜只管想。谢廖沙只管吹口哨。安娜零碎听进几个词。
“…施舍给比你自己更穷的人。忘恩负义的人忘了，上帝记得。他要偿还的。…”
“一笔投资罢了——不是礼物，”安娜想，站在那儿出神地掻头，直到下一句闯进来：
“…同女人睡觉，谢廖沙。记住，你的俄罗斯血统是纯净的神圣遗产…拿你的血同…混杂，是对你父辈之地的侮辱…”
“对可怜的谢廖沙，”安娜想，“俄罗斯不幸正在变成光剩那么一样东西——他父辈之地——‘父亲’——‘俄罗斯’——全是些又闷又躁的词。可那高高的俄罗斯原野啊…”
“晚饭好了，”她出声说。谢廖沙叫空肚子一催，猛地站起来，带倒了椅子。可老谢尔盖还在说——用他那张虚弱的、瞎的、仰起的脸，命令他们留下。
“…值得你去挣的，像我挣来的这样：一个可靠的人的名声——一个在地方上有身家的人——欠人的如数还，不多一分，不少一厐。…”
安娜的念头跑到了薄板隔壁那爿小店——自打中国兵洗劫之后关了张的生意——可里头还堆着高高一摞没人要的“奶立克”罐头，两打花哨的日记本，四五打赛璐珞手镜（马卡龙色的），一箱斗牛犬开罐器，百十来个绸面衣架，还有几样连贼都勾不起念头的诱惑。这些货，安娜觉得，在她老丈夫的想象里闪着理想化的光，给了他一项权利——在他自己眼里——宣称自己在地方上有身家。

===Original===

“… and never was drunk in my life, Seryozha,” she heard. It was true, she knew, the taste of alcohol had always made him feel sick. “Some young men think that manliness is found in drunkenness and coarseness and fornication, but there is a truer manliness﻿—”

“For God’s sake!” said Anna. “Supper is ready, I tell you.” As Old Sergei’s blind face turned to her, Anna remembered what it was that had offended her: he had suggested, by accident, what was probably true, that the offer of the kid had been accepted more precipitately than Mrs. Butters had intended. “Do you want to starve yourself as well as talk yourself hoarse, you silly old man?” she said, vehemently.

Old Sergei was conscious of an indecorous anticlimax to a Dying Man’s Advice to His Son. “My son is going on a journey and I am giving him a few parting words of advice, since I am an old man and by the time he comes back I may have been called away on the long﻿—”

“What journey do you mean?”

Old Sergei felt that her horrified question gave him an opportunity for tragic drama such as he seldom wrested from his family. “What journey? Why, death, Anna.”

“No. I mean what journey is Seryozha taking, idiot?” said Anna, stamping irritably with both feet.

“I’m going to Seoul,” said Seryozha, happily, “to fetch some money that papa forgot﻿—”

“Forgot, Seryozha!” exclaimed his father. “It is you that forget﻿—”

“But Seoul is four days’ journey by train and road even when you get to the train,” said Anna. “And on foot.﻿ ﻿… The police in Korea are most dangerous to poor Russians.﻿ ﻿… The bandits on the border﻿—”

“Oh, that’s all quite easy,” said Seryozha. “Three weeks’ walking will do it. And I’d like to see the Japanese policeman or the Chinese bandit that﻿—”

“You will see neither,” said Anna. “You will take no such journey. The idea!﻿ ﻿… Only a couple of imbeciles would have such an idea. Dancing off alone into nowhere. What a notion﻿—and you a mere child still! Let us hear no more of it. Come, must I tell you for the hundredth time that supper is ready? For God’s sake, old man, are you glued to your chair?”

===Chinese===

“…我一辈子没醉过，谢廖沙，”她听见。这是真的，她知道，酒味儿从来叫他恶心。“有的年轻人以为，男子气概在酗酒、粗野和淫乱里。可有一种更真的男子气概——”
“看在上帝份上！”安娜说。“我告诉你，晚饭好了。”老谢尔盖那张瞎脸朝她转过来的当口，安娜想起方才叫她受辱的是什么了：他无意间说出了那句多半是真话——小羊的事，她应得比巴特斯太太的本意急切了。“你这傻老头子，莫非要把自己饿死，再把自己说哑？”她狠狠地说。
老谢尔盖意识到，“临终人对儿子的遗训”落了个不体面的虎头蛇尾。“我儿子要出远门，我叮嘱他几句临别的话——我老了，等他回来，我兴许已经被召去走那条漫长的——”
“什么远门？”
老谢尔盖觉得，她这一声惊问给了他一个机会，演一出他难得从家里人手里夺来的悲剧。“什么远门？嗐，死亡啊，安娜。”
“不。我问的是谢廖沙要去哪儿，傻子，”安娜说，两只脚气得直跺。
“我去汉城，”谢廖沙欢欢喜喜地说，“去取爸爸忘掉的一笔钱——”
“忘掉！谢廖沙！”他父亲喊。“忘掉的是你——”
“可汉城，坐火车加走路也得四天，还得先赶得上火车，”安娜说。“还是走着去…朝鲜的警察，对穷俄国人顶凶。…边境上的胡子——”
“哦，那全好办，”谢廖沙说。“走它三个礼拜也就到了。我倒要看看，哪个日本警察、哪个中国胡子敢——”
“哪个你也看不着，”安娜说。“这趟门你哪儿也不许去。亏想得出！…也就一对傻子想得出这样的主意。蹦着跳着，一个人钻进没影儿的地方。什么名堂——你还是个孩子呢！这话再也休提。来呀，晚饭好了这话，难道要我喊第一百遍？看在上帝份上，老头子，你粘在椅子上了？”

## IV / 四
===Original===

Tatiana Pavlovna Ostapenko and the servant, Katya, bent side by side over washtubs, looked like a sow and a hind feeding side by side from one trough. Both wore the same kind of headkerchief and faded blue cotton bodice and skirt. But Tatiana bent like someone finding flowers at her feet, and Katya bent like a bear offering a goblin a ride on its back.

A young man watched them from the shade of the wide tree that stood behind the village at the junction of three footpaths across the barley and bean fields. The young man, Piotr Gavrilovitch Isaev, almost wished that Tatiana would never turn round. He knew her face so well and was so much afraid of it. Yet he whistled﻿—do-mi-do﻿—his old call to her, and she turned. He knew she would make no gesture of enthusiasm. She looked at him across fifty yards of shimmering evening sunlight for a long moment and then flapped her hand toward him with an abashed, rather rigid, gesture. He watched her talk for a minute to Katya as she dried her hands on her apron. Then Katya went into the house and Tatiana walked along the raised footpath toward the young man under the tree. He could see her exquisite pale face, her russet hair dragged tightly from her forehead under the kerchief, her rather sunken light eyes, now twitching with nervousness. Twice, as she approached, she smiled, as though rehearsing a smile﻿—just a little abrupt delicate grin﻿—a tautening and an instant slackening of her cheeks. It was a smile that seemed to mean nothing but a good intention, and was obliterated like a duty done. As soon as she reached him her expression changed like a changing light on her face, and she said, “Forgive me, dear Piotr Gavrilovitch. I have forgotten something; I must go back to the washing for a minute.” She turned away from him at once and began walking back along the footpath, as a ladybird hurrying along a leaf, on being turned round, walks just as industriously the other way.

Young Isaev, for a moment taken aback, caught up with her in three strides. “What’s the matter, Tatiana Pavlovna? I want to tell you something. Won’t you come and sit in the shade of the tree for half an hour?”

“Yes, of course I will, presently, dear Petya. I must do something I had forgotten, first.”

===Chinese===

塔季扬娜·帕夫洛夫娜·奥斯塔片科同女仆卡佳并排弯腰在洗衣桶上，像一头母猪同一头母鹿并排从同一个食槽里进食。两人裹着同一路数的包头巾、褪色的蓝布褂子蓝布裙。只是塔季扬娜弯腰，像一个人在脚边寻见花朵；卡佳弯腰，像一头驮人取乐的熊，在向小鬼献殷勤。
一个年轻人从村后那棵大树底下的荫凉里望着她们；三条横过大麦田、豆子田的小径，在树下交汇。这年轻人，彼得·加夫里洛维奇·伊萨耶夫，几乎巴望塔季扬娜永远别回头。她的脸他熟透了，也怕透了。可他还是吹了口哨——哆—咪—哆——他唤她的老信号——她回过头来。他知道她不会做出什么热情的姿势。她隔着五十码闪闪烁烁的夕照望了他好一会儿，随后朝他摆了摆手，那姿势羞怯、僵直。他看着她同卡佳说了一分钟话，一面在围裙上擦手。随后卡佳进了屋，塔季扬娜沿高起的小径，朝树下的年轻人走来。他看得见她那张精致的苍白的脸，栗色头发在头巾底下从额头上紧紧拽向脑后，那双微陷的浅色眼睛，此刻正紧张地抽动。走近的路上，她笑了两回，像在排练一个微笑——一个小小的、突兀的、纤细的咧嘴——两颊一紧，随即一松。那笑好像除了一个好意之外什么也不表示，又像尽完一桩义务似的，随即抹掉。她一走到他跟前，表情就变了，像脸上换了一层光，她说：“原谅我，亲爱的彼得·加夫里洛维奇。我忘了件东西；我得回洗衣桶那边去一下。”她当即转身，沿小径往回走，像一只瓢虫顺叶子上急急赶路，你把它掉个头，它就照样勤勤恳恳朝另一头赶。
小伊萨耶夫怔了一霎，三大步追上她。“怎么了，塔季扬娜·帕夫洛夫娜？我有话要同您说。来树荫下坐半个钟头，好不好？”
“好，当然，这就来，亲爱的彼佳。我得先办一件忘掉的事。”

===Original===

She always called people *dear* in a cold voice. Like her twitched-on, twitched-off smile, the dear was a sort of concession, kind yet shy, to humanity. She walked back to the washtubs and Piotr walked beside her.

“My aunt heard from my cousin Sasha Weber this morning,” said Piotr, after wondering for a moment what to say.

“Did he find the world as wide as he expected?” asked Tatiana.

“He hasn’t been all over it yet, Tanya, so he can’t tell. He has only reached Pa-tao-kou.”

“Oh,” said Tatiana, with her apologetic smile. She minded very much when her questions evoked dead answers. They often did. She tried to join in the talk in the manner of other talkers, but so often the talk mysteriously died of her gentle intervention. I think that a remark of hers, though dressed in the trappings of ordinary convenient comment, was often like a fairy coming into a room full of flesh-and-blood men and women. There was the fairy, in no tangible way different from themselves, dressed like themselves, walking, moving like themselves, yet somehow accompanied by cold airs, aloof, terrifying, humiliating. And one man finds that he has forgotten a letter he meant to write, another that he has a business appointment, another that he promised to take the dog for a walk﻿ ﻿… and so the poor fairy is left alone﻿—not rudely but inexorably﻿—left alone, looking itself up and down in the mirror, wondering what was wrong﻿ ﻿… wondering how they knew.﻿ ﻿…

Tatiana looked at Piotr with remorse, and saw uneasiness in his pink face. His face was ugly and anxious; his brassy hair and eyelashes looked lighter in tone than his face; his nose was sunburnt, prominent, and fat. Tatiana’s ready, cold pity was aroused by the tight puckered skin that enclosed his hurt feelings. She thought of the skull inside that skin﻿—sensitive to a blow; of the brain inside that skull﻿—protesting, defensive, bewildered, also afraid of assault. She saw him as a besieged creature in a fortress, marshaling its defenses against her. She felt as if she were trespassing against her will on something almost unbearably sacred, by simply seeing his face. She was seeing too much. Poor Piotr! this is not the way young girls should see young men﻿—yet so it was! Tatiana, however, though only eighteen, was wise enough not to put her compassion into words.

===Chinese===

她叫谁“亲爱的”，用的都是冷腔调。同她那猛地一绽、猛地一收的微笑一样，这声“亲爱的”是对人类的一番让步——和善，却怕生。她走回洗衣桶，彼得走在她身边。
“我姑妈今早收到我表兄萨沙·韦伯的信，”彼得说，先琢磨了一阵子不知说什么好。
“他觉得世界同他料的一样宽吗？”塔季扬娜问。
“他还没走遍呢，塔尼娅，说不上。他才到八道沟。”
“哦，”塔季扬娜说，带着她那个赔罪的微笑。她的问题换来死答案的时候，她心里很介意。常常这样。她学着别的说话人的样子参加谈话，可这场谈话又常常莫名其妙地死在她温柔的插话里。我想，她的一句话，虽然穿着寻常应景话的衣裳，却常常像一位仙女走进一间满是血肉男女的屋子。仙女就在那儿，摸不出同旁人有什么两样，穿戴同旁人一样，走路、动作同旁人一样，可不知怎么，她随身带来寒气，疏远，吓人，叫人无地自容。于是这个人想起还有一封信忘了写，那个人想起还有一桩买卖上的约会，又一个想起还答应过带狗出去遛遛…于是可怜的仙女被撇下了——不粗鲁，却不容分说——被撇下，独自在镜子前把自己上上下下打量，纳闷是哪儿出了错…纳闷他们是怎样知道的。…
塔季扬娜悔悔地看着彼得，看见他粉红的脸上那份不安。他的脸又丑又焦；黄铜色的头发睫毛，色调比脸还浅；鼻子晒脱了皮，又大又肥。他那份受伤的情感，包在那紧绷皱缩的皮里，勾起了塔季扬娜现成的、冷冷的怜悯。她想到那层皮底下的头骨——挨不得一击；头骨底下的脑子——抗议着，设防着，惶惑着，也怕挨打。她把他看成一员困守堡垒的生物，正调集防御，对付她。她觉得自己仿佛是在违背本意地窥看一样近乎无法承受的神圣之物——单是看见他的脸。她看见得太多了。可怜的彼得！年轻姑娘看年轻男人，不该是这样的看法——可偏偏就是这样！不过塔季扬娜虽然只有十八岁，已经懂事到不肯把怜悯说出口。

===Original===

She could not think of anything else to put into words, either. She always boggled over words, and would not have recognized the properly girlish ones, even if they had occurred to her. Nor was she interested enough in spanning this giddy space between herself and Piotr to risk anything for the sake of building a bridge over it. She did not know how to approach him; she could not bear that he should approach her. Her body she did not know, but in her mind she was fanatically virgin. Every approach was a danger, she could not have explained why. And yet *she* must be allowed to trespass secretly upon her neighbors; she must have hostages in many camps; she must send herself often far away from home to be a protesting prisoner in other bodies. A little pang in everyone’s pain seemed hers, just as a lamb’s leaping, an impudent flirt of a free bird’s wing, so often seemed part of her vicarious youth﻿—a word she herself had known but left unspoken, a satisfaction in itself, like a flattery. Perhaps she was an egoist﻿—an egoist whose center had slipped﻿—an egoist whose ego had spilled over, tainted too much. She was like a person who lived on a mountain instead of in her own house. That was poor Piotr’s trouble, though he did not know it﻿—Tatiana was never at home, waiting inside herself for visitors, as other young people are﻿—waiting behind her own threshold﻿—watching out of her own eyes. You might call﻿—Tanya, Tanya﻿—at her pretty ear, and her voice would reply, as it were, from a long way off. Her sight was unglazed by eyes and therefore too coldly clear﻿—like frosty air as you come out too early in the dawn from your smoky house. Tanya, Tanya, you might call, posturing before her window, but she would be away, watching you quietly from the hill, seeing you, not as you, but as a little far part of herself, dancing in the distance.

She was both too far and too near. She loved her neighbor as herself because she found herself in her neighbor, but if you were her neighbor, you found that she loved you no better than herself﻿—and therefore not at all.

===Chinese===

旁的话她也想不出可说的。她对词语永远磕磕绊绊，正经姑娘家该说的那些词，就算撞到她嘴边，她也认不出。她对跨越她自己同彼得之间这段晕眩的空隙，也没有足够的兴味，肯为架一座桥冒什么险。她不知道怎样接近他；又受不了让他接近她。她的身体她自己不认识，可在心里，她狂热地守身如玉。每一次接近都是危险，为什么，她解释不了。可是反过来，她却必须获准悄悄潜进邻人们中间；她必须在许多营盘里押有人质；她必须常常把自己派出家门老远，到别人的身体里当一名抗议的囚徒。人人疼痛里的一小下疼仿佛是她的，正如一头羊羔的腾跃、一只野鸟翅膀没遮拦的一挑，也常常像她那替代的青春的一部分——“替代”这个词她认得，却从不说出口，本身就是一种满足，像一句奉承。兴许她是个自我主义者——一个中心滑了位的自我主义者——一个自我泼洒出去、染污得太多的自我主义者。她像一个人不住在自己家里，住在山上。这正是可怜的彼得的苦处，虽然他自己不知道——塔季扬娜从不在家，从不像别的年轻人那样守在自己里头等客——守在自家门槛后——从自家的眼睛里朝外张望。你尽可以喊——塔尼娅，塔尼娅——凑着她那好看的耳朵喊，她的声音答应你，也仿佛隔着老远。她的视界没有蒙眼睛的水汽，因此冷得过分清明——像你大清早从烟熏的屋里出来，迎面一线霜晨的空气。塔尼娅，塔尼娅，你尽可以喊，在她窗前摆着姿势，可她不在，她在山上静静地看你，看见的不是你，而是她自己的一小片远景，在远处舞着。
她又太远，又太近。她爱邻人如爱自己，因为她在邻人里找见了自己；可你若是她的邻人，你会发现她爱你并不胜过爱她自己——也就是说，压根儿不爱。

===Original===

What a detestable advantage it gave her, to be high on the hill, safe, away from home, yet near enough to hear, with her remote cold senses, your heart beating. How wrong that she should claim to have the key to your lock and yet, herself, present no lock, no door, no house, even, for your unlocking. And yet her face and body were so lovely that you must love them even more than you hated her passionless mind and heart﻿—you could not help calling﻿—Tanya, Tanya﻿—before the empty windows of a deserted house at the foot of the hill, hoping always to lure her home, inside herself, to welcome you in at last.

She had a smile that pulled the corners of her lips up and the corners of her eyes down, but it was never meant for you, except secondarily; when you smiled in reply, hers vanished, was twitched away.

===Chinese===

高踞山上，安全，离家，却又近得能用她那遥远冰冷的感官听见你的心跳——这给了她一种多么可憎的优势。多么不公平：她有权声称握有你那把锁的钥匙，她自己却拿不出锁、拿不出门、甚至拿不出一间屋子，供你开启。可她的脸和身体偏偏那样可爱，你恨她那无欲无情的心思，却不能不爱它们——你忍不住要喊——塔尼娅，塔尼娅——对着山脚那座空屋一排空窗喊，永远巴望着诱她回家，回到她自己里头，终于开门迎你进去。
她有一种微笑，嘴角往上牵、眼角往下垂，可那笑从来不是给你的，至多捎带给你；你回她一笑，她的就没了，一抽就抽走了。

===Original===

Tatiana did not know that she lived on a hill; she only knew that she had no neighbors; her neighbors all must harbor *her*, but she had no neighbors. To be approached was entirely unbearable; a desiring or acute glance was in itself an assault; see she must, but to be seen was somehow insult. She loathed touch and always avoided it; the lightest accidental touch rasped her like a cat’s tongue. Love of her neighbor was a thing felt stilly, thinly diffused among pitied lovers﻿—puppies﻿—parents﻿—flowers﻿—insects﻿—even things (she often felt guilty for disappointing *things*)﻿—even *invented* things﻿—blank pensioners of her compassionate fancy. She drew no ecstasy except through her eyes. And she felt a little giddy always because she saw so many things and had so little known self﻿—or such a wide, unknown self﻿—out of which to see them. She saw now, as she walked, a collapsing hourglass of blue sky. She watched clouds crushing it in, and a sand of light spill out of it. Then, as she came to the washtub, her attention swooped suddenly to the reason of her return﻿—a woolly-bear caterpillar, swimming in the suds. It had been swimming there for some time﻿—not exactly swimming, for it was too light to break through the soapy skin of the water, and occasionally it found a sodden island of linen to walk across. Its fur was dry, but it looked exhausted. Tatiana, most of whose diversions of the mind were curiously cruel, had dared herself to let it nearly drown so that its relief at ultimate rescue might be the more glorious. Then Piotr’s whistle had made her forget it﻿—made her prolong the poor insect’s ordeal more than she had intended. She put her finger under it now and caught it up to safety. She laid her finger against a blade of grass and, when the caterpillar had found its footing on its own world, she knelt down and watched it. She was imagining its incredulous delight. Piotr, puzzled, knelt down beside her. They looked as if they were about to pray together.

“But what did you come back for, Tanya?”

“For this.”

“For what? This slug?”

“Well﻿ ﻿… I left it drowning. I forgot it for the moment. Then I remembered﻿—so I had to come back.”

“But﻿—oh, Tanya﻿—a caterpillar! When I told you I had something to tell you! Don’t be so foolish, for God’s sake, Tanya; don’t be so cold. Listen to me﻿—don’t laugh at me.”

===Chinese===

塔季扬娜不知道自己住在山上；她只知道她没有邻人；她的邻人们全都得收留她，她自己却谁也不收留。被人接近，全然受不了；一道含欲的或尖锐的目光，本身就是一场侵犯；她必须看，可被人看，不知怎么就是侮辱。她厌恶触碰，永远躲着它；最轻的偶然一碰，也像猫舌头一样剐她。对邻人的爱，是一种静静体察到的东西，薄薄地散在受她怜爱的东西中间——小狗——父母——花——虫子——甚至物件（她常为叫“物件”失望而负疚）——甚至凭空造出来的东西——她那副慈悲心肠的白食客。她的极乐只从眼睛里过。她永远有一点晕，因为她看见的东西太多，而用来观看的那个自我又知道得太少——或者说，那个自我太宽、太未知。此刻她一面走，看见一个蓝天的沙漏正在坍塌。她望着云把它挤瘪，一沙一沙的光从里头漏出来。随后，走到洗衣桶跟前，她的注意猛地俯冲到她折返的缘由上——一条毛毛虫，“羊毛熊”，泡在皂沫里游泳。它已经在那儿泡了些时候了——也不算游泳，它太轻，钻不透水面上那层皂膜，只偶尔寻着一块浸透的亚麻布小岛，从上面走过。它的毛是干的，可瞧着已经精疲力竭。塔季扬娜——她的种种心戏出奇地残忍——原是向自己打了赌的：放它淹到快死，好叫它终获拯救时的释然更辉煌些。随后彼得的口哨叫她把它忘了——叫这可怜虫的考验又超出了她本来的打算。这会儿她把手指垫在它底下，捞起它，送到安全处。她把手指贴上一片草叶；等毛毛虫在它自己的世界上站住了脚，她跪下来看它。她在想象它那份不敢相信的狂喜。彼得莫名其妙，也挨着她跪下。两人那样子，像要一同祷告。
“可你到底回来干什么，塔尼娅？”
“为这个。”
“为什么？这条鼻涕虫？”
“这个嘛……我把它撂在这儿淹着。一时忘了。后来想起来了——所以就非回来不可。”
“可是——哦，塔尼娅——一条毛毛虫！我说了我有话要告诉你！看在上帝份上，别这么傻；别这么冷。听我说——别笑话我。”

===Original===

He looked at her and could not pretend to himself that she was even paying him the compliment of laughing at him. Nor was he sure that she was listening. She was breathlessly following the caterpillar’s course. It rippled earnestly along like a little machine running on concealed wheels well provided with shock-absorbers.

Piotr uttered a mild curse and then, seizing an empty glass jar that had contained washing soda, he placed it upside down over the caterpillar, involving that unlucky insect in yet another unmerited dilemma.

“Tanya, I believe I’m glad﻿—I’m glad that you are so contrary and unkind. It makes it easier to say these things to you. Listen, I don’t want to stay in Mi-san any more﻿—I don’t want to see your face any more.﻿ ﻿… I’m tired of your face. There’s something wrong with it; though it is so pretty, there’s no heart behind it. Listen, Tanya﻿—don’t look at that damned bug﻿—listen. I’m going away. There was no reason why you should have treated me so﻿—we were betrothed. There is nothing to keep us apart now, except your own hard heart. That and my feeling of being tired of you, of course. You have lost something by your hardheartedness, I can tell you. Some day you will be sorry. You are kinder to that caterpillar than to a man, Tanya. I can tell you, some girls know a man’s value better. Once lost, I am lost forever. You will be sorry.﻿ ﻿… What do you expect? No man of flesh and blood can go on forever loving a girl that only smiles at﻿—caterpillars. What is the matter with you, that you hold yourself so much above love? What else is there for you? Do you want to live and die alone?”

===Chinese===

他望着她，没法哄自己说，她哪怕赏了他一个笑话他的脸面。他也不拿得准她在听。她正屏息追踪毛毛虫的路线。它认真地一波一波向前挪，像一台小车，暗轮驱动，减震良好。
彼得轻轻咒骂了一声，随后抓起一只装过洗涤碱的空玻璃罐，倒扣在毛毛虫上，叫那不幸的虫子又陷入了一场无妄之灾。
“塔尼娅，我看我倒高兴——高兴你这样别扭、这样狠心。这样这些话反倒好说了。听着，我不想再待在米桑了——我不想再看见你的脸。…你的脸我看腻了。它有毛病；尽管那么好看，背后没有心。听着，塔尼娅——别看那只该死的虫——听着。我要走了。你本没有道理那样待我——我们是订了婚的。如今拆散我们的，只剩你自己那颗硬心。那颗心，加上我对你的腻烦，当然。你因为心硬，已经失了东西了，我告诉你。有朝一日你要后悔的。你待那条毛毛虫都比待一个男人好，塔尼娅。我告诉你，有的姑娘更识得男人的价值。我一失，就永远失了。你要后悔的。…你还指望什么？哪个血肉做的男人，能没完没了地爱一个只朝——毛毛虫微笑的姑娘？你是怎么了，把自己抬得比爱情还高？你还有什么别的路？你想一个人活着、一个人死吗？”

===Original===

“No﻿—no﻿—Petya,” she said at once in tears. The word “alone” had a terrible sound to her. Yet she had no defense against it, because the *reality*, loneliness, was her right﻿—her unassailable pride. To live and die alone was like living and dying on a throne; she took her queenship so much for granted that she did not know of it. It was only the word “alone” that had such a cruel, insulting sound﻿—synonymous with *undefended*. Her wordless diffused egoism demanded defense against all that was implied by the word “alone.” A queen has a right to be defended. Yet, of course, Tatiana defended herself﻿—she would have resented that intrusion, actual defense. Perhaps she needed fairy counselors and was only offered lovers. Perhaps she needed the comfort of God and was only offered the love of men. At any rate, the word “alone” made her cry. *Live and die alone.* It was uttered like a threat and therefore it made her cry﻿—just as the words *be crowned a queen*, uttered portentously, might make a queen-beginner cry. Words, heard by the ear, bring tears from the eyes. But hearts are left firm on their thrones, deep down, beyond the reach of threats and tears. *Alone*﻿—how ugly a word! *Alone*﻿—how fierce a threat! *Alone*﻿—how sore and smarting must Piotr’s poor vanity be, to utter such a threat. She felt an unbearable compassion for him. She imagined she could hear his baffled vanity﻿—rejected﻿—driven home﻿—going round and round in his breast, crying﻿—why﻿—why﻿—why? Other girls, he said, knew his value better. That was his poor darling vanity that spoke; he was besieged inside himself﻿—firing off the failing ammunition of his vanity from behind that pink serious face, those blinking blue eyes, that hard healthy nose, that deeply-breathing chest. Of course there was value in that bewildered body of his﻿—of course other girls knew that value. Why not? She knew it herself. Yet suddenly, as she reviewed his deserts, the very thought of his touching her outraged her. She felt sick. She stopped crying.

“It’s no use, dear Petya,” she said in a quavering voice. “I’m not proud of this fear in me. I don’t pretend it. I am what I am.”

===Chinese===

“不——不——彼佳，”她当即含泪说。“一个人”这个词，在她听来声音可怕。可她对它毫无防御，因为那现实——孤独——是她的权利——她那不可侵犯的骄傲。一个人活着、一个人死，就像在王座上活着、死去；她的女王身份，她受之泰然，泰然到浑然不知。只有“一个人”这个词才有这样残忍、这样侮辱人的音响——同“不设防”是同义词。她那无言的、弥散的自我主义，要求防御“一个人”这个词所暗示的一切。女王有权受保卫。当然，真要谁来保卫塔季扬娜，她又要恼——那也是一种侵犯。也许她要的是仙人的谋臣，送上门的却尽是情人。也许她要的是上帝的慰藉，送上门的却尽是男人的爱。不管怎样，“一个人”这个词叫她哭了。一个人活着，一个人死。这话说出来像一句威胁，所以她哭——正如“加冕为王”四个字，煞有介事地说出来，也许会叫一位见习女王哭。耳朵听进的词，引出眼里的泪。可心留在宝座上，纹丝不动，深在威胁与眼泪够不着的地方。一个人——多难听的一个词！一个人——多凶的一句威胁！一个人——可怜的彼佳那份体面，得又疼又辣到什么地步，才说得出这样的威胁。她对他涌起一股难忍的怜悯。她想象她听得见他那份受挫的体面——遭了拒——正中要害——在他胸膛里一圈一圈打转，哭着——为什么——为什么——为什么？别的姑娘，他说，更识得他的价值。那是他可怜的、宝贝的体面在说话；他困守在自己里面——从那张粉红的、一本正经的脸、那双眨巴的蓝眼睛、那只结实健康的鼻子、那副深呼吸的胸膛后面，放着他体面那所剩无几的弹药。当然，他那具惶惑的身体里自有价值——当然别的姑娘识得那价值。有什么不可以？她自己就识得。可突然间，她一掂量他的种种好处，一想到他碰她，就怒从心起。她想吐。她不哭了。
“没有用的，亲爱的彼佳，”她声音发颤地说。“我并不为这份害怕得意。我不装假。我就是我这样。”

===Original===

But, very deep down in her heart, she was proud of this birthmark of remoteness. It was not a fear﻿—it was not a fleeing away, but a repelling. Somehow she knew without knowing it, that to be alone was to be judged by a strange calm standard﻿—to be judged, in fact, by herself only﻿—the ideal of pride. Loneliness was in itself a sort of license to live strangely﻿—to live according to an outlaw’s law.

There was a long silence during which Tatiana, her tears drying on her cheeks, watched the caterpillar under the jam-pot. She thought it was arguing to itself: “Now I must keep my head and think clearly. I got in here, so there must be a way out. That stands to reason.” A perfectly good argument. But there was no way out.

Piotr, who had turned his face away, looked at her and saw where her attention was. Grunting with irritation, he knocked the jam-pot over, and the caterpillar, congratulating itself on this justification of its logic, rippled away.

“Come over to the tree, Tanya, and listen to what I have to say,” said Piotr, hoping there would be no insect life or other distracting livestock there. But not hoping very firmly, for anything, he knew, could hold Tatiana’s attention﻿—anything, except a lover.

She was very docile. She walked by his side, back toward the tree. But, halfway, she stopped and said: “But Petya, is it any good talking? You know what happened﻿—that day. It isn’t words that can alter things like that.”

===Chinese===

可在心底最深的地方，她为这块“疏远”的胎记得意。那不是害怕——那不是逃避，是一种拒斥。她不知怎么知道着（连自己也不知道）：一个人，就是受一种奇异的、平静的标准审判——实际上，只受她自己的审判——骄傲的理想境界。孤独本身就是一种特许，准你活得古怪——按一部亡命徒的法典活。
长长一阵沉默。塔季扬娜颊上的泪渐渐干了，她看着果酱罐底下的毛毛虫。她想，它正在心里盘算：“如今我得沉住气，想清楚。我是进来的，那就必有出去的路。天经地义。”一个再好不过的论证。可是没有出去的路。
彼得本来把脸别开的，这时看看她，看见她的注意在哪儿。他烦躁地哼了一声，把果酱罐掠翻，毛毛虫——正为这番验证了自己逻辑的变故暗自庆幸——一波一波挪走了。
“到树那边来，塔尼娅，听听我要说的话，”彼得说，指望那边没有虫豸性命、没有别的牲口分神。可他指望得不大牢，他知道，随便什么都抓得住塔季扬娜的注意——随便什么，除了一个情人。
她乖乖的。她走在他身边，朝那棵树回去。可走到半路，她停下说：“可是彼佳，谈还有什么用？那件事您知道的——那一天。那样的事，话改不了。”

===Original===

Piotr remembered. The memory stabbed deeply and quickly through his tender body. He could feel still the generous heat of his accepted love﻿—accepted, for she was docile, and had not refused her lips. Why should she refuse? They were betrothed. His memory still rang with her wild scream; his hands tingled still to recall the stiffening of her body as she had fainted. Thunderstruck, almost unbearably hurt, he had looked up﻿—round﻿—down﻿—as he released her. Had she seen a tiger﻿—heard a shot? No, nothing had happened except the natural gesture of a quite ordinary young man’s quite ordinary love.﻿ ﻿… Words to alter this? He ground his teeth to think that such difficult unsimple things as words should be needed. For he knew no fresh words; he hoped for no inspiration of eloquence. All he had to say was, “But why﻿—why﻿—why?” His only argument was being what he was﻿—a healthy decent young man in love with a beautiful healthy girl, whose parents sanctioned their betrothal. What was wrong with that? What was there left for words to explain in that? When he said, “Well then, I shall go away,” he pictured himself obscurely in two halves; one half walking inexorably away over hill and dale, completely carefree, the other half gloating over the sight of the bereaved Tatiana’s remorse, as she lay, cured of her folly, crying, Come back, come back. Petya my darling.﻿ ﻿…

“Well then, Tanya, I shall go away. You will not see me again.”

Tatiana smiled at once. “Will you really, Piotr Gavrilovitch? Will you really be happy again? I shall think of you happy again, finding a new thing every minute﻿—waving your stick﻿—walking happily along.﻿ ﻿…”

“Happy? I am happy now,” said Piotr, sullenly. “It isn’t a woman that could make me unhappy.”

She looked apologetic again. “Oi, Petya﻿—I hurt you.﻿ ﻿… I wish I had never been born.”

“You didn’t hurt me. How could you hurt me? Certainly we would have been married; there was nothing to prevent it except some whim of yours, Tanya. But why should I care? I am the freer for your whim. This place is too small for a man like me. Perhaps I shall join the Chinese army as an officer. Danger doesn’t frighten me. Almost certainly I shall never come back. There will be nobody ever again to bring you mushrooms.”

“How frightful for him,” thought Tatiana, “that he can’t hurt me, though he is hurt by me. I wish I could seem hurt.”

===Chinese===

彼得记得。那段记忆又深又快地扎透他娇嫩的身子。他还能觉出他的爱被领受时的那阵慷慨的热——是领受了，因为她顺从，没有拒绝她的唇。她为什么要拒绝？他们是订了婚的。他的记忆里还响着她的狂叫；一想起她身子一僵、晕了过去，他的手至今发麻。他惊雷轰顶、疼得几乎受不了，松开她时朝上望——四下望——朝下望。她是看见了老虎——听见了枪响？没有，什么也没有发生，只有一个再平常不过的年轻人，做出了他再平常不过的爱的姿势。…拿话来改变这个？他想到竟还需要词语这样艰深、这样不简单的东西，就咬牙。因为他没有新鲜的话；他不指望口才显灵。他有的只是：“可是为什么——为什么——为什么？”他唯一的论证，就是他是他这样的人——一个健康正派的年轻人，爱着一个美丽健康的姑娘，双方父母都认可了婚约。这有什么错？这还有什么留给词语去解释的？当他说“那么，我走了”，他影影绰绰把自己想成两半：一半无情无虑，翻山越谷，头也不回地走远；另一半洋洋得意，看着那悔恨的、被撇下的塔季扬娜——她躺着，痴病已愈，哭着喊：回来吧，回来吧，我的彼佳心肝。…
“那么，塔尼娅，我走了。你再也见不着我了。”
塔季扬娜当即一笑。“真的吗，彼得·加夫里洛维奇？您真的会重新快活起来？我会想着您重新快活了——每分钟发现一样新东西——挥着手杖——一路快快活活地走。…”
“快活？我这会儿就快活，”彼得悻悻地说。“一个女人还弄不坏我。”
她又一脸赔罪。“哎，彼佳——我伤了您。…我巴望我没生下来过。”
“你没伤着我。你怎么伤得着我？咱们本来当然要结婚的；什么都拦不住，只除了你哪根神经搭错，塔尼娅。可我为什么要在乎？你这神经一搭错，我倒更自由了。这地方容不下我这样的人。兴许我去投中国军队，当军官。危险吓不倒我。几乎肯定，我再也不回来了。往后，再没有一个人给你采蘑菇了。”
“他多可怜，”塔季扬娜想，“他伤不了我，我却伤了他。我巴望能显得受了伤。”

===Original===

“Of course I shall be very sorry,” she mumbled, awkwardly.

“Sorry! Sorry to miss the mushrooms,” said Piotr, wildly. “You and your mushrooms!” The very mention of mushrooms suddenly filled his eyes with tears. Mushrooms, patterned on a morning field, seemed to spell Tanya to him for a moment. “Mushrooms, indeed!” he croaked. “You think of nothing but yourself.”

“Myself﻿—myself﻿—myself,” thought Tatiana. “Where is myself?” She sought through herself for some essential bone of personality to lay a finger on. “What is it that likes mushrooms? What is it that fears to be alone and yet *must* be alone? What is it that dies of horror when men come too near? Are my eyes, watching caterpillars and watching Petya’s red face﻿—are my eyes myself? What else? What else?” She tried even to imagine what her outer self looked like, sitting here on a tree-root one cautious yard away from Piotr. She could see the spreading tree, spangled with green light; she could see the red hills under that clear tense light that comes just before sunset, the gullies filling with long smoke-blue shadows.﻿ ﻿… She looked along the bent perspective of the gully that stretched below the village to a wide purple and gold valley. The crops, in all colors but all tinged with the same rich yellow late light, and in all shapes﻿—uneven squares, stripes, oblongs, rhomboids﻿—grew from a bloodred soil, so that the near barley seemed like pale green armies wading in blood. Here and there were intervals of naked red﻿—acres that had been ploughed for a new sowing. The paths, angling about among the many-angled crops, were deep set, as though stitched firmly into the texture of patched quilted velvet. Villages, of the same dreamlike smoke-blue as the far mountains, were tucked into gullies and tributary gullies, and over each village a thin taut string of smoke﻿—the smoke of evening cooking﻿—was stretched flat on the windless air.

And in the middle of this jewel-like elaboration of shape and color﻿—where was Tatiana? She could not see herself or put herself into words, but in her mind’s eye a pillar of nothingness reared﻿—a white mirror, passively accepting the image of hills and valleys, insects and lovers.﻿ ﻿…

“Well, have you nothing to say to me﻿—before I say goodbye?”

“I wish you happiness, dear Piotr Gavrilovitch.”

===Chinese===

“当然我会很惋惜，”她笨拙地咕哝。
“惋惜！惋惜那些蘑菇，”彼得狂乱地说。“你和你那些蘑菇！”单是提起蘑菇，忽然就叫他眼里涌上泪。洒满清晨田野的蘑菇图案，有一霎在他看来拼出的全是“塔尼娅”。“蘑菇，可不是！”他哑着嗓子说。“你心里只有你自己。”
“我自己——我自己——我自己，”塔季扬娜想。“我自己在哪儿？”她在自己身上搜寻，想找出一根个性的主骨，好把手指按上去。“是什么东西喜欢蘑菇？是什么东西怕一个人、却又非一个人不可？是什么东西在男人靠得太近时吓得要死？我这双眼睛——看毛毛虫、看彼佳红脸的这双眼睛——是我自己吗？还有呢？还有呢？”她甚至试着想象，她那外面的自己坐在树根上、同彼佳谨慎地隔着一码，是什么模样。她看得见那棵铺展的大树，缀满绿色的光斑；看得见落日前那种清冽绷紧的光底下的红山丘，沟壑里渐渐灌满长长的烟蓝色阴影。…她顺着一条沟壑弯曲的透视望过去，沟壑从村底一直伸展开去，通向一片紫金色的大谷。庄稼，五色俱全、却一律罩着同一层浓郁的晚黄，形状各异——不方不正的方块、长条、长方、斜方——从血红的土里长出来，近处的大麦地，像一支支淡绿的军队蹚血而行。这里那里，隔着一栏栏裸露的红——那是翻好待播的地。小径，在多角庄稼间斜来拐去，深深下陷，像牢牢缝进打补丁的绗缝丝绒的纹理里。一座座村庄，同远山一样的梦幻的烟蓝色，掖在沟壑和支沟里，每座村子上头，绷紧着一细缕炊烟——做晚饭的烟——平平地平展在无风的空气上。
在这一片珠光宝气、千形万色的经营正中——塔季扬娜在哪儿？她看不见自己，也说不成自己，可她心眼里立起了一根“无”的柱子——一面白镜，被动地收受着山与谷、虫与情人的像。…
“怎么，我道别之前，你没有话对我说？”
“我祝您幸福，亲爱的彼得·加夫里洛维奇。”

===Original===

“You really want me to go away and be happy somewhere else and leave you alone?”

“What else is there to do?”

“Do you realize what it is, you foolish girl, for a woman to live and die alone?”

“I realize very well.”

“Well then.﻿ ﻿… Ah, Tanya, would you let me kiss your eyes﻿—just once﻿—before I go?”

Her heart froze. “Petya﻿—would it make you go more happily?” There was a hissing in her ears, like something boiling over﻿—louder and louder﻿—higher and higher. “Ah, but *no﻿—no﻿—no﻿—no*!” She burst into tears and jumped giddily to her feet. She began running back toward her father’s house. She reached the washtubs and plunged her arms in among the wet clothes, pounding, crying, gasping, trembling. A terrified glance back toward the tree showed her that Piotr had gone. A puff of dust at the corner of the temple was all that reminded her eyes of him. If her eyes were her only self﻿—he was gone from her sight now, gone from her self. She felt suddenly safe﻿—safe from seeing his poor face﻿—safe from having to pity him﻿—safe from invasion. The blank page of herself was safe from inscription now. She flapped a wet garment with wild joy in the air.

“Oi, what a splashing!” said the servant, Katya, coming out, carrying two cans of hot water. “You have been crying again, Tatiana Pavlovna.”

“Only for a minute,” said Tatiana. With the strength of excitement she emptied out her washtub into the ditch and wrung out the clothes. She poured the fresh hot water into the tub from a foolish height, saying to herself, “The awed traveler stood and watched the stupendous cataract from a neighboring height.” She imagined the awed traveler, about half an inch high, standing on the opposite brim of the washtub﻿—but she drowned him at once, by mistake, for the water, violently poured in, splashed violently over the brim.

“You are wasting half the hot water, you foolish girl!” shouted Katya. “I have been nearly an hour heating that water and now you throw it on the mud. Can’t you be careful?”

“If I like,” said Tatiana. She began swirling the water round and round in the tub, saying below her breath, “The horrors of the maelstrom,” and pretending that a little ship, the size of a peanut shell, full of despairing pinhead sailors, was whirling round and round, nearer and nearer to the fatal dark siren dint in the middle of the whirl.

===Chinese===

“你真要我去别处快活，留下你一个人？”
“不然还有什么可做的？”
“你可知道，傻姑娘，一个女人一个人活、一个人死，是怎么回事？”
“我知道得很。”
“那么。…啊，塔尼娅，让我亲亲你的眼睛——就一回——临走之前？”
她的心冻住了。“彼佳——那能让您走得更痛快些吗？”耳朵里一片嘶嘶，像什么煮溢了——越来越响——越来越高。“啊，不——不——不——不！”她放声大哭，晕头晕脑跳起来，朝她父亲的房子跑回去。她跑到洗衣桶前，把两条胳膊插进湿衣裳堆里，捶着，哭着，喘着，抖着。朝树那边惊惶回望的一眼告诉她，彼得已经走了。庙墙拐角一蓬尘土，是眼睛里仅存的他的纪念。要是她的眼睛就是她唯一的自我——这会儿他从她的视界里没了，也从她的自我里没了。她忽然觉得安全——不会再看见他那张可怜的脸了——不必再怜悯他了——不受侵犯了。她自己那页白纸，此刻安全无字。她把一件湿衣裳高高抡起，在空中狂喜地啪啪抖。
“哎哟，多大的水花！”女仆卡佳提着两罐热水出来说。“又哭过了，塔季扬娜·帕夫洛夫娜。”
“就哭了一小会儿，”塔季扬娜说。她借着兴奋的力气，把一桶水倒进沟里，把衣裳拧干。她把新热水从傻高傻高的地方往桶里倒，一面自言自语：“那位肃然的旅人伫立邻崖，俯观这飞瀑之奇观。”她想象那位肃然的旅人，约半英寸高，站在洗衣桶对沿上——可她一失手当场把他淹死了：水灌得太猛，泼过桶沿，溅了他一头。
“你糟蹋了一半热水，傻丫头！”卡佳嚷。“这水我烧了差不多一个钟头，你全泼泥地上了。你就不能小心点？”
“看我的高兴，”塔季扬娜说。她开始让水在桶里一圈一圈打旋，低声念着：“大漩涡的恐怖”，假装有一艘花生壳大小的小船，满载绝望的针头大的水手，一圈一圈越转越近，转向漩涡中心那致命的、黑黑的海妖窝。

===Original===

“*Tck tck!*” said Katya, and, pushing Tatiana aside, she plunged an armful of soapy linen into the water, instantly calming the cyclone. “Now please, Tatiana Pavlovna, don’t waste time, but help me with the rinsing. It will be dark in half an hour.”

Tatiana began thoughtfully steeping the linen in the water, pulling it, plunging it slowly, letting the white spines of linen hems come to the surface here and there like slow porpoises.

“Where is Piotr Gavrilovitch?” asked Katya.

“He is gone.”

“Gone for good?”

“Going for good.”

“Well, Tatiana Pavlovna, I hope you are properly proud of yourself﻿—emptying this village completely of its young men. Piotr Isaev was the last. Now they are all gone. Seven Russian boys came over in our party from Vladivostok when we all settled here in Mi-san, and now they are all gone, thanks to you. In my young days a pretty girl had all the young men from miles around coming round her like wasps round honey; she didn’t drive them away as though she were a bad smell.”

“Some young woman somewhere’s the better for each of these goings-away of young men,” said Tatiana in a high voice. “Seven pots of honey somewhere have one bad smell in Mi-san to thank for their seven wasps.”

She had no defense against Katya’s talk. Katya could not help vomiting spiteful talk, thought Tatiana. One had to forgive other people with weak stomachs, even if they disgusted one﻿—so why not Katya’s surfeit and indigestion of crude words?

===Chinese===

“啧啧！”卡佳说着，把塔季扬娜推到一边，把一抱打了皂的亚麻衣裳插进水里，旋风当即平息。“好了，求求你，塔季扬娜·帕夫洛夫娜，别浪费时间，帮我过水。半个钟头就天黑了。”
塔季扬娜若有所思地开始把衣裳浸进水里，拉一拉，慢慢按一按，让亚麻贴边的白色脊背这儿那儿浮上来，像一群迟缓的海豚。
“彼得·加夫里洛维奇哪儿去了？”卡佳问。
“走了。”
“走利索了？”
“正走利索呢。”
“好啊，塔季扬娜·帕夫洛夫娜，但愿你挺得意——把这座村的年轻男人清得干干净净。彼得·伊萨耶夫是最后一个。这会儿全走光了。当年我们那一伙从海参崴过来、在米桑落户，同来的俄国小伙子有七个，这会儿全走光了，托您的福。我年轻那会儿，一个俊姑娘，方圆多少里的年轻人全围着她转，像黄蜂围着蜜；她可不撵人家，把自己当什么臭味似的。”
“年轻男人这一走一走，天边哪个年轻姑娘就受益一回，”塔季扬娜高声说。“天边七罐蜜，都该谢谢米桑这一股臭味送来的七只黄蜂。”
卡佳的话她毫无防御。卡佳憋不住要呕吐毒话，塔季扬娜想。胃弱的人，恶心归恶心，也只好饶恕——那卡佳这满腹粗话的积食不化，也一样饶恕吧？

===Original===

“It’s no good pretending you don’t care,” went on Katya. “No young girl wants to be an old maid. That’s what you’ll be, Tatiana Pavlovna﻿—a finicky old maid, whining over a fat cat. Look at you now﻿—left alone﻿—not another young man of your own race within a hundred miles. What do you want to do about it﻿—marry a smelly Korean or a Japanese shopkeeper who doesn’t come up to your elbow and blows wind through his teeth? Do you like the idea? What’s the matter with you that all the young men run away at the last minute? It’s a disgrace to this house, I assure you. I’ve known you almost as long as your mother has, and I can tell you it keeps me awake of nights. The disgrace of it. It’s not natural. Young men didn’t run away from me, I can tell you, when I was a pretty girl. Of course, after I’d borne seven children and buried five and lost my figure, that was a different matter. Men always run away from a red nose and three hundred pounds of flesh﻿—it’s their nature. But from a pretty girl﻿—*that’s* not nature, Tatiana Pavlovna, there’s something funny about that.”

Tatiana did not speak. She tried to make a loud secret story inside her mind to drown Katya’s voice. She pretended, as she wrung out the linen, that she was a hero, after a shipwreck, saving the drowning, applying artificial respiration. She did not know what artificial respiration was, but amused herself a little by pretending it was rather like this wringing process. Here, she thought, picking up one of her father’s thick unbleached nightgowns﻿—here was a fat old rich Jew all sodden and limp, and here she folded him up and twisted him round, wringing, jerking, laughing as she thought of his dignity all mixed up and intertwisted, his nose and his toes, his eyeglasses and his ankles, all in a little buckled salutary wet lump, being saved by her﻿—and then, shake, flap there he was, the old moneygrubber, flat, bloodless, and pale, but almost his own shape again, the light evening wind blowing him out as she ran him up on the clothesline.

===Chinese===

“装不在乎，没有用的，”卡佳接着说。“哪个年轻姑娘也不想当老姑娘。你就是那个下场，塔季扬娜·帕夫洛夫娜——一个挑剔的老姑娘，搂着一只肥猫哼哼。瞧瞧你这会儿——掠下单身了——方圆一百里，你本族的小伙子再没第二个。你打算怎么样——嫁个臊烘烘的朝鲜人，还是嫁个够不着你胳膊肘、说话牙齿漏风的日本铺子老板？喜欢这主意吗？你是怎么了，年轻小伙子们到最后一分钟全跑？我跟你说，这是这户人家的耻辱。我认你，认得差不多同你妈一样久，我告诉你，这事愁得我夜里睡不着。丢人呐。不自然。我当俊姑娘那会儿，小伙子们可没临阵跑过，我告诉你。当然，后来我生了七个、埋了五个、身段没了，那是另一回事。男人见了红鼻子、三百磅肉，永远要跑——那是他们的天性。可从一个俊姑娘跟前跑——那就不天性了，塔季扬娜·帕夫洛夫娜，那里头有名堂。”
塔季扬娜不说话。她在心里编一个响亮的秘密故事，好淹掉卡佳的声音。她假装——一面拧衣裳——自己是一位海难后的英雄，正救溺水的人，做人工呼吸。人工呼吸是什么她不知道，可她假装它同这道拧衣工序差不多，自娱了一会儿。这儿，她拾起她父亲一件厚厚的本色睡衣——这儿是一个肥胖的老富犹太人，湿透瘫软；她把他折起来，拧着转，绞，抖，一面想着他那份尊严绞在一处：鼻子同脚趾、眼镜同脚踝，绞成湿淋淋一小团受教的疙瘩，由她救着——随后，一抖，一抡，那老守财奴又是他自己了，扁扁的、无血色的、白白的，几乎恢复了原形，晚风把他吹得鼓起来，她把他挂上了晾衣绳。

===Original===

“What do you think women are for?” went on Katya, gasping and wheezing as she pounded and wrung. “What do you think men want of women? Pretty talk﻿—poetry﻿—sitting side by side and looking at stars? Why, my girl, I can tell you men wouldn’t mind if women were dumb and imbecile, as long as the women could give them the one thing they want. I’ll tell you what marriage is, Tatiana Pavlovna﻿—it’s just getting out of bed, cooking three meals, and getting back into bed again. Women can’t run away from that﻿—unless they’re nuns. There’s nothing makes a man so angry as a woman who plays the coward in bed﻿—nothing else that a woman can do can hurt his feelings at all, except, perhaps, bad cooking. It makes a man mad for a woman not to know her duty; it’s like stabbing him﻿—it turns his love to bile. Love, indeed!﻿ ﻿… Why that’s what love is﻿—just the hope of going to bed together. But running away from fate is what you’re doing, Tatiana Pavlovna, and I’m telling you for your good.”

Tatiana had been pounding one pillowcase ever since she saved the scorned Jew. It was an old pillowcase and now she suddenly pounded a hole in it.

“Why, you ought to have been proud to be wanted by all those fine young men,” persisted Katya, in a grinding voice. “A thin, white little thing like you﻿—and all the good red-blooded Russian wenches that have to shrivel up as virgins, these days, or sleep with yellow men. God knows Piotr Isaev was no catch for your father’s daughter, and he a common gardener’s son; still, he was a man﻿—the last man in Mi-san﻿—and now he’s turned his back on you. You ought to be ashamed, breaking your father’s and mother’s hearts by your whimsies.”

“Real people like to be nagged at,” thought Tatiana. “Nagged at by love and other things﻿—asked and asked to give something. Only hills and rivers and flowers and animals are allowed to be free﻿—not to ask for anything. The more you ask of people the more sure they feel that they are people. It’s their *me*﻿—to be nagged at. I don’t ask for anything, so I’m not allowed to be alive. I’ll be kind to them﻿—I’ll cry for them﻿—I’ll laugh for them﻿—I’ll pretend I’m them﻿—but people don’t want that; they want to claw my *me* and they want me to claw theirs. Not to nag is to insult them.”

===Chinese===

“你说女人是干什么用的？”卡佳一面捶一面拧，喘着气接着说。“你说男人要女人什么？俏皮话——诗——肩并肩坐着看星星？嗐，我的姑娘，我告诉你，女人就是哑巴、就是傻子，男人也不在乎，只要她们给得了他们要的那样东西。我告诉你婚姻是什么，塔季扬娜·帕夫洛夫娜——就是下床，做三顿饭，再上床。女人逃不脱这个——除非去做修女。女人在床上当逃兵，没有比这更叫男人光火的了——女人干的别的事，伤不了他一根毛，兴许除了坏厨艺。女人不懂自己的本分，男人要气疯的——那等于捅他一刀——他的爱立马变苦胆。爱，亏你说！…爱就是这么回事——就是一起上床的盼头。可你这是从命运跟前逃跑，塔季扬娜·帕夫洛夫娜，我是为你好才说的。”
自打救下那个受轻蔑的犹太人，塔季扬娜就一直捶着一只枕套。那是只旧枕套，这会儿她猛一捶，捶出一个窟窿。
“这么多好小伙子要过你，你本该得意的，”卡佳磨着牙音还不死心。“你这号又瘦又白的小东西——如今这年头，多少好血性的俄国大姑娘，要么枯成老处女，要么去跟黄男人睡。天知道彼得·伊萨耶夫配不上你父亲的家门，一个园丁的儿子；可他到底是个男人——米桑最后一个男人——这会儿也掉头走了。你该害臊，由着性子伤你父亲母亲的心。”
“真人爱挨人唠叨，”塔季扬娜想。“被爱唠叨，被别的什么唠叨——被讨啊讨啊、讨你拿出点什么来。只有山、河、花、牲口，才获准自由——什么都不讨。你越向人讨，人越拿准自己是人。挨讨，就是他们的‘我’。我什么都不讨，所以不许我活。我要待他们好——为他们哭——为他们笑——假装我是他们——可人们不要这个；他们要抓我的‘我’，也要我抓他们的。不讨，就是侮辱他们。”

===Original===

She saw little circles in outline, flying about on blankness, each circle trying to pursue, attack, overlap and obliterate another. Whenever one circle succeeded in overlapping another, the area of their intersection was suffused with black; the words “wicked black” formed in her mind. No circle seemed content to let its outline rest coolly on another’s﻿—to admit another’s integrity. Black trespass was the inspiration of all.

“It’s something not natural in you; it’s like a devil in you,” said Katya. “A devil sticking a knife into men’s stomachs. It’s like killing something﻿—to scream and faint and kick up a fuss when a man offers to give you﻿—all he has to give, poor beast. A man feels killed. It’s as if you’d killed those seven lovers of yours, Tatiana Pavlovna.”

“Yet they’re not dead. They’re offering it to someone else by now,” thought Tatiana. “And I’m alive, too.” Then that thought broke. “But *am* I? Am I? Is this a life﻿—this seeing﻿—this thinking for caterpillars and men? If in the morning I was hanging from a branch of that tree there, would there be one life less in the world﻿—or only a pair of eyes shut for good? Where is Tanya﻿—washing clothes﻿—hanging from a tree? Tanya the nothing﻿—who by her nothingness killed seven loves and broke the hearts of her father and mother.﻿ ﻿…”

And then, thinking of lovers, she saw the circles trespassing more and more within one another’s outlines till some wholly covered others, each couple becoming one black circle. Her brain began to freeze. A high throbbing note began to sound in her ears. All the hills began rolling slowly on an upward slant behind the darkening window of her eyes.

“Katya! Katya!”

“Oh, little fool! Oh, my darling! Katya’s coming!” cried the old woman, running towards her.

===Chinese===

她看见一个个空心的圆圈，在一片空白上飞，每个圆圈都追着、攻着另一个圈，要把它压住、抹掉。每当一个圈压住了另一个，相交的那块就洇黑；“邪黑”两个字在她心里成形。哪个圈也不肯让自己的轮廓凉凉地搭在另一个上——认下另一个的完整。黑色的侵犯，是它们共同的天启。
“你这是身上的不自然；像你里头有个鬼，”卡佳说。“一个往男人肚子里捅刀子的鬼。男人把他所有的一切献给你——这可怜的牲口——你又叫又晕又大闹，这跟杀生差不多。男人觉得自己被杀了。就等于你杀了你那七个情人，塔季扬娜·帕夫洛夫娜。”
“可他们没死。这会儿都献给别人去了，”塔季扬娜想。“我也还活着。”这个念头随后断了。“可我真活着吗？我？这算活吗——这样的看——这样替毛毛虫、替男人想？要是明天早上，我吊在那棵树的一根枝子上，世上会少一条命——还是只少一双永远闭上的眼？塔尼娅在哪儿——在洗衣裳——在树上吊着？塔尼娅这个‘无’——凭她的‘无’，杀了七场爱，伤透了她父亲母亲的心。…”
随后，想到情人，她看见那些圆圈彼此越侵越深，直到一些圈整个盖住另一些圈，每一对并成一个黑圈。她的脑子开始上冻。耳朵里响起一声高高的、突突的鸣音。群山开始在她眼睛那扇暗下来的窗后，缓缓地沿一道向上的斜坡滚去。
“卡佳！卡佳！”
“哦，小傻瓜！哦，我的心肝！卡佳来了！”老太太喊着一头跑过来。

## V / 五
===Original===

Seryozha lay in bed in his little room, which was just an oblong bite taken out of the kitchen. His bed was the original kang of this Korean house﻿—a brick-and-plaster oven. On it﻿—since it was summer﻿—were spread all the winter quilts and blankets to intervene between Seryozha’s heavy young bones and the hard kang. The colder it grew in autumn, the more quilts were taken from beneath his body to be placed above it. Seryozha always connected winter with heavy dreaming, since at that season he always had to sleep flat on his back to save his hip bones from contact with the almost unpadded heated kang. But now, in summer, there were three quilts beneath him, and Seryozha could lie comfortably on his side, bent into the shape of a query mark, glaring at the window. The window, carved by Anna long ago with a blunt saw out of the boards of the outer wall, was high up in the corner made by the flimsy kitchen partition and the ceiling. It was very crooked. Anna was, in all her contrivings, too ardent ever to measure things. “I work by eye only,” she would boast, triumphantly daring you to make the obvious retort as she flaunted her results all askew.

Seryozha glared at the window till the foolish, crooked block of sunny morning light printed itself on his retina, so that when his eye wandered, a black crooked window was stamped all over the room. He was so full of angry single-hearted determination that, as he clenched his teeth, he felt as if his burning self, pregnant with its passion to get its own way, was too big for his skin; he had to keep his aching jaws and the smarting muscles round his eyes locked against its rising, bursting growth. There seemed to be a kind of explosion impending of swelling, thwarted will.

“Kept at home like a little schoolboy,” he thought, tears of irritation squeezing out of the corners of his eyes. Seoul, the city denied, rose like a palace before him. He thought of his mother in a distorted paroxysm of anger. Her ungainliness, her uncontrolled loud voice, her dusty abundant hair, her thick ankles, the hiccups that afflicted her when she was agitated﻿—all made her a hateful effigy in his mind, into which he stuck pins of impotent protest.

===Chinese===

谢廖沙躺在他的小屋的床上——那屋子不过是从厨房上咬下来的一块长方。他的床是这座朝鲜房子的老火炕——一座砖砌泥抹的炉台。因为是夏天，炕上铺开了全部的冬被冬毯，垫在谢廖沙沉重的少年骨头同硬炕之间。秋气越冷，从他身子底下抽走的被子越多，添到他身子上面。谢廖沙永远把冬天同沉甸甸的梦连在一起，因为一到那个季节，他只能平平地仰睡，好叫膀骨免于贴上那几乎不垫什么的烧热炕面。可如今是夏天，身下垫着三层被子，谢廖沙能舒舒服服侧身躺着，弯成一个问号的形状，瞪着窗户。那扇窗，是安娜多年前拿一把钝锯从外墙的木板上锯出来的，开在厨房薄板隔墙同天花板夹成的高角上。歪得厉害。安娜的种种手艺里，处处太心热，从不肯量尺寸。“我全凭眼力，”她会夸口，得意洋洋地炫着她那些歪歪扭扭的成品，公然挑衅你说出那句明摆着的回嘴。
谢廖沙瞪着那扇窗，直瞪到那块歪歪扭扭、傻里傻气的晴晨光斑印上他的视网膜；随后眼睛一游移，一扇黑黑的歪窗便盖满整间屋子。他满心是一股怒气冲冲的、一心一意的决意，牙齿一咬，只觉得自己那个燃烧的自我——怀着一腔非如意不可的激情——胀得皮肉都盛不下；他只好把作痛的牙关、发辣的眼窝肌肉死死锁住，锁住那股越涌越涨、要炸开的长大。仿佛一场受挫的意志的膨胀，眼看要爆。
“像个小学生似的关在家里，”他想，气恼的泪从眼角挤出来。汉城，那座被剥夺的城，像一座宫殿升在他眼前。他带着一场扭曲的怒火想到他的母亲。她的笨拙，她管不住的大嗓门，她落满灰的厚发，她的粗脚脖子，她一激动就打的嗝——这一切把她在他心里塑成一尊可恨的像，他往像上扎着一根根无用的抗议的针。

===Original===

He felt alternately rooted to this unloved little house﻿—and far away, walking heroically along a resilient bright road﻿—stopping where he liked﻿—getting wet when he liked﻿—never having to argue﻿—kicking the behinds of rude little Chinese boys﻿—buying sweets and cigarettes recklessly﻿—banging his stick against trees with a various resonance﻿—lighting little fires at the feet of fantastic rocks. Then suddenly﻿—zip﻿—his future telescoped, his traveling thoughts were snatched back to reality﻿—back to prison, as the Reverend Mr. Butters’s pince-nez, when he let them go, were snatched back to concealment by a spring under his lapel.

Seryozha’s dog, with a coarse, confident scratch born of long habit, threw open the latchless door from the kitchen and came in. Seeing Seryozha awake, the dog began to curtsy, to make a little falsetto humming noise through its nose, to wag about two-thirds of its spine﻿—from shoulderblades to tail tip﻿—as it waltzed about the floor, never taking its eyes from Seryozha’s face. Seryozha glared at the dog, making no answer to its extravagant greeting. He was fond of the dog, or rather, fundamentally accustomed to it; he felt as if the dog were part of himself, so it never occurred to him to be responsive or polite to it. Sometimes, to the dog’s rapturous delight, he swung it about by the teeth, or threw it violently across the room in a frenzy of joviality. But today he stared moodily through it, and the dog, though experience had not given it much ground for hope, redoubled its efforts. It reared itself delicately up and planted one tentative paw on the edge of the kang, swishing its tail wildly from side to side. No protest, no encouragement. It stretched its neck shyly forward and lightly licked the tip of Seryozha’s nose. As if this chaste kiss﻿—like the prince’s kiss on the lips of the Sleeping Beauty﻿—had called the dormant energies of the prostrate god into action, Seryozha suddenly sat up, and his bare leg, swinging out of bed with a scythe-like action, cut down the erect figure of the dog as though it had been a swathe of corn. Seryozha had heard through the door left open by the dog’s exuberant entrance sounds of someone in the kitchen. He could begin worrying his mother again. The impotent watches of the night were past.

===Chinese===

他忽而觉得生根在这座不讨人喜欢的小屋里——忽而又远在千里，英姿勃勃走在一条弹性的亮路上——爱停就停——爱淋雨就淋雨——永远不必同人争辩——踢粗野中国小子的屁股——大手大脚买糖买烟——手杖敲着树，敲出种种回响——在奇形怪状的岩石脚下点起小火堆。忽然——嗖——他的前程一收缩，他游历的念头被猛地拽回现实——拽回监狱，正如巴特斯牧师那副夹鼻眼镜，一撒手，就让翻领底下一根弹簧嗖地拽回藏身处。
谢廖沙的狗带着长久养成的、粗放自信的一挠，从厨房那扇没有门闩的门扒开进来。见谢廖沙醒着，狗开始行礼，鼻子眼里哼出小小的假嗓，从肩胛到尾尖——大约三分之二的脊梁——一路摆着，在地板上跳华尔兹，眼睛始终不离开谢廖沙的脸。谢廖沙瞪着狗，对它盛大的问安不作回应。他喜欢这狗，或者说，根本上是习惯了它；他觉得这狗是他自己的一部分，所以从没想到要对它客气。有时——叫狗狂喜——他拎着它的牙抡圈子，或者在兴头上把它狠狠摔过整个屋子。可今天他闷闷地从它身上看穿过去；狗呢——虽然经验没给它多少盼头——加倍卖力。它娇娇地立起来，一只试探的爪搭上炕沿，尾巴左右狂扫。没呵斥，也没鼓励。它怯怯地伸长脖子，轻轻舔了一下谢廖沙的鼻尖。仿佛这一记贞洁的吻——如同王子吻在睡美人唇上的那一吻——唤醒了这位卧倒的神明沉睡的力气，谢廖沙猛地坐起，赤裸的腿镰刀似的抡出床外，把狗直立的身影齐齐斩倒，仿佛斩的是一垄麦。谢廖沙已从狗欢天喜地进门时敞开的门里，听见厨房里有人。他可以重新去磨他母亲了。夜里那些无用的守望，过去了。

===Original===

The dog picked itself up, pleased. For it, too, the day had begun. It began scratching its mastoid energetically.

Seryozha pulled on his trousers, wriggled his blouse over his head, and, while still buckling his belt, was in the kitchen, glaring across the table at his mother.

Anna, dressed in a cotton underbodice that much accentuated her stout flabby bust, a crocheted shawl, and a thick crimson flounced petticoat, was sitting at the table, playing patience. She looked up, a little abashed, as Seryozha came in, and mumbled in tones that sank lower and lower, “I was looking through the cupboard for that bar of washing-soap﻿—I am sure I did not use it up; it is somewhere, I know﻿—and I came across the old pack of cards and I thought I would try if I could remember that game that Mrs. Atkinson﻿ ﻿…” Her voice faded away into a deep growling, “The four on the five and then the king goes up.﻿ ﻿…”

Her son’s cold eye was fixed upon her in disgust. “Mamma, about the matter we were talking of last night,” he cried, hoarsely. He had been rehearsing arguments during the small hours; they had seemed irresistible at the time, but now they eluded him.

“What were we talking of last night?” asked Anna, a mulish look coming over her face. She added, “And then the red nine on the black ten.”

“Mamma, it isn’t fair.﻿ ﻿… I’m a man now.﻿ ﻿… Mamma, remember I am in my nineteenth year.﻿ ﻿… You don’t realize.﻿ ﻿… Look at the way I arranged things with the Chinese when papa was away﻿ ﻿… did I behave like a child then? And now, when it is simply a matter of a little business journey﻿ ﻿…”

“It is useless to go on talking like this, Seryozha. You and your father must have lost your wits, to have such an idea. Walking all alone to Seoul. What an idea! Why, you can’t even walk from here to Erh-tao-kou without getting into a fight with Chinese coolies.﻿ ﻿… And it isn’t age that makes any difference. Your father’s as big a fool as you are, and God knows *he’s* old enough to know better.”

===Chinese===

狗爬起来，很高兴。对它来说，一天也开始了。它开始起劲地挠耳后。
谢廖沙套上裤子，脑袋上胡乱钻进衬衫，皮带还扣着，人已在厨房里，隔着桌子瞪他的母亲。
安娜穿着一件把她肥软的胸脯衬得格外雄伟的布胸衣、一条钩针织的披肩、一条猩红的厚荷叶边衬裙，坐在桌边玩单人纸牌。谢廖沙进来，她抬头，有点讪讪的，声音越咕哝越低：“我在碗柜里找那块洗衣皂——我拿准没用完；就在哪儿搁着呢，我知道——翻见了这副旧牌，就想试试还记得不记得阿特金森太太那套玩法……”她的声音渐渐沉成一阵低低的咆哮：“四压五，然后王上桌。…”
儿子冷冷的眼睛厌恶地钉着她。“妈妈，昨儿晚上咱们谈的那件事，”他哑着嗓子喊。后半夜他一直在排练说辞；当时觉得无往不利，这会儿全溜了。
“昨儿晚上谈什么了？”安娜问，脸上一股犟劲。她又添一句：“红九压黑十。”
“妈妈，这不公道。……我是男人了。……妈妈，记着，我十九了。……您不明白。……瞧瞧爸爸不在家的时候，我把同中国人打交道的事办得……我那时像孩子吗？如今呢，不过是出一趟小小的差……”
“再说这些没有用，谢廖沙。你同你父亲准是丢了魂了，想出这么个主意。一个人走着去汉城。什么主意！嗐，从这儿到二道沟你都走不到，半道就得同中国苦力干一仗。…再说这跟年纪不相干。你爸爸同你一样是个大傻瓜，天知道他那把年纪，本该更明白些。”

===Original===

“But, mamma, I have admitted that *that* was a folly﻿—it was papa’s idea, and I protected him as far as I could. You said yourself at the time.﻿ ﻿… And look at those men we buried that day﻿—soldiers﻿—officers﻿—and one of them looked younger than I am. *Their* mothers﻿ ﻿…” For a moment he thought enviously of the countless orphans of the revolution. Probably those dead soldiers had no mothers. “At any rate, that fight was, I admit, a stupid business﻿—a whim of papa’s. This Seoul idea is a real business matter.”

“A pretty business matter,” said Anna, sweeping her cards violently together, making a sort of splash of cards. “A very pretty business matter﻿—taking a three weeks’ holiday from your good regular work in order to wander off to ask an old rogue for some money your father lent him ten years ago. A likely thing, isn’t it?﻿ ﻿… And even if you got the money, how long do you think you would keep such a sum?﻿—thieves along every road and in every inn, and rogues and harlots always on the lookout for young fools like you. What about that five yen I gave you last Christmas to buy a boiler, and you spent it all on a present for that little brothel-grub, Sonia?”

“I was young then,” said Seryozha, confused. “And in any case I give all the money I earn to you, so surely I have a right to spend five yen once in my life. However, I confess that I was mistaken in Sonia’s character. It was a long time ago﻿ ﻿…”

Where were all the telling phrases that had filed so orderly through his mind in the dark clearness of the night?

“Mamma, another thing is﻿ ﻿…” He stopped distractedly. He had forgotten the other thing﻿—all the other things.

“Well?”

===Chinese===

“可是妈妈，我认了，那桩事是胡闹——那是爸爸的主意，我尽力护了他。您当时自己也说……再说，那天咱们埋的那些人——兵——军官——有一个看着比我还年轻。他们的母亲…”有一霎他艳羡起革命造成的那无数孤儿来。那些死掉的兵多半没有母亲。“无论如何，那一仗我认账，是件蠢事——爸爸一时兴起。这回的汉城，是正经的买卖。”
“好一个正经买卖，”安娜说，把牌哗啦一声狠狠扫作一摞。“好正经的买卖——从你正经的好活计里歇上三个礼拜的假，跑去管一个老滑头讨你爸爸十年前借他的钱。像话吗？…就算你要着了钱，这么一笔数，你看得住多久？条条路上有贼，家家客栈有贼，骗子、婊子，永远盯着你这样的小傻瓜。去年圣诞我给你五日元买只锅炉，你全买了礼物，送索尼娅那个窑子里泡大的丫头——这怎么说？”
“那时候我还小，”谢廖沙发窘地说。“再说，我挣的钱全交给您，一辈子总该有权花一回五日元吧。不过我认了，索尼娅的人品，我看走了眼。那是老早的事了…”
那些在夜里清明黑暗中排着队、齐齐走过他脑子的、句句见血的话，都哪儿去了？
“妈妈，还有一件事是…”他心乱地停住。另一件事——所有别的事——全忘了。
“嗯？”

===Original===

Seryozha stamped his foot with an oath. He thought of the rather dashing, mocking, grown-up attitude he habitually maintained toward his father and mother, in his own eyes﻿—and even, to some extent, in theirs. He remembered, in a rather glorified form, his conversation with his father about the two hundred yen. “My good papa, to leave two hundred yen in the hands of a probably dishonest and certainly incompetent peasant! Was that businesslike?”﻿ ﻿… “Dear old man, do you mean rubles, dollars, or yen? I want to understand this matter thoroughly so that I can help you﻿ ﻿…” That was the proper tone﻿—the usual tone. And now﻿—“Oh, mamma﻿—oh, please, mamma, let me go!﻿ ﻿… Really, mamma, do believe that I’m not a child any more.﻿ ﻿…” Poor Seryozha, he felt robbed of something that he violently resented losing﻿—a king robbed of his crown and suddenly made a beggar. He began to despair. Never had the journey to Seoul seemed more necessary to his happiness than at that moment. Then suddenly, with a sort of artificial revulsion of feeling, he let it go. The only thing left, then, was not to want to go﻿—to want *not* to go. Not only to cease pleading, but to be actually glad that he was not going on a hot, dusty journey among silly, whining, flapping Koreans. By ceasing to want to go he could get even with his mother, he could save his self-esteem. Even if his mother were to implore him to go now, he would refuse. “Too much trouble, mamma,” he rehearsed inwardly in a languid, superior, secret voice. “And nothing to show for it in the end. You and papa would waste the money on muddles and follies, even if I were to get it for you.”

===Chinese===

谢廖沙跺了跺脚，骂了一声。他想起自己一贯在那对父母跟前保持的、相当潇洒、讥诮而老成的派头——在他自己眼里如此，甚至在一定程度上在他们眼里也如此。他想起（已经美化过了的）他同父亲关于那二百日元的谈话。“我的好爸爸，把二百日元掠在一个多半不老实、肯定不顶事的庄稼汉手里！这叫生意经吗？…”“亲爱的老人家，您说的是卢布、美元，还是日元？我要把这桩事弄透彻了，好帮您…”那才是应有的腔调——惯常的腔调。可这会儿——“哦，妈妈——哦，求求您，妈妈，让我去吧！…真的，妈妈，您千万相信，我不是孩子了。…”可怜的谢廖沙，他觉得自己被抢走了一样东西，抢得他暴怒——一个国王被摘了王冠，转眼成了乞丐。他开始绝望。去汉城这件事，从来没有哪一刻比此刻更关乎他的幸福。随后，忽然，带几分做出来的心灰意冷，他撒手了。那么，剩下唯一的一条路，就是不去想去了——巴望着不去。不光是停止恳求，而是当真庆幸：自己不用在那群又蠢又哼哼唧唧、衣衫拍打的朝鲜人中间，走一趟又热又土的路了。靠着不再想去，他就能同母亲扯平，就能保住自尊。这会儿哪怕母亲跪下来求他去，他也要回绝。“太麻烦了，妈妈，”他在心里拿一副懒洋洋、居高临下的腔调排练着。“到头来两手空空。就算我把钱要回来，您同爸爸也要把它糟蹋在一团糊涂和一串蠢事上。”

===Original===

He looked round with a cold, critical look, collecting evidence of his parents’ inferiority, in order to comfort himself. Anna had upset a saucepan full of greasy water in the corner near the stove, and, since the mud floor was slightly concave, a long dark snake of water stretched slowly toward the middle of the kitchen. There was no neatness anywhere. The edge of the window﻿—now hanging open on limp homemade hinges﻿—was tattered with the strips of paper that in winter had been pasted round the edges of the panes to keep out the cold. The wall was all mottled with damp patches; a great marbled shape of damp had been there for years, and, in Seryozha’s unconsciously ingenious eyes, represented a woman in a flying cloak reaching for a great flying rose. She had three arms, to be sure, but you can’t expect nature’s artistic byproducts to be so accurate as all that.﻿ ﻿…

Poor Seryozha bit his lips and sucked them in as he turned away to stare moodily down at his dog. The dog would have enjoyed the walk to Seoul; they would have been like two parts of the same Seryozha﻿—one part walking proudly, the other part leaping, blowing in the wind, barking at larks, scratching at rat-holes, drinking puddles.﻿ ﻿… However, Seryozha thought, I wouldn’t go now if I were asked. Certainly the dog would have had a good time. But a man can’t take a three weeks’ walk simply to please a dog.

The dog lay in a curve on the floor. Evidently some other dog﻿—probably its last ladylove﻿—was thinking of it, for its ear itched﻿—the traditional symptom. It wriggled and wagged its ear repeatedly. Its eyes were open. It liked the sound of voices. That damn goat, too, was somewhere else. Everything seemed to the dog to be going nicely.

===Chinese===

他四下冷眼扫视，搜罗父母低人一等的证据，好安慰自己。安娜把一锅油腻的水打翻在炉子近旁的角落，泥地微微凹陷，一条长长的黑水蛇，正缓缓朝厨房中央爬。处处没有个整洁。窗沿——此刻吊在自制的软合页上敞着——一冬糊在窗格四边挡寒的纸条，撕成了破絮。墙上斑斑驳驳全是潮印；一大块大理石纹的潮渍，在那里待了多年，在谢廖沙无意识的艺术眼光里，是一身披飞斗篷的女人，正伸手去够一朵巨大的飞玫瑰。她有三条胳膊，这倒是真的，可你不能指望大自然的艺术副产品精确到那个地步。…
可怜的谢廖沙咬着嘴唇、嘬着嘴，转过身去，闷闷地低头瞪他的狗。狗会喜欢走去汉城这一路的；他们本该像同一个谢廖沙的两半——一半昂首走，另一半又蹦又跳，兜风，朝云雀吠，刨老鼠洞，喝泥坑水。…不过，谢廖沙想，这会儿就是请我去，我也不去了。狗当然会玩个痛快。可人不能光为着叫一条狗痛快，就走三个礼拜的路。
狗蜷成一条弧线躺在地板上。分明有另一条狗——多半是它上一位相好——正在想它：它耳朵痒——老话说的那种症候。它扭了扭，耳朵一抖一抖。眼睛睁着。它爱听人声。那条该死的羊，也在别处待着。在狗看来，样样都顺遂。

===Original===

Anna squeezed her face into an upside-down isosceles triangle between her hands, her elbows pressed on the table among the cards. She looked at her son, restless with pity for him, as he stood staring down at the dog, raising his eyebrows in childish and studied indifference, shuffling his toe, pretending to tread on the dog’s tail. She would make him some curd cakes this afternoon; he loved those. Perhaps she could afford that Brownie kodak in the Japanese shop, if she did without her new dress length. After all, he gave her almost all his earnings. It never occurred to her to relent on the Seoul question. All the dangers that lurked for him outside her sight accumulated round the very thought of the ridiculous journey﻿—brigands, swindlers, earthquakes and other convulsions of nature, tigers, brothels, Japanese policemen, prisons, diseases, drownings in rivers.﻿ ﻿… Seryozha would have been a super-boy to have suffered even a third of them in three weeks. But Anna’s imagination was always over-exuberant. She did not follow up her fears at all because﻿—well, simply, he was not going to Seoul. Curd cakes and perhaps a kodak for him. Still, the sight of his tremulous eyebrows and pursed lips made her throat ache with pity.
