“Where do you come from, my dear sir, and may I know your name?”

“We come from Chi-tao-kou,” mumbled Seryozha, feeling too large for the door as he was drawn in. Wilfred Chew, coming across the sitting-room between the two big Russians, looked like a coconut palm between two oaks.

Then Pavel said, “Do you know my cousin, Sergei Dmitrivitch Malinin?”

“We know him,” said Seryozha, with the young boor’s natural instinct to begin by being disobliging in the giving of information. He added to Wilfred in English, “They speak, do I know my own papa?”

This was the kind of joke that appealed to Wilfred. That someone else should not know something that he himself knew, seemed to him essentially flattering and amusing. (One notices the same ready sense of simple fun in a rustic, directing a stranger through his village﻿—“Turn by Winkler’s Corner.﻿ ﻿… What, you don’t know Winkler’s Corner where Mrs. Thompson’s mare fell down dead last year? Well, it’s opposite the Glebe Field.﻿ ﻿… For goodness’ sakes, you don’t know that, either?” etc., etc.) Wilfred Chew laughed with delighted hissings, because these simple Ostapenkos did not know that Seryozha was his father’s son.

Pavel was baffled by Seryozha’s uncommunicative manner. “Is Sergei Dmitrivitch in good health?” he asked, though he really wanted to say: “What’s the matter? Is my old cousin dead or has he turned you out of doors, young man?”

“He is in good health,” said Seryozha, and added, indifferently, “He is my father.”

===Chinese===

帕维尔爽朗地一笑。“告诉你的朋友，”他用俄语对谢廖沙说，“我不会讲英语，也听不懂。写下来我认得，因为我的消息全靠英文报纸；可一出口，对我就什么也不是。”同往常一样，他这副得意的嗓音，把这项无知说成了一种美德或一种机灵。听到帕维尔这样用得意的腔调交代自己的短处，人自然会应声说：“好好——恭喜恭喜。…”
“您从哪儿来，亲爱的先生，可否见告尊姓大名？”
“我们从七道沟来，”谢廖沙咕哝，被让进屋时只觉得自己大得门口盛不下。威尔弗雷德·赵穿过起居室，走在两个大块头俄国人中间，像两棵橡树中间一棵椰子树。
随后帕维尔说：“您认得我堂弟谢尔盖·德米特里耶维奇·马利宁吗？”
“认得，”谢廖沙说——年轻人天生那股劲儿，报信先从不讨好开始。他用英语对威尔弗雷德添了一句：“他们问，我认不认得我自己的爸爸。”
这一路笑话正对威尔弗雷德的胃口。居然有人不知道他自己知道的事，在他看来，本质上又抬举人、又好笑。（乡下人给生人指路，也有这份现成的简单的乐子——“在温克勒拐角转弯。…什么，你不知道温克勒拐角？去年汤普森太太的母马就是在那儿倒毙的呀！呃，就在牧师领地对面。…老天爷，这个你也不知道？”云云。）威尔弗雷德咝咝地欢笑着，因为这些朴素的奥斯塔片科们不知道，谢廖沙就是他父亲的儿子。
谢廖沙这副爱答不理的做派，把帕维尔难住了。“谢尔盖·德米特里耶维奇身子骨还好？”他问，心里实际想说的是：“怎么回事？我的老堂弟死了，还是他把你们轰出了门，年轻人？”
“身子骨好，”谢廖沙说，随即淡淡补上一句：“他是我父亲。”

===Original===

“Well, well!” shouted Pavel, leaping up and clapping his hands about Seryozha’s shoulders. “My dear, dear boy, you don’t know what it means to us in our exile to have a kinsman walking in like this. We haven’t heard anything of any of our kin since the revolution, and though your father and I are only distant cousins, I used to have the greatest admiration for him. Can he still move his ears and the hair on the top of his head?”

“He has no hair to move, now,” said Seryozha. “And he isn’t merry enough to do that kind of thing now. He is blind.”

“Blind!” Pavel’s emotions, always a little exaggerated by the excitement of a drink just over or that of a drink to come, at once materialized in the form of a tear in each eye. “Ah, poor man! poor man! Blind!﻿ ﻿… I am very tenderhearted. Excuse me.”

“Explain me what he is saying,” said Wilfred, anxiously. But there was no time, for Pavel went on:

“Blindness﻿—the most terrible of all afflictions, especially to a man of your father’s sturdy independence of character.﻿ ﻿… He used to be so kind to me when I was a young boy and he a youth about town in Moscow. His hair, falling backward, looked like an accident. Lord! How I used to laugh! And now he is blind. Ah, how carefree children are﻿ ﻿… how little they know.﻿ ﻿…”

His beautiful deep voice, uttering these sad words, seemed to bring to the minds of all three Ostapenkos the unearthly and tragic glamour of a remembered Ostapenko childhood. Egoists always have unhappy childhoods, and always look back on them in an agony of rapture and emotion. The eyes of both Varvara and Tatiana were wet at the thought of the laughter of little Ostapenkos, unconscious of a threatened doom.

“They are all crying,” said Wilfred, feverishly. “Explain me, please, Saggay Saggayitch, what is being said.”

===Chinese===

“好好好！”帕维尔喊，跳起来，两手在谢廖沙肩上拍。“亲爱的、亲爱的孩子，你不知道，我们这些流亡在外的人，一位亲族这样走进门来，意味着什么。革命以来，亲族们的音信一概全无；你父亲同我虽然只是远堂，我当年对他可敬重极了。他还会动耳朵、动头顶的头发吗？”
“他如今没有头发可动了，”谢廖沙说。“人也快活不起来干那个了。他瞎了。”
“瞎了！”帕维尔的感情——喝下去的那口或要来的那口，永远把它放大一号——当即化作两颗眼泪，一眼一颗。“啊，可怜的人！可怜的人！瞎了！…我心软。恕我失态。”
“给我讲讲他在说什么，”威尔弗雷德着急地说。可没工夫，因为帕维尔接着说：
“失明——一切苦难里最可怕的，尤其对你父亲那样一副刚强自立性子的人。…我小时候在莫斯科，他那时是个浪荡青年，待我顶和气。他那头发往后一倒，看着像一场事故。老天！我笑得！这会儿他瞎了。啊，孩子们多无忧无虑…多不懂事啊。…”
他那漂亮的低嗓音说者这些伤心话，仿佛把三颗奥斯塔片科的心，一齐领回了记忆中那超凡而悲惨的、奥斯塔片科式的童年。自我主义者永远有着不幸的童年，也永远怀着狂喜与热泪回望它。一想到那些大祸临头而不自知的小奥斯塔片科们的笑声，瓦尔瓦拉同塔季扬娜的眼睛都湿了。
“他们全哭了，”威尔弗雷德火烧火燎。“请给我讲讲，萨盖·萨盖伊奇，说的是什么。”

===Original===

“It is curious how all members of our family﻿—mine and no doubt yours, Sergei Sergeievitch﻿—” Pavel included Seryozha’s family as a polite afterthought, “are haunted by this sense of doom﻿—this atmosphere of tragedy﻿—from birth till death, and always a tragically sudden death, mind you. A hard-drinking, hard-riding, passionate, gloomy, sensitive, tragic breed.﻿ ﻿…” He rolled these delicious words in his throat, drew himself up to his full splendid height, and glared at Seryozha, as though scorching into his young kinsman’s intelligence the baneful splendor of his connection with the tragic Ostapenkos. “This air of doom﻿—your father’s blindness is a fearful example of it﻿—seems to affect even those who come in contact with us. My daughter’s betrothed, Alexander Petrovitch Weber﻿—By the way, were you still at Chi-tao-kou when that tragedy culminated?”

“What tragedy? You mean when Alexander Petrovitch lost his papers?”

“He lost his life,” said Pavel. “He cut his throat.”

Seryozha caught his breath and looked at Tatiana. There seemed to him to be no spontaneous connection between this live young woman and that dead young man. For he had an extravagant respect for life; unconsciously, he enshrined it as a holiness. The power of movement, the sight of movement, and the feeling of movement were his trinity.

Tatiana rubbed her hands slowly together and passed the tip of her tongue across her lips, looking here and there, but not at Seryozha.

“It is certainly a curse,” boomed Pavel, standing swinging his weight from one foot to the other, almost as though he were dancing. “It is part of the family curse which you and we, as kinsmen, share. Tanya has had seven admirers, and all have felt the force of the family doom. We are certainly accursed.﻿ ﻿… If you were not a cousin of ours﻿—and thus involved in any case﻿—I would advise you to keep away from us, young man. Ostapenkos affect all who approach them.”

“Kindly explain what he is saying, Saggay Saggayitch,” twittered Wilfred.

“That case of champagne, Varitchka my dear,” said Pavel, “may be said to have been long waiting to be opened in honor of a kinsman.”

Varvara, frowning with excitement, went out of the room, and as she passed Seryozha she said in a low voice to him, “Sergei Sergeievitch﻿—you mustn’t judge her by love.”

===Chinese===

“说来也怪，我们家的人——我家的，无疑还有你家的，谢尔盖·谢尔盖耶维奇——”帕维尔客气地补上一句，把谢廖沙家也圈了进来，“从生到死都缠着这股厄运感——这层悲剧气氛——而且，记着，回回都是猝死。一个能喝、能骑、火热、阴郁、敏感、悲剧的族类。…”他把这些美味字眼在喉咙里滚了滚，挺直到他堂堂的全高，瞪着谢廖沙，仿佛要把同悲剧的奥斯塔片科家结亲那份凶艳，烙进这年轻亲族的智力里去。“这股厄运之气——你父亲的眼盲就是一个可怕的例证——连沾着我们的人都要受染。我女儿的未婚夫，亚历山大·彼得罗维奇·韦伯——对了，那场悲剧到头的时候，你还在七道沟吗？”
“什么悲剧？您是说亚历山大·彼得罗维奇丢了证件那回？”
“他丢了性命，”帕维尔说。“割喉了。”
谢廖沙倒吸一口气，看看塔季扬娜。在他看来，这个活生生的年轻女人，同那个死去的青年之间，不存在什么天然的关联。因为他对生命怀着一种铺张的敬意；不知不觉间，他把它当作一种圣洁供奉。动的力量、动的景象、动的感觉，是他的三位一体。
塔季扬娜慢慢搓着两手，舌尖舔过嘴唇，东望望、西望望，就是不望谢廖沙。
“这实在是个咒，”帕维尔瓮声瓮气，站着把重心一只脚倒到另一只脚，几乎像在跳舞。“是家咒的一部分，你同我们，作为亲族，一体分担。塔尼娅有过七个倾慕者，个个都领教了家运的分量。我们实在是遭了咒的。…你要不是我们的堂亲——横竖已经卷在里头了——我倒要劝你离我们远些，年轻人。奥斯塔片科家的人，沾着谁谁遭殃。”
“劳驾解释一下他在说什么，萨盖·萨盖伊奇，”威尔弗雷德尖声说。
“那箱香槟，亲爱的瓦里奇卡，”帕维尔说，“可以说早就在等着开了，专为敬一位亲族。”
瓦尔瓦拉激动得皱着眉，出了屋；走过谢廖沙身边时，低低对他说：“谢尔盖·谢尔盖耶维奇——你可不能拿‘爱情’来评判她。”

===Original===

Seryozha turned quickly and looked after her, gaping. “Well, these are queer birds, these Ostapenkos,” he thought, and had an impulse to step to the door and shout after his hostess, “I don’t intend to.”

“What did Mrs. Ostapenko whisper to you, Saggay Saggayitch?” cried Wilfred, rumpling his thick black hair in a frenzy of thwarted enthusiasm.

“Alexander Petrovitch,” said Pavel, “was my darling future son-in-law﻿—the ideal son-in-law. Sometimes I think, Sergei Sergeievitch, that women exist only to suck the blood out of men.﻿ ﻿… Excuse me a moment﻿ ﻿…” He could hear that Varvara was looking in the wrong place for the champagne.

“Please, please, what is he talking about, Saggay Saggayitch?” said Wilfred desperately, as their host went out.

“Oi﻿ ﻿… about mans﻿ ﻿… and womans﻿ ﻿… plenty things,” said Seryozha. He was looking blankly at Tatiana’s reflection in a looking-glass. It was an old dark glass that made everything seem twilit and leaden﻿—even Tatiana’s bright hair.

Tatiana, crouched over something on the table, said, “Have you ever noticed that there are always pictures of spotted deer on all Japanese matchboxes? Sometimes in one attitude, sometimes in another, but always under a little tree. I wonder why.”

Seryozha did not know why, so he only grunted humbly. Wilfred Chew, feeling anxiously that an opportunity for wit or sentiment was being missed, leaned forward, grinning with all his teeth, and said, “*Horosho*, Miss Ostapenko, *horosho horosho*.﻿ ﻿…” *Horosho* was the only Russian word he knew, and of course it was an enthusiastic and ambiguous one. Poor Wilfred, it was torture for him to be left out of talk.

Pavel came back with the hurried gait of one who has thought of several good things to say while he has been away.

“Women, Sergei Sergeievitch, are like a kind of dry rot in a man’s world,” shouted Pavel, who was suffering from one of the momentary spasms of dislike of women that come to a man who lives alone with devoted women to whom he is reluctantly devoted.

===Chinese===

谢廖沙猛回头，张着嘴目送她。“好家伙，这奥斯塔片科家，全是些怪鸟，”他想，一时恨不得追到门口，朝女主人喊一句：“我没打算。”
“奥斯塔片科太太同你咕哝什么来着，萨盖·萨盖伊奇？”威尔弗雷德喊，热望落空，狠狠揉着他那头厚黑发。
“亚历山大·彼得罗维奇，”帕维尔说，“是我心爱的准女婿——理想的女婿。有时候我想，谢尔盖·谢尔盖耶维奇，女人的存在，就是为了吸男人的血。…失陪一下…”他听得出，瓦尔瓦拉把香槟找错了地方。
“求求你，求求你，他在讲什么，萨盖·萨盖伊奇？”主人出去后，威尔弗雷德绝望地说。
“哎…讲男人…和女人…好些事，”谢廖沙说。他正茫然望着镜子里塔季扬娜的映像。那是一面老旧的暗镜，照什么都带暮色、带铅灰——连塔季扬娜的亮头发也是。
塔季扬娜伏在桌上一样什么东西上，说：“你留意过没有，日本火柴盒上，回回印着梅花鹿？有时这个姿势，有时那个姿势，总在一棵小树底下。我纳闷为什么。”
谢廖沙不知道为什么，只好谦卑地哼了一声。威尔弗雷德·赵，焦灼地觉得一个卖弄机智或情致的机会正在流失，探身向前，露出一口牙笑，说：“哈拉少，奥斯塔片科小姐，哈拉少，哈拉少。…”哈拉少是他唯一会的俄文词，当然，又热烈又含混。可怜的威尔弗雷德，被掠在谈话之外，于他是酷刑。
帕维尔回来了，步子匆匆，是那种离开片刻、想好了好几句话等着说的人的步子。
“女人，谢尔盖·谢尔盖耶维奇，是男人世界里的干腐菌，”帕维尔喊——他正犯着一阵男人的老毛病：同一个他勉勉强强挚爱着的、尽心尽力的家中女眷们关起门过日子，就时不时发作一阵厌女。

===Original===

“I must say, I don’t understand this grumbling at things for being things,” said Seryozha, feeling very manly. “You can’t grumble even at a louse for being a louse; only if it pretends to be a beetle﻿—then you can grumble. Or if it bites you, you can grumble at the bite. But bite or no bite, it is what it is. I don’t think women are dry rot, or anything but just women. They do what they were born to do, just as we do and lice do.”

“You are like all young people in these days, cousin, full of contradictory arguments,” said Pavel, genially, without giving attention to a word Seryozha had said. “I’ll tell you a story out of my own experience﻿—a story that always seems to me to typify the mean part women play in men’s affairs. Excuse me a moment while I open this bottle. Tanya, the wire-cutter.”

Tatiana, without rising and without lowering her eyes, which were fixed in a kind of blurred stare on the matchbox on the table, pulled out a drawer close to her. She was for a second obliged to focus her eyes on the contents of the drawer as she selected the wire-cutter and handed it to her father. Then she fixed her blurred, trance-like gaze on her father. He was half turned away from her toward the guests, and she could see the layer of healthy fat at the corner of his jaw, his cheek, the side muscle of his neck, and even his ear, wobbling as he talked. She thought, what an inconceivably over-elaborate use we make of these strips of flesh﻿—our lips, our tongues, our hands, our feet﻿ ﻿… praying, singing, telling lies, explaining philosophies, opening champagne bottles, making watches and guns, dancing, treading out grapes.﻿ ﻿… What a complicated destiny for something that is, after all, nothing but *meat*.﻿ ﻿… The natural thing for lips to utter is a grunt, thought Tatiana﻿—a kind, calm grunt like Seryozha’s. Yet there was the flesh on the sides of her father’s skull all quivering like a jelly to no purpose, the bones all shaking anxiously with superfluous effort.

Pavel poured out three glasses of champagne for his two guests and himself. Wilfred shook his head vigorously. “I never touch alcohol,” he said. “Its dangers have been so well explained to me by Reverend Mr. Oswald Fawcett. And, Miss Ostapenko, I see, does not touch alcohol, either.”

===Chinese===

“我说，这种怨东西是东西的牢骚，我不懂，”谢廖沙说，自觉很男子汉。“就是虱子是虱子，你也怨不着；除非它装作甲虫——那可以怨。要么它咬了你，你可以怨那一口。可咬不咬，它就是它。我不觉得女人是干腐菌，也不觉得她们是别的什么，女人就是女人。她们干她们生来该干的，正像我们干我们的，虱子干虱子的。”
“你们如今的年轻人啊，堂弟，满脑子自相矛盾的议论，”帕维尔和和气气地说，谢廖沙一个字也没听。“我给你讲一段我亲身经历的故事——一段在我看来最能说明女人在男人事务里那份下作角色的故事。失陪一下，开这瓶酒。塔尼娅，钢丝钳。”
塔季扬娜没有起身，也没有放下那双蒙蒙瞪视的眼睛——正落在桌上的火柴盒上——拉开手边一只抽屉。她挑出钢丝钳递给父亲的那一秒，眼睛不得不对准抽屉里的东西。随后她又把那蒙蒙的、出神的眼光定在父亲身上。他半侧着身子朝客人，她看得见他下颌角那层健康的肥肉、他的腮、脖子的侧肌、连耳朵，都随着他说话一颤一颤。她想，我们对这些条状的肉——唇、舌、手、脚——的使用，铺张得多么不可思议…祷告、唱歌、撒谎、讲哲学、开香槟、造表造枪、跳舞、踩葡萄。…一样归根结底不过是肉的东西，命运竟这样繁复。…嘴唇天生该发的话音是哼，塔季扬娜想——一声和善、平稳的哼，像谢廖沙的那样。可父亲脑壳两侧的肉，就这么白白地颤成果子冻，骨头也全带者多余的使劲焦焦地摇。
帕维尔给两位客人同他自己斟了三杯香槟。威尔弗雷德使劲摇头。“我滴酒不沾，”他说。“酒精的危险，奥斯瓦德·福西特牧师给我讲解得太透了。还有，我看奥斯塔片科小姐也滴酒不沾。”

===Original===

Pavel hesitated and then poured out half a glass for his daughter. His attention was drawn by this to Wilfred, and he said: “Your friend might be interested in my story too. What a pity that he speaks no civilised language.”

Wilfred, feeling that he was being referred to, bowed excitedly several times.

“About ten years ago,” said Pavel, settling down. “Or wasn’t it ten years ago? Anyway, at the time the Japanese and Americans were at Vladivostok, I happened to be buying ponies in Mongolia. Wherever there is war, Sergei Sergeievitch, somebody will be ready to pay for horses, and horses are my skill. All Ostapenkos have an eye for a horse. Some people have skill in writing poetry, some in starching evening dress shirts. I have skill in horses. Those little Mongolian horses are excellent in their way﻿—the English race them and play polo with them in Shanghai, I believe﻿—and in the war I found it several times worth while to go down into Mongolia, leaving my wife and baby in Vladivostok, and buy direct from the Mongolian breeders. An amusing expedition, that; the Mongol horse-breeders are decent, hospitable men if you treat them fairly, though﻿—God!﻿—they smell! They live in tents and feed you on mutton﻿—mutton﻿—mutton﻿—cooked sour, somehow﻿—nothing but mutton (except that once I found a horsetail in the big family stewpot. Lord! I can tell you I went out and vomited).”

“What is all this about, Saggay Saggayitch?”

“He eat sheep and horse’s tail,” said Seryozha, and poor Wilfred, astounded, fell back in his chair, trying to hook this information on to anything that had gone before.

“At the time I crossed the border into Mongolia, the out-of-the-way districts were hardly affected at all, as yet, by the revolution or its after effects. Once one got away from the railway zone, one was off the track of politics. So when I returned into Siberia, with fifty good ponies, I was surprised to come across a troop of Red cavalry﻿—the offshoots of Boudeny’s activities, I suppose﻿—under the command of a scoundrel called Ivanov.”

“Bolshevik horses,” said Seryozha in English, seeing Wilfred drawing breath to bleat another hopeless appeal.

===Chinese===

帕维尔迟疑了一下，给女儿斟了半杯。这事把他的注意引到威尔弗雷德身上，他说：“你那位朋友，对我的故事想必也有兴趣。可惜他不会一句文明语言。”
威尔弗雷德觉出说的是自己，兴奋地连连鞠躬。
“约莫十年前，”帕维尔坐定，开讲。“要不不是十年前？反正，日美联军占着海参崴那阵子，我恰好在蒙古买马。哪里有战事，谢尔盖·谢尔盖耶维奇，哪里就有人肯出钱买马，而马是我的专长。奥斯塔片科家的人，个个生就相马的眼。有人擅长写诗，有人擅长浆硬礼服衬衫。我擅长马。那种蒙古小马，自有它的好处——听说英国人在上海拿它们赛马、打马球——战时我几度丢下海参崴的妻小，深入蒙古，直接从养马人手里买，都划得来。好一场有趣的远行；蒙古养马人，你公平待他，是体面好客的汉子，不过——上帝！——他们那个味儿！住帐篷，顿顿羊肉——羊肉——羊肉——炖得发酸，也不知怎么——除了羊肉什么也没有（只有一回，我在他们家的大炖锅里发现一根马尾巴。老天！我跟你说，我出去吐了）。”
“这到底讲的是什么呀，萨盖·萨盖伊奇？”
“他吃羊，还吃马尾巴，”谢廖沙说。可怜的威尔弗雷德惊得往椅背上一倒，拼命想把这条信息钩在前文任何一句话上。
“我过境进蒙古那会儿，僻远地方还几乎没受到革命及其余波的影响。一离开铁路沿线，就出了政治的轨道。所以等我赶着五十匹好马回到西伯利亚，冷不丁撞上一支红军骑兵——多半是布琼尼那一路派生出来的——领头的无赖叫伊万诺夫。”
“布尔什维克的马，”谢廖沙用英语说，他看见威尔弗雷德正吸气、又要哀号一声无望的恳求。

===Original===

“Now I, Sergei Sergeievitch, am an unsentimental man without any prejudices. I am always ready to receive new ideas. My skill in horses is my only fixed idea. Perhaps I shall shock you by saying that﻿—at any rate at that time﻿—though I had steered clear of politics my sympathies were rather with the revolution than against it. Twenty years before, my younger brother had been sent to Siberia, as a student, for speaking at a meeting in Moscow. I never heard of him again, and naturally that had made an impression on me that was hostile to the Tsarist regime. I always behaved as a good and cautious subject of the Tsar, of course, still, I was open-minded about a change of government. So that when I found myself, without warning, in a camp of Red soldiers, and was detained, with my four Korean grooms, I was able to face Ivanov without prejudice or panic. ‘General,’ I said, ‘I’m a man of no prejudices. I’m not afraid of you, and I tell you straight out, I can see that most of your horses are worn out and only fit for a merciful bullet, and that half your men don’t know how to put a saddle across a horse properly or ride the beast when saddled.’ Ivanov was astounded at my sangfroid. ‘Hmph!’ he said.”

Pavel’s voice was quite enough to make himself the hero of his story, unaided by the sense of his words. He quoted himself in a voice of noble clarion courage, and Ivanov in a barbarous snarl. The *hmph* of Ivanov whetted Wilfred’s appetite for explanation to an almost unbearable keenness.

“My goodness gracious! Saggay Saggayitch, what does that *mmpp* mean?”

“He speak, Bolshevik has bad horses.”

“But this story cannot be *all* about horses,” wailed Wilfred.

Pavel, however, swept on: “Well, of course, Ivanov could see at once what kind of man I was, and, in a word, he not only promised to buy my horses, but, since he was likely to be in that valley most of the hot weather, employed me, informally, to lick his detachment into shape, both horse and man. It *was* a job. As a rule, our peasants have what I call ‘horse sense,’ but those louts﻿—my God!﻿—they must have been brought up with newts in a swamp or polar bears on an iceberg. They simply didn’t know which end of a horse was which.”

“I thought you said this story was about women,” said Seryozha, who had been rather touched by Wilfred’s last cry.﻿ ﻿… He added aside to Wilfred, “He speak still about horse.”

===Chinese===

“我这个人，谢尔盖·谢尔盖耶维奇，是个不感情用事、毫无偏见的人。新思想，我随时乐于领受。相马的本事，是我唯一的定见。我这么说兴许吓你一跳——至少在当时——政治我躲得远远的，可我的同情，毋宁在革命一边，而不是反对它。二十年前，我弟弟作为学生，因为在莫斯科一个集会上讲了话，被发配西伯利亚。再无音讯。这自然给我留下一个对沙皇政体敌视的印象。当然，我一向做沙皇规矩、谨慎的臣民；可对改朝换代，我心胸是开的。所以，冷不防落到红军营里，同我的四个高丽马夫一齐被扣，我能不带偏见、不慌不忙地面对伊万诺夫。‘将军，’我说，‘我是个没有偏见的人。我不怕你，我直说：您那些马，多数已熬得脱了形，只配一颗慈悲的子弹；您那些兵，一半连鞍子都上不正，上了鞍也不会骑。’伊万诺夫叫我这份镇定吓着了。‘哼！’他说。”
帕维尔的嗓音，本身就够他稳当故事的主角，用不着词句帮衬。他拿高贵的号角式勇敢腔引自己，拿蛮野的咆哮引伊万诺夫。伊万诺夫那一声“哼”，把威尔弗雷德渴求解释的胃口，吊到了几乎熬不住的锋头。
“我的老天爷！萨盖·萨盖伊奇，那声‘嗯哼’是什么意思？”
“他说，布尔什维克的马不好。”
“可这故事总不能全是讲马吧，”威尔弗雷德哀鸣。
帕维尔却一泻而下：“当然，伊万诺夫一眼看出我是什么路数的人；一句话，他不但答应买下我的马，还——因为热天他多半要泡在那条山谷里——非正式雇了我，把他那支队伍，马和人，一并操练出个样子。那可真是一桩活。咱们庄稼人，照我的说法，天生带‘马感’；可那些粗坯——上帝！——准是沼泽里同蝾螈、冰山上同白熊一块儿长大的。马头马tail，他们压根分不清。”
“我记得您说这故事是讲女人的，”谢廖沙说——威尔弗雷德方才那声哀叫叫他心里软了一软。…他侧头对威尔弗雷德添了一句：“他还在讲马。”

===Original===

“Wait,” said Pavel, with a breathy laugh. “The women will come﻿—they always do, curse them.” He poured out more champagne. He felt very much alive, as he always felt when he had been more than half drunk and, after a period of irritation and partial sobriety, had begun to drink again. He tingled with a glorious heroism; every muscle, every nerve, every thought felt bright as a sword, after a little hour of rusty eclipse. “They were waiting to be reinforced by some artillery before tackling a stray independent party of White military engineers and miners, in a high mining village in the mountains. The Whites were in a very strong position. The only way up to the village was up a very steep ravine, or in a little dangling gravity trolley on a wire (which had, of course, been cut by Ivanov). The village must have been quite fifteen hundred feet above valley level; you could see it like﻿—like﻿—well, like the gold crown on a tall tooth, if you can imagine it, clamped, one might say, on a tiny peaked plateau. A couple of machine-guns could, and did, easily defend the pass. It was a difficult job for Ivanov, and he was particularly anxious to put it through because Colonel Rodin was said to be in command up there﻿—a colonel of engineers who had given a good deal of trouble up and down that region. The place, too, was a regular magnet to all the miners of White sympathies in those mountains. The only thing Ivanov could do for the present was to stop their valley water supply﻿—an elaborate hydraulic business that fed several of the mines from the river. I remember it being constructed several years before by an American mining engineer. Ivanov, of course, put that out of action, but still they held on, so they must have had mountain streams up there, or pretty big emergency cisterns.”

Wilfred bounced in his chair, performing a little impromptu dance on his buttocks. “What is all this about, please?”

“They stop water-pipe,” said Seryozha, and Wilfred, though profoundly puzzled, was at least relieved that the story had left the subject of horses.

===Chinese===

“别忙，”帕维尔说，带者气声的笑。“女人会来的——她们总来，该死的。”他又斟上酒。他自觉生气勃勃——每回他喝过半醉、又经一阵烦躁和半醒、重新开喝，都是这感觉。他浑身一阵辉煌的英雄气；每一块肌肉、每一根神经、每一个念头，经过锈蚀的一小钟头，又亮得像剑。“他们正等炮兵增援，好去收拾山里一座高矿村里一小股零散自守的白军工兵和矿工。白军地势极好。上村只有一条极陡的涧谷，要么坐一只吊在钢索上的小小重力吊车（钢索自然早叫伊万诺夫砍了）。那村子高出谷底足有一千五百英尺；望上去像——像——唔，像一颗高牙上套的金牙冠，你要是想象得出的话，可以说，扣在一小片尖顶平地上。两挺机枪就把山口守得死死的，也确实守着。这对伊万诺夫是块硬骨头，他又特别急于啮下来，因为据说上头统领的是罗金上校——一个工兵上校，那一带叫他搅得不轻。那地方，还是山里所有亲白矿工的一块磁石。眼下伊万诺夫能做的，只有掠断他们谷里的水——一套精巧的水利，从河里喂着好几座矿。我记得是几年前一位美国矿业工程师修的。伊万诺夫自然把它废了；可他们照样撑着，那么上头准有山泉，要不就是顶大的应急水窖。”
威尔弗雷德在椅子上弹跳，拿屁股即兴跳了一小段舞。“请问，这到底讲什么？”
“他们掐了水管，”谢廖沙说。威尔弗雷德虽然一头雾水，总算松了口气：故事总算离开马了。

===Original===

“ ‘My dear Ivanov,’ I said, ‘excuse me, but you simply don’t know the kind of people you’re up against. I know this region; I know these miners; I know dozens of the men in charge of these mines; you’re up against something as stubborn as the mountains themselves, given just these kinds of conditions. They’re adventurers, these miners, and that means they’re individualists, and that again means that in ordinary circumstances every man is for himself﻿—they’re not the “shoulder-to-shoulder” kind, when nothing threatens them; they’d as soon break a man’s nose as shake his hand. Sometimes they’re the sons of political exiles, and sometimes they’ve lived in these mountains so long that they’ve almost forgotten their mother tongue. They don’t sing pretty songs about Holy Russia, or go, with clean faces, to church on Sunday. But once something *does* happen to bring them together (and you may depend on it, Stepan Rodin knows how to handle them), well, I tell you they’ll never give in. You know what Rodin is﻿—people say he’s the son of a priest, you know, and only the adopted son of old Rodin, and I bet he’s made a holy war of this.﻿ ﻿… What’s more, I’ll bet they’ve got a priest in that village, praying night and day while we sit here, to a little packed church. They’ve got their women and children to defend up there, too, and that always makes a difference. Once these fellows remember God﻿—once they feel they’ve got God behind them﻿—well,’ I said (and it was a funny thing to say to a Red anarchist like Ivanov), ‘God *is* behind them.﻿ ﻿…’ You see, Sergei Sergeievitch, I remembered I was a religious man myself. Political reform is one thing, but I was sick of the blasphemy and filthy twaddle in that camp. I don’t see why you can’t set up an enlightened government and still worship God. Well, that’s what I said, anyway﻿—and it was disastrous for me.”

===Chinese===

“‘亲爱的伊万诺夫，’我说，‘恕我直言，你压根不知道你对上的是什么人。这一带我熟；这些矿工我熟；这些矿上管事的我认得一打；条件既然是这样，你对上的，是同大山本身一样倨的东西。这些矿工，是冒险家；冒险家就是个人主义者；个人主义者又意味着：寻常日子人人为己——没人威胁他们时，他们不是那种“肩并肩”的料；握手同打破人鼻子，他们一样情愿。他们有的是政治流放犯的儿子；有的在山里住得太久，母语都快忘了。他们不唱圣俄罗斯的甜歌，礼拜天也不洗净脸上教堂。可一旦真有事情把他们拢起来（你放心，斯捷潘·罗金会使唤他们）——那我告诉你，他们绝不降。你知道罗金是什么人——人家说他是神父的儿子，老罗金的养子——我敢打赌，他把这一仗打成了一场圣战。…再者，我敢打赌，那村里有位神父，趁咱们坐在这儿，正日夜对着挤挤一堂的小教堂祷告。他们上头还有妻小要护着，这一条永远大不一样。这些人一旦想起上帝——一旦觉得上帝在他们背后——那么，’我说（对一个伊万诺夫这样的红色无政府者说这话，是有点滑稽），‘上帝就是在他们背后。…’你瞧，谢尔盖·谢尔盖耶维奇，我想起我自己也是信教的。政治改良是一回事，可那营里的渎神和肮脏昏话，我腻透了。我不明白，为什么立一个开明的政府，就不能照旧敬上帝。反正，我是这么说了——这话给我招了大祸。”

===Original===

The good-natured Seryozha realized that this part of the story would certainly interest the student from the Wesleyan Academy. “In such high mountains they are praying to God, he speak,” said Seryozha, and was rewarded by Wilfred’s look of bland, if blank, gratification. Wilfred’s glass of champagne was strangely empty. Wrapt away in his emotional prison, he had been absently sipping his wine all this time, for lack of anything better to do with his lips. It was at this point that Wilfred was inspired to get out his notebook and his fountain pen and begin to write in English in his insipid clerkly hand. The plot of Pavel’s story as it filtered through to Wilfred, was so very exiguous as hardly to be able to engage even the most optimistic attention, and Wilfred had thought of another way to reestablish himself in the center of the stage. Hardly had he put pen to tooth when, as by a miracle, his glass was full again. He wrote earnestly on, sipping his wine, sucking his pen, writing again, and only rarely lifting up his voice to inquire affably after the progress of the story.

Seryozha was getting bored. He looked at Tatiana across the stream of her father’s voice that ran between them. She seemed to be getting smaller and smaller; she was sinking lower and lower in her chair. She was really trying to be unobtrusive﻿—to be part of the twilight﻿—because if her father should notice her, she was likely to be sent to help her mother and Katya in the kitchen. Tatiana, when still, was very indolent, afraid to break a spell of peace; when moving, she moved ardently, she danced, she ran. Now she hardly breathed. She listened to the story, lending her faint changes of expression to the changing phases of the story﻿—frowning for Ivanov, tossing her head for Rodin, assuming a delicate insolence for her father’s defiances. She and Seryozha, all the time, looked at each other across the river of talk with eyes endowed by wine with a sort of magnifying intensity of sight.

===Chinese===

好心的谢廖沙认定，故事的这一段准能让这位卫斯理学院的学子起劲。“在那样高山上，他们向上帝祷告，他这么讲，”谢廖沙说，得的报偿是威尔弗雷德那一脸虽茫然却受用的喜色。威尔弗雷德那杯香槟奇怪地空了。他囚在自己感情的牢里，这一路嘴巴闲着没事，一直无意识抿酒喝。就在这当口，威尔弗雷德灵机一动，掏出笔记本同钢笔，用他那笔乏味的誊写体，拿英文写起来。帕维尔的故事渗到威尔弗雷德那儿的情节，稀薄得再乐观的注意力也挂不住；威尔弗雷德想出了另一条路，好把自己重新安到戏台中央。笔一沾纸，他的杯子就奇迹般又满了。他一路认真地写，抿一口酒，嘬嘬笔，再写，只是偶尔抬嗓亲切地问一句故事讲到哪儿了。
谢廖沙腻了。他隔着挡在两人之间的她父亲那道话的溪流，望塔季扬娜。她好像越缩越小；在椅子里越坐越低。她是真想不惹眼——想化进暮色里——因为一旦叫父亲看见，多半要打发她去厨房帮母亲和卡佳。塔季扬娜静下来时极懒，怕破了安宁的咒；动起来时又动得炽烈，跳着，跑着。这会儿她几乎不喘气。她听着故事，把脸上淡淡的表情变化，借给故事的各个阶段——替伊万诺夫皱眉，替罗金扬头，替父亲的历次叫板摆出一副纤细的放肆。她同谢廖沙，自始至终隔着那条话语之河对望，眼睛叫酒赋予了一种放大似的强光。

===Original===

Pavel was opening a third bottle of champagne, but he did not stop talking. “What I said made Ivanov uncontrollably angry. I’ve often thought since that he must once have been a man of simple orthodoxy, before Bolshevik propaganda filled his stupid mind. That would account for his anger at what I said; one is always more annoyed by hearing something one might once have said oneself. He said a good many things about my infecting his sound men with outworn superstitions﻿—treason to the Soviet﻿—you know the kind of thing﻿ ﻿… and then he literally kicked me out of camp﻿—unarmed﻿—without a kopek. He’d got my horses, mind you, and not a penny did he pay for them, the dirty scoundrel. ‘You can carry a message to your friends, sitting trusting in their divine cockalorum on that mountain top,’ he shouted. ‘You can tell them that with the hot weather coming on and their water supply in my hands, they’d find it wisest to change sides.﻿ ﻿… You go there and tell them that and stay there,’ he said. ‘Perhaps I’ll pay you your money when you bring them all down, all with your tongues hanging out, ready to lick our hands for a drink.﻿ ﻿…’ I really think he wanted me to carry that message﻿—that’s why he let me go. Anyway, I went. I couldn’t do anything else. His men had taken all my money and papers and my grooms had disappeared. I just thrust my chest out and walked up the pass﻿—as well as I could for a kick one of the dirty brutes had given me on the ankle. The defenders wouldn’t waste their machine-gun ammunition on one lame man. I shouted, ‘Don’t shoot,’ and waved my arms, and they let me come. They searched me, and then I had a talk with Rodin. Would you believe it? there were some quite good-looking women in the village﻿—Russian refugee women, I mean, not the miners’ wives﻿—refugees who’d drifted there in Rodin’s wake, I suppose.﻿ ﻿… Everything was upside down in those days. My God! they were hard up for water. There was a little cascade in the ravine, but it was almost dried up, and their cisterns were almost empty. No likelihood of rain, either, and, of course, not a drop coming up from the valley. Ivanov had seen to that. People were allowed to drink by measure. I saw a woman faint in the street, the day I got there, and next day a young fellow had a kind of fit. I liked Rodin, and he saw at once what kind of man I was. We found we had friends in common.

===Chinese===

帕维尔开第三瓶香槟，话没停。“我那番话把伊万诺夫气得按不住。我后来常想，在布尔什维克宣传灌满他那个蠢脑子之前，他准一度是个信正教的简单人。这可以解释他那场火：听见一句自己从前也许说过的话，永远最叫人冒火。他数落了我一大篇——说我拿过时的迷信腐蚀他手下健全的人——背叛苏维埃——你知道那路话…随后他真的把我踢出了营——赤手空拳——一个戈比不给。他占了我的马，你记着，一个大子儿也没付，那脏无赖。‘你可以给你的朋友们捎个信，’他喊，‘他们坐在那山顶上，信赖他们那座神圣的空中楼阁。你告诉他们：热天眼看就到，水攥在我手心里，识相的赶紧换边。…你去那儿，把话带到，就留在那儿，’他说。‘等你把他们全带下来——一个个舌头耷拉着、巴望着舔咱们的手讨口水喝——兴许我付你钱。…’我到如今还认为，他真想叫我捎那句话——所以才放我走。反正，我去了。我别无出路。他的人拿走了我全部的钱和证件，我的马夫也不见了。我就那么挺起胸膛，往山口上走——脚脖子还挨了那些脏畜生一个踢，能走成什么样走什么样。守军犯不上为一个瘸腿的汉子浪费机枪子弹。我喊‘别开枪’，挥着胳膊，他们放我上去了。他们搜了我，随后我同罗金谈了。你信不信？村里有几个相当好看的女人——俄国流亡女人，我是说，不是矿工的老婆——多半是跟着罗金的尾巴漂到那儿的。…那些年头，什么都颠倒。上帝！他们缺水缺得凶。涧谷里有一线小瀑布，可几乎干了；水窖也快见底。又没有雨的指望；谷里，当然，一滴水也休想上来。伊万诺夫把这手办绝了。喝水按人头定量。我到的那天，亲眼见一个女人晕倒在街上；第二天一个小伙子发了羊癎风似的抽风。我喜欢罗金，他也一眼看出我是什么路数。我们发现彼此有共同的朋友。

===Original===

I was with him when a group of men﻿—the village storekeeper, the publican, a couple of mine agents, and some such outsiders, came and asked him to give up the village or take his soldiers somewhere else. ‘Better give up than die of thirst,’ they said. ‘We’ll have to give up some time, at this rate. You said we were to trust in God, but he doesn’t send us water, so it couldn’t be much worse for us if we put our trust in Red Ivanov.’ Rodin said, ‘Wait five days’﻿—just like that﻿—‘wait five days.’ He said it so confidently and so mysteriously that the deputation thought he’d got some idea up his sleeve. When they’d gone I said, ‘My dear Colonel, what in the world do you expect will happen in five days? The Second Advent?’ ‘Oh, anything may happen,’ he said. ‘I never look more than four days ahead; the fifth day is the one I can’t see, so I always expect a good deal of it.’ And that day, while he and I were talking, Julia Arcadievna sent to ask Rodin to spare her a few minutes. She was the widow of a man who’d died of sunstroke during the retreat across Siberia, and there she was drifting east with the refugees, and had somehow drifted here﻿—one never knew where people would turn up in those days﻿—but she was absolutely virtuous, my dear Sergei Sergeievitch, absolutely virtuous.”

Seryozha shifted in his chair, his big limbs tired of stillness. He became aware that a certain activity in the slowly spinning room was to be identified as the appearance of dinner. He saw the bottles fly upward to allow for the laying of the cloth and then descend again, upright, like angels. He saw blue plates settling like leaves upon the cloth. The movements of Varvara and the servant were like a little controlled wind making all these orderly displacements.

“What does *dobbri-dyet-il-niar genstchina* mean?” asked Wilfred, but not in the manner of one who really wants very much to know. His notebook, like the bottles and glasses, had been mysteriously snatched to heaven, a white cloud of cloth filled his sight, and now the notebook alighted again, ready for use, with a full glass of champagne beside it.

Seryozha could not remember the English words for a virtuous woman. “*Nyet* singing girl,” he said, uncertainly.

===Chinese===

“我正在他跟前，一伙人——村中杂货店主、酒馆老板、两个矿上经纪人，几个这一类的外人——来求他交出村子，或把兵带往别处。‘与其渴死，不如交出，’他们说。‘照这样下去，早晚总要交。你说咱们信上帝，可他不送水来；那么咱们信红色伊万诺夫，也不见得更糟。’罗金说：‘等五天’——就那么一句——‘等五天。’他说得那样笃定、那样神秘，代表团以为他袖里藏着锦囊。他们一走，我就说：‘亲爱的上校，您到底指望五天里出什么事？基督再临吗？’‘哦，什么都可能出，’他说。‘我从不往前看超过四天；第五天是我看不见的，所以我总指望它出点大事。’就在那天，我们正谈着，尤利娅·阿尔卡季耶夫娜打发人来，请罗金赏几分钟。她是个寡妇，丈夫在横穿西伯利亚的撤退中中了暑死的；她随难民往东漂，不知怎么漂到了这儿——那些年头人会在哪儿冒出来，谁也说不上——可她绝对贞洁，亲爱的谢尔盖·谢尔盖耶维奇，绝对贞洁。”
谢廖沙在椅子里挪了挪，大个子四肢坐乏了。他察觉，那间慢慢旋转的屋子里某一阵活动，可以认定为晚饭的降临。他看见酒瓶先腾空而起，让出铺桌布的地方，随后又落回来，立得端端正正，像天使。他看见蓝盘子像叶子落满桌布。瓦尔瓦拉同女仆的动作，像一阵有节制的小风，调度着这井然的迁移。
“‘多布里-焦季-尼尔-金斯奇纳’是什么意思？”威尔弗雷德问，可不像真心想知道的样子。他的笔记本，也同酒瓶酒杯一样，神秘地被摄上了天，一团白布云灌满他的视野，随后笔记本又落回来，可以用了，旁边还配了一满杯香槟。
“贞洁女人”的英文，谢廖沙想不起来了。“不是歌女，”他含含糊糊地说。

===Original===

Katya, the servant, leaning over Pavel with a plate, said: “Have you heard the news, Varvara Alexeievna? Piotr Gavrilovitch has joined the Chinese army. He wrote from Mukden yesterday. I heard it from Olga Ivanovna’s niece’s woman.”

Pavel glanced with vindictiveness at his daughter. “Ha, Tanya! News about your Petya. That disposes of another of them, doesn’t it? Tanya, you are listening to my story, aren’t you?” He went on: “Well, Sergei Sergeievitch, when this virtuous woman saw Rodin, she said, ‘Stepan Stepanovitch, what have you said? Five days? You have given God five days to save us in. What blasphemy to set such a limit. If faith can sustain us for five days, why not five hundred? Surely we can wait on God’s will for longer than five days.’ ‘There’s such a thing as being too thirsty to trust in God,’ said Rodin. ‘There’s such a thing as having too dry a throat to pray with. Not for people like you and me, Julia Arcadievna, but for the ordinary ruck. Even five days is something. If you pray for us, perhaps something will happen to help us, even in five days.’ He had a great admiration for Julia Arcadievna. I always say myself that women are in the way when there’s fighting to be done. If it hadn’t been for all those women and children, we could have given up our position and tried our luck against Ivanov’s men in the valley. They outnumbered us, of course, but they were a poor lot, as I knew. Still, Julia Arcadievna was a damned handsome woman, and intelligent, too. She said: ‘I’ve got an idea. I won’t tell you what it is. If it fails, nobody but me loses anything. So don’t ask me any questions, but come to the head of the pass tonight, Stepan Stepanovitch, and wish me luck.’ ‘Why, where will you be going?’ ‘Don’t ask questions, I tell you. I’ve always thought I was born to be a spy.’ Would you believe it, Rodin thought so much of that woman, he let her go. He even told me he had great faith in her. It’s wonderful how good looks in women go to men’s heads. Wonderful,” he repeated in a different and savage tone, turning his white glare on his daughter.

===Chinese===

女仆卡佳端着盘子，俰身对帕维尔说：“您听说新闻没有，瓦尔瓦拉·阿列克谢耶夫娜？彼得·加夫里洛维奇投中国军队了。昨天从奉天来信。奥莉加·伊万诺夫娜的侄女家的女仆告诉我的。”
帕维尔狠狠瞥了女儿一眼。“哈，塔尼娅！你的彼佳的消息。这又结果了一个，是不是？塔尼娅，你在听我的故事吧？”他接着讲：“就这样，谢尔盖·谢尔盖耶维奇，这位贞洁的女人见了罗金，说：‘斯捷潘·斯捷潘诺维奇，您说了什么？五天？您给上帝限了五天来救我们。限定这样的期限，是何等的亵渎。信心若能撑我们五天，为什么不能五百天？等候上帝的旨意，我们总能等过五天。’‘有一种情形，叫渴得信不动上帝，’罗金说。‘有一种情形，叫喉咙干得祷告不出。不是说你我这样的人，尤利娅·阿尔卡季耶夫娜，是说普通的一群。五天也是个数。您若替我们祷告，兴许就有什么来助我们，哪怕就在五天里。’他对尤利娅·阿尔卡季耶夫娜怀着大敬仰。我自己总说，真打仗，女人碍事。若不是那些妇幼，我们本可让出阵地，下山谷去同伊万诺夫的人碰碰运气。他们人多，当然，可那是一伙孬种，这我清楚。不过，尤利娅·阿尔卡季耶夫娜真是他妈的漂亮女人，还聪明。她说：‘我有个主意。我不告诉你们是什么。要是砸了，赔的只是我一个。所以别问我问题，今晚到山口顶上来，斯捷潘·斯捷潘诺维奇，祝我好运。’‘哦，您上哪儿去？’‘叫你别问。我一向认为，我天生是当密探的料。’你信不信，罗金那样看重那女人，竟放她去了。他甚至对我说，他对她有十足的信心。女人的好相貌，上男人的头，真是灵极了。灵极了，”他又说一遍，换了一副狠腔，把白瞪的眼转到女儿身上。

===Original===

“Rodin was a curious fellow﻿—a fine-looking fellow, but quite bald,” went on Pavel, stroking his own thick auburn hair, glad to establish superiority over the hero he spoke of. “Plenty of hair on his upper lip, but none on his head. He and I went to the sandbag barricade at the top of the pass that evening﻿—for by that time he trusted me absolutely, and certainly it wasn’t likely I should hold any more with these revolutionaries after they’d behaved like that over my horses. At sunset Julia Arcadievna, with her little Korean maid carrying a parcel, came mincing down the pass. Good lord!﻿—what a change! She’d put aside all her widow’s blacks and there she was in a sandy-colored Paris-looking frock with red embroidery, and no hat or veil to hide her pretty yellow hair. She looked stunning. She’d rouged up her lips and blacked her eyes a little. She’d a way of opening her eyes suddenly very wide as she looked at you.﻿ ﻿… ‘Let your gunners and sentries know I shall be coming back in four days, Stepan Stepanovitch,’ she said. So she went. She went in the evening, I suppose so that she could pretend to Ivanov’s men that she’d slipped away unseen down the pass.”

“Well, did she come back?” asked Seryozha. The widow, Julia Arcadievna, sounded to him almost too mature to be interesting. He imagined a brazen hard-bosomed blonde of thirty﻿—almost an aunt’s age. Seryozha could just remember meeting his mother’s sister in Russia before the revolution, and women admired by older men always ever since appeared in his mind’s sight as shrill, plump, vivacious old women of twenty-nine or so.

“Yes, she came back,” said Pavel, looking almost alarmed at the climax of his story. “She came back on the evening of the fourth day, with a parcel.”

“Well﻿ ﻿… she went away with a parcel didn’t she?” said Seryozha.

“Yes﻿ ﻿… but this was a different parcel﻿—bigger and heavier. She opened it in the presence of Rodin and me and a lot of the men, on the terrace looking over the valley. It was wrapped up in an ordinary army blanket. It was Ivanov’s head.”

Seryozha snorted with surprise. “Ivanov’s head! How had she killed him?”

===Chinese===

“罗金是个怪人——仪表堂堂，可是全秃，”帕维尔接着讲，摸着自己厚厚的赭色头发，乐于压倒他讲到的英雄一筹。上唇的毛倒是厚，头顶上一根无。那天傍晚，他同我上了山口顶的沙袋工事——那时节他已信我信到十足；他们那样待了我的马，我当然不会再同那些革命党裹在一处。日落时分，尤利娅·阿尔卡季耶夫娜带着她的小高丽女仆，抱着一个包裹，一路小碎步下了山口。老天爷！——多大的变化！寡妇的一身黑全褪了，换了条沙色、巴黎款式的连衣裙，绣着红，不戴帽子不蒙纱，好一头黄头发亮着。她美得惊人。唇上了红，眼描黑了一点。她有一种本事：望着你时，眼睛忽然睁得极圆。…‘告诉您的炮手同哨兵，四天后我回来，斯捷潘·斯捷潘诺维奇，’她说。她就这样去了。傍晚走的，我看，为的是好对伊万诺夫的人装作趁黑溜下山口、没叫人看见。”
“那么，她回来了吗？”谢廖沙问。那位寡妇尤利娅·阿尔卡季耶夫娜，听着他只觉熟过了头，提不起劲。他想象一个三十岁、黄头发、胸脯硬邦邦、脸皮老厚的女人——差不多是姨妈的年纪。谢廖沙还依稀记得革命前在俄国见过母亲的妹妹；打那以后，凡是叫年长男人倾倒的女人，在他心眼里一概是二十八九岁、又胖又欢、嗓子尖利的老太婆。
“回来了，”帕维尔说，讲到故事顶点，他自己倒几乎一惊。“第四天傍晚，她回来了，抱着个包裹。”
“唔…她走时不也抱着个包裹么？”谢廖沙说。
“是…可这是另一个包裹——更大，更沉。她当着罗金、我同好多人的面，在俯瞰山谷的平台上打开。裹在一床普通军毯里。是伊万诺夫的头。”
谢廖沙惊得哼了一声。“伊万诺夫的头！她怎么杀的他？”

===Original===

“*Tschah!* I leave you to imagine. In a woman’s way, of course. Poor devil! Playing on his manhood. That’s a woman’s way, Sergei Sergeievitch. And what do you think she told Rodin and me? That she hadn’t actually slept with Ivanov. She hadn’t even given the poor beast that satisfaction. Lord! it was a disgusting thing to see her holding up that head﻿—all bloody it was﻿—with both hands, smiling a smug, rouged, womanly smile.”

“Well, what happened? I suppose the revolution didn’t stop for lack of Ivanov’s head,” said Seryozha, prosaically.

“No, but the troop moved off in the dawn of the next day. I watched them go﻿—little lolloping specks in loose formation. I knew why, of course﻿—they were nothing but raw louts; only Ivanov held them together; they were lost without him. Some of our miners went out along the mountains and sniped them when they came to the narrow head of the valley. They even got back some of my horses.”

Varvara watched her husband. Only she knew how little of his story was true, and she did not mind. On the contrary, the story seemed to her a wonderful and brilliant fruit to have grown out of the seed of a little drab anecdote about a miner’s Yakut woman who went out spying. Nobody was like Pavel, thought Varvara. He was her contribution to the sum of things﻿—and, through him, Tatiana. She made no other contribution, and therefore had no other return. She sat self-contained, contributing no smile, no wit, no generosity, no money, no tyranny, no song﻿—none of those forms of invested capital which alone pay dividends of friendship, fear, gratitude, love, or power. Nothing comes to the heart that hasn’t first gone *from* it; Varvara knew that. But she didn’t mind, because Pavel and Tatiana were her songs, and the voices of Pavel and Tatiana the returning echoes of her songs.

“Is this the end of the story?” asked Wilfred, noticing a silence, in spite of a slight fogginess of perception.

“Yist. She cutted off Bolshevik head.”

“Good!” said Wilfred, feeling himself at last in possession of the facts. “*Horosho, horosho*,” he added, nodding brightly to Pavel.

“Did you listen to my story, Tanya?” asked Pavel, suddenly turning on his daughter.

“Yes, papasha.”

“A good one, wasn’t it?”

“Good, but perhaps a little long,” said Tatiana’s mother, hastily.

“I’m glad, at least, she kept herself clean of him,” said Tatiana between trembling lips.

===Chinese===

“嚓！你自己去想吧。反正用女人的法子。可怜鬼！拿他的男子气做文章。这就是女人的路数，谢尔盖·谢尔盖耶维奇。你猜她后来同罗金同我说了什么？说她其实没同伊万诺夫睡过。她连那可怜畜生这点满足都没给。老天！看她双手捧着那颗头——血淋淋的——脸上挂着又自得、又搽了红的、女人家的笑，那景象真叫人恶心。”
“后来呢？我想革命不会因为缺一颗伊万诺夫的头就停了，”谢廖沙平淡地说。
“不会，可那支队伍第二天拂晓就开走了。我看着他们走的——稀稀拉拉一小摄蹦跳的黑点。原因我当然清楚——那不过是一伙生坯子；全靠伊万诺夫一个人拢着；没了他，他们就散了。我们的矿工有几个顺山梁出去，在谷口窄处放了他们的冷枪。连我的马，都要回来几匹。”
瓦尔瓦拉望者丈夫。只有她知道这故事里有多少是真的；她也不在乎。相反，在她看来，这个故事，是从一粒灰扑扑的小传闻——一个矿工的雅库特女人出去探信——的种子里，长出的一只又奇又亮的果子。没人比得上帕维尔，瓦尔瓦拉想。他是她投向万物之总和的一份——并通过他，还有塔季扬娜。她没有别的投放，也就没有别的回收。她自给自足地坐着，不放出一个微笑、一句机锋、一分慷慨、一文钱、一次专横、一支歌——不放出任何那类资本；唯有它们，才付得出友谊、畏惧、感激、爱或权力的股息。不曾从心出去过的东西，永远到不了心；瓦尔瓦拉懂这个。可她不在乎，因为帕维尔同塔季扬娜就是她的歌，帕维尔同塔季扬娜的声音，就是她的歌回荡的回声。
“故事讲完了吗？”威尔弗雷德问，尽管知觉已有几分雾蒙蒙，还是察觉出一场安静。
“完了。她把布尔什维克的头割下来了。”
“好！”威尔弗雷德说，自觉终于掌握了事实。“哈拉少，哈拉少，”他朝帕维尔亮亮地点头。
“你方才听我的故事了吗，塔尼娅？”帕维尔忽然转向女儿。
“听了，爸爸。”
“好听吧？”
“好听，就是也许长了点儿，”塔季扬娜的母亲赶忙说。
“我很高兴，至少她没让自己被他弄脏，”塔季扬娜哆嗦着嘴唇说。

===Original===

“What?” roared Pavel.

The usually restrained Varvara suddenly made a great noise, clapping her hands, rapping the table, speaking in a shrill brisk voice: “Do you know what we are going to have for dinner? A gosling﻿—a gosling in honor of our young cousin.” She looked desperately at her husband and spoke more loudly still: “Oh, so good﻿—you’ll never guess how good, Pavlik!﻿ ﻿… You’ve all been talking so long, you men, that Katya and I had plenty of time to roast it to a turn. A gosling﻿—a gosling﻿—*oh* so good!” She banged the table for her husband’s attention.

“What is she saying?” asked Wilfred, startled.

“She speak, we bite goose for dinner.”

“My goodness gracious!” breathed Wilfred. “I thought she was announcing some calamity. So loud.” He looked ruefully at his notebook, for the sudden noise had caused his shocked hand to make a little blot in the middle of one page of his neat writing.

Pavel, though his impulse of anger was a little softened by the goose, would not allow his attention to be entirely distracted by his wife’s irrelevance. “Very well done, Varitchka. A gosling. Very good. But, Tanya﻿—*what* was your comment on my story?”

Tatiana was silent. “Tanya made no comment,” said Varvara.

===Chinese===

“什么？”帕维尔吼道。
素来矜持的瓦尔瓦拉忽然大响起来，拍手，敲桌，尖利而急促地说：“你们知道晚饭吃什么吗？一只小鹅——一只小鹅，敬我们年轻堂弟的。”她绝望地看看丈夫，嗓门更高：“哦，好得很——你猜不着多好，帕夫利克！…你们男人聊了这么半天，卡佳同我有的是工夫，把它烤得恰到火候。一只小鹅——一只小鹅——哦，好得很！”她拍桌子拍得山响，好夺回丈夫的注意。
“她说什么？”威尔弗雷德吓了一跳。
“她说，晚饭我们吃鹅。”
“我的老天爷！”威尔弗雷德出了口气。“我还当是宣布什么灾祸呢。嗓门这么大。”他心疼地看看笔记本——方才那一声巨响，震得他那只受惊的手，在工整的一页字正当中，洇了一小团墨。
帕维尔虽然那阵火气叫小鹅软化了几分，却不容妻子的风马牛把注意整个岔开。“办得好，瓦里奇卡。一只小鹅。很好。可是塔尼娅——你对我那段故事，到底‘评’了句什么？”
塔季扬娜不作声。“塔尼娅什么也没评，”瓦尔瓦拉说。

===Original===

Yet in Seryozha’s hearing was ringing quite clearly the comment that Tatiana had made. I’m glad, at least, she kept herself clean of him. What a curious word﻿—“clean”﻿—he thought, and his thoughts went round and round it in a slow spin. *Clean﻿—clean﻿—clean*﻿ ﻿… women *clean* of men﻿—men *clean* of women. He was young enough to be very impressionable by words, and the word “clean” did not strike him as being a euphemism for something much less attractive. On the contrary, in his present thin-spun, rarefied, wine-blessed mood, it seemed to him to express an ideal of some sort. That was the word that fitted his own rare moon-washed feeling﻿—that hunger in the presence of quietness and color for something that would not appease the demands of either stomach, brain, or sex. Poor Seryozha! He almost recognized that hunger of his at that moment, though he could give it no other word than “clean.” It was his body hungering to be free of talk and understanding﻿—hungering to be a bit of world, a blade of grass, a tiger﻿—anything that was not dirtied by talk and thought. Everything between men and women was dirty﻿—except one thing. “That girl﻿—one could *eat* that girl, and remain clean,” thought Seryozha.

He was obsessed with the image of hunger, since the gosling had been mentioned.

“Tanya,” said Varvara, clearing away the *zakuska*, “come and help me dish up the goose.”

===Chinese===

可谢廖沙的耳朵里，还清清楚楚响着塔季扬娜评的那一句：我很高兴，至少她没让自己被他弄脏。多古怪的一个词——“干净”——他想，念头绕着它慢慢打转。干净——干净——干净…女人对男人“干净”——男人对女人“干净”。他还年轻，极容易被词打动；“干净”这个词，在他听来丝毫不像什么不堪之物的婉辞。相反，在他此刻这薄纱似的、稀薄的、蒙酒福的心境里，它像表达了某种理想。那个词，正配他自己难得的、月光洗过的感觉——在安静与颜色跟前的那场饥饿，胃、脑、性都填不了。可怜的谢廖沙！那一刻他几乎认出了自己那场饥饿，只是除了“干净”再也给不出别的名字。那是他的身体，饿着要摆脱言语与理解——要做一小片世界、一片草叶、一只虎——任何没叫言语和思想弄脏的东西。男人女人之间的一切都是脏的——除了一样。“那个姑娘——人可以‘吃’那个姑娘，还保持干净，”谢廖沙想。
小鹅一提，他就叫饥饿的意象缠住了。
“塔尼娅，”瓦尔瓦拉收拾着下酒小菜说，“来帮我盛鹅。”

===Original===

The two women went to the door. As Tatiana passed Seryozha, he expected her to smile at him; he felt so sure that an understanding had been established between them, that she would be grateful to him for so keenly appreciating her word “clean.” But she did not smile at all. She walked past him, meeting his eager eyes without a change of expression, grave, blank, as though thinking of something else. And so strange was the chill that this blank look gave him, that his memory, shocked, threw back to the context of her remark, and he knew instantly that he had entirely misunderstood her words. She kept herself clean of him. By clean she meant cold, dead﻿—all life was dirty to her. Her ideal was coldness. No tiger of life could hunger for that white unsmiling ghost. Seryozha thought, “If I had her, she’d melt like ice in my arms﻿—she’d be *clean* of me, then.” The word *cold* occurred to him many times﻿—*cold﻿—cold﻿—cold*﻿—before he realized what it suggested to him, and then he got up, a little unsteadily, and followed mother and daughter toward the kitchen. In the passage he paused beside where his pack of possessions hung on a peg. As he unstrapped the buckle of the pack he heard Wilfred, in the sitting-room, begin to entertain Pavel Ostapenko in a bright voice. Wilfred was not sober enough to mind that his host did not understand a word that he was saying; his automatic reaction to the silence was an impulse to emit information. Pavel was not sober enough to mind not understanding, and in any case he had something else to think of.

“The Japanese police, Mr. Ozz﻿ ﻿…” said Wilfred, “show themselves indeed marticrats﻿—(marticrats? Yes, mautonets)﻿—to travelers crossing the border, and at one point I really began to think, I really began to think that I really began to think﻿—oh, well, Mr. Ozzabanko, you know all that without really beginning to think﻿—I mean, without my telling you. But being a barrister-at-law of the Middle Temple, London, I could say to them.﻿ ﻿… And the Diamond Mountains﻿—Kongo-san﻿—though honeysuckled with soup﻿—I mean soupstition﻿—su‑per‑stit‑ion﻿—Saggay Saggayitch said ‘Buddhanok on every up-look,’ twelve thousand peaks, you know, but not really more than﻿—well﻿—what would be a conservatory estimate?” He began to think very deeply.

===Chinese===

两个女人朝门口走去。塔季扬娜经过谢廖沙身边时，他等她朝自己一笑；他那样确信两人之间已立了一份默契，确信她该感激自己这样痛切地品出了她那句“干净”。可她根本没有笑。她走过他跟前，迎上他热切的眼睛，神色不变，端肃，茫然，像正想着别的。这一记白眼叫他心头的凉，凉得那样怪，他的记忆吃了一惊，把她的评语倒回上下文里重放了一遍，他当下明白：他把她的话整个儿听岔了。她没让自己被他弄脏。她说“干净”，意思是冷，是死——一切生命于她都是脏。她的理想是冷。生命的猛虎，决不会朝着那尊不笑的白鬼去饿。谢廖沙想：“我要是得了她，她会在我的怀里化，像冰——那时她就‘干净’地摆脱我了。”冷这个词，在他心里转了许多回——冷——冷——冷——他才悟出它暗示的是什么；随后他站起来，脚下有点飘，跟着母女俩往厨房去。过道里，他在他那包行李挂着的钉子旁停下。他解着背包的搭扣，听见起居室里威尔弗雷德正拿一副亮嗓，招待帕维尔·奥斯塔片科。威尔弗雷德还没清醒到在乎主人一个字也听不懂；沉默之于他的自动反应，就是一阵倾吐信息的冲动。帕维尔也没清醒到在乎听不懂，再说他另有心事。
“日本警察，奥佐先生…”威尔弗雷德说，“对过境旅人，实在摆足‘马提克拉特’（贵族）的架子——（马提克拉特？不对，‘毛托内’——专制者）——有一阵我真开始觉得、真开始觉得我真开始觉得——哦算了，奥扎班科先生，这些不用我开始觉得您也知道——我是说，不用我告诉您。不过呢，身为伦敦中殿的出庭律师，我本可以对他们说。…还有金刚山——Kongo-san——虽然让汤缠满了——我是说‘汤信’——迷-信——萨盖·萨盖伊奇说‘抬头处处佛塔’，一万二千峰，您知道，可其实不会多过——唔——该算暖房估值呢？”他开始深深地思考。

===Original===

Seryozha in the passage took out of his pack the little smelly package that contained the heart, gall, and liver of the fish he had caught at Pa-tao-kou. It was in a perfectly disgusting condition, yet to Seryozha the smell was a smell of fate and magic; everything that Wilfred had said about it seemed to fit the circumstances so well. Blindness. Woman’s coldness. Real magic indeed. The thing *must* have other powers besides its powerful smell. With his clasp-knife he cut a small piece from the heart and another small piece from the liver, and then, wrapping the rest in its paper, he replaced it in his pack. The two small flabby chunks he concealed in his hand and, striding into the kitchen, dropped them in the fire.

“What a curious smell!” said Varvara at once. Tatiana, pouring sauce into a bowl on the table, made no comment. She held her body too upright﻿—almost bent a little backward; her head was high and she was looking down over her pale cheeks at the sauce as though she despised it. Really she was thinking of Seryozha’s skin. As he had stood for a second at the fireplace, she had noticed that the skin at the back of his neck was not pitted or pimpled like Sasha’s or Petya’s. This meant a great deal to her. His neck was as neat as the fur of a dog, or the charming behind of a well-kept horse, she thought.

The servant, Katya, seized the dish that bore the gosling in her brawny bare arms and carried it towards the sitting-room. Varvara followed with the vegetables.

Tatiana still stood at the table, but now, instead of looking down at the sauce, she looked sidelong at Seryozha, soberly tightening her lips.

“Why do you look like that, Tatiana Pavlovna?” asked Seryozha, holding the corner of the table, for he felt extremely dizzy.

Tatiana was silent for an unexpectedly long second, and then, “O Christ!” she exclaimed in a high sudden voice. “Can I not even *look* like myself without being asked questions﻿—without surprising somebody, offending somebody, hurting somebody’s feelings, making somebody reproach me? Even if I do nothing﻿—good or bad﻿—only just *be*﻿—my *being’s* not allowed.”

“Who doesn’t allow it?” asked Seryozha, swaying over the table toward her, feeling very uneasy.

===Chinese===

谢廖沙在过道里，从背包里取出那个小臭包——八道沟那条鱼的心、胆、肝。它已经沤得相当不堪；可对谢廖沙，那气味是一股命运和法术的气味；威尔弗雷德讲过的一切，仿佛都同眼下这局面严丝合缝。失明。女人的冷淡。真正的法术。这东西除了气味厉害，必定还有别的威力。他拿折刀从鱼心上削下一小片，又从肝上削下一小片，其余的原纸包好，放回背包。那两片软塌塌的小块，他攢在手心，大步进厨房，丢进了火里。
“什么怪味儿！”瓦尔瓦拉立刻说。塔季扬娜正往桌上一只碗里倒调味汁，不作声。她身子挺得太直——几乎微微后仰；头昂着，越过苍白的腮帮俯视那碗汁，像看不起它似的。其实她想的是谢廖沙的皮肤。他在炉边站过一秒，她注意到他后颈的皮肤不坑不痘，不像萨沙的、也不像彼佳的。这对她非同小可。他那个脖子干净，像狗的毛，像一匹好生饲养的马那可爱的臀，她想。
女仆卡佳用两条粗赤的胳膊端起盛鹅的大盘，朝起居室送去。瓦尔瓦拉端着蔬菜跟上。
塔季扬娜还站在桌边，可这会儿，她不低头看调味汁了，侧过眼来看谢廖沙，庄重地抿紧嘴唇。
“您为什么那样望，塔季扬娜·帕夫洛夫娜？”谢廖沙问，一手扶着桌角，他晕得厉害。
塔季扬娜静了出人意料的长长一秒，随后，“哦基督！”她尖地喊出声来。“难道我连‘像我自个儿’地看一眼都不行——不叫谁吃惊，不冒犯谁，不伤谁的感情，不招谁数落？哪怕我什么都不干——好坏都不干——光是‘在’——我的‘在’也不被准许。”
“谁不准许？”谢廖沙问，朝她晃过桌面，心里很不安。

===Original===

“You﻿—papa﻿—mamma﻿—Petya﻿—Sasha. Why do you *look* like that, Tanya? Why do you *do* like that, Tanya? Why are you alive in *that* way, Tanya? Why aren’t you alive in *this* way, Tanya? What can I do less than just be born, breathe, and at last die? I’m not attacking, not pretending﻿ ﻿… I just *am*. I only ask to leave you all alone and be left alone.”

“Oh, not be left alone, surely!” exclaimed Seryozha, shocked to imagine such a small creature growing old alone.

“No, not to be left alone any more than a common sparrow is left alone because it is a sparrow,” said Tatiana, violently. “Just to be allowed to walk on the earth in my natural way, as a sparrow is allowed to fly in the air. No bird cuts its throat for the sake of another bird. No father bird nags at his egg for just lying quiet in the nest.”

They both smiled for a second or two at this.

“I could leave you alone,” said Seryozha. “God knows it’s a thing I ought to understand. I only want things to walk about and fly about by themselves.﻿ ﻿… I like my dog to go on laughing at his own jokes without me.﻿ ﻿… What am I saying? I’d never cut my throat for anybody, Tatiana Pavlovna.”

“Come, children, come,” called Varvara from the sitting-room.

But as Tatiana and Seryozha, carrying plates and sauce, joined their elders, Pavel said, “Here’s this snake of a daughter of mine﻿ ﻿… here’s this virtuous murderous heroine coming back with the head of a new murdered lover in her hands. Here’s this﻿—”

“Pavlik! Pavlik! Think! Think!” said Varvara, in a low violent voice.

“Come, everybody,” she added, clapping her hands with a relapse into her unnatural shrill vivacity. “Gosling to eat﻿—good gosling to eat. Come and eat gosling.”

“I won’t eat,” said Seryozha, suddenly seizing the end of the table and bending, with an insolent but tremulous grin, toward his host and hostess﻿—“I won’t eat until you say I can marry Tanya. Please, Mr. Chew, help me. Oh, but, damn it, he can’t speak the language! Well, there it is.﻿ ﻿… Pavel Nicholaievitch, may I marry your daughter?”

===Chinese===

“你——爸爸——妈妈——彼佳——萨沙。你为什么‘那样’看，塔尼娅？你为什么‘那样’做，塔尼娅？你为什么用‘那种’法子活着，塔尼娅？你为什么不用‘这种’法子活着，塔尼娅？我能做的，比出生、呼吸、末了一死，还能更少吗？我不攻击谁，不装假…我就是我。我只求让你们大家清静，也让我清静。”
“哦，总不能清静！”谢廖沙喊，一想到这样一个小小的人儿孤零零变老，他吓了一跳。
“不，并不比一只寻常麻雀因为它是麻雀就更该被撇清，”塔季扬娜激烈地说。“只求容我照我天生的样子在地上走，像麻雀天生被容在空中飞。没有一只鸟为另一只鸟割喉。没有一只父鸟数落他的蛋，怪它只在窝里安安静静躺着。”
两人为此都笑了一两秒。
“我能让你清静，”谢廖沙说。“天知道，这道理我本该懂。我巴不得样样东西自个儿走、自个儿飞。…我喜欢我的狗没我看着也自顾自笑它的笑话。…我在说什么呀。我可不会为谁割喉，塔季扬娜·帕夫洛夫娜。”
“来吧，孩子们，来吧，”瓦尔瓦拉在起居室里喊。
可塔季扬娜同谢廖沙端着盘子端着汁，刚要归入长辈一桌，帕维尔说话了：“瞧我这蛇一样的女儿…瞧这贞洁的杀人女英雄回来了，手里捧着一颗新宰的情人的头。瞧这——”
“帕夫利克！帕夫利克！想想！想想！”瓦尔瓦拉低声而凶狠地说。
“来吧，各位，”她又拍起手，跌回那副不自然的尖利欢快里。“吃小鹅——好小鹅吃啊。来吃小鹅。”
“我不吃，”谢廖沙说，忽然抓住桌沿，朝主人主妇弯下身去，咧着一副放肆而发颤的笑——“不说‘可以娶塔尼娅’，我不吃。赵先生，帮帮我。哦，可是，见鬼，他不懂话！反正就这么着。…帕维尔·尼古拉耶维奇，我可以娶您女儿吗？”

===Original===

“God bless you, why not?” shouted Pavel. “I’ve told you the truth once and I’ll tell it to you again. She’s been betrothed seven times and all seven men are dead﻿—dead, aren’t they, Tanya? Or if not all, what do you care? She kills lovers for pleasure. She bites them when they kiss her. She slits their throats when they touch her. She cuts off their heads when﻿—”

“Pavlik! Pavlik!” said Varvara. “*Think!* You are talking nonsense.”

“Well, you can’t say I haven’t told you the truth, young man. My daughter’s a snake. If you want a snake for a wife﻿—God bless you, boy, you can follow the rest. I’ll dig your grave in my meadow tomorrow. Nevertheless, for the present, be merry﻿ ﻿…”

“I won’t eat till it’s all properly arranged and signed,” cried Seryozha, bravely, though his heart sank. This was the first transaction he had ever made all by himself, and he felt dimly that it was not being made in the grown-up, businesslike way he would have wished to boast of to his mother. “Mr. Chew, listen. Ah, *tschah*! Mr. Chew is asleep!”

For Wilfred, overcome by the wine, sat forward, his body telescoped upon itself, his elbows wide, his hands clasped upon his plate, his chin on his hands. His eyes had been shut, but now, as he heard his name, he opened them and rolled them roguishly at Seryozha. He opened his mouth, too, and waved his tongue, but no words came forth. He tapped his notebook with an idiotic significance.

There was a pause. Varvara looked nervously at her speechless guest. Pavel did not seem to notice Mr. Chew’s unusual demeanor, but he drew the notebook from beneath his guest’s plate and focused his frowning eyes on it at arm’s-length, inviting Seryozha to read over his shoulder. With care, Pavel, who was accustomed to reading English newspapers, could read what Wilfred had written. It was this:

===Chinese===

“上帝保佑你，为什么不能？”帕维尔喊。“实情我同你说过一遍了，再同你说一遍。她订过七回婚，七个男人全死了——全死了，是不是，塔尼娅？就算不齐全，你在乎吗？她杀情人取乐。他们吻她，她咬他们。他们碰她，她割他们喉。他们一怎么样，她就砍他们的头——”
“帕夫利克！帕夫利克！”瓦尔瓦拉说。“想想！你在说明话了。”
“好了，总不能说我没把实话告诉你，年轻人。我女儿是条蛇。你想要条蛇做妻子——上帝保佑你，孩子，跟上去吧。明天我就到草场里给你掘坟。不过眼下嘛，且乐……”
“不安排妥当、不签字，我不吃，”谢廖沙勇敢地喊，虽然心往下一沉。这是他平生头一回独自经手的一桩交易；他隐隐觉得，这交易办得完全不像他本想夸给母亲听的那种大人派、生意派。“赵先生，听着。啊，嚓！赵先生睡着了！”
威尔弗雷德果然叫酒征服了，朝前坐着，身子缩成一节，胳膊肘大大张开，两手扣在盘子上，下巴抵着手。他的眼睛本来闭着，听见自己的名字，睁开，朝谢廖沙顽皮地转了转。他也张开嘴，朝舌头挥了挥——没有词出来。他敲了敲笔记本，带着一种白痴的深意。
一阵停顿。瓦尔瓦拉紧张地看那位语塞的客人。帕维尔似乎没留意赵先生的异状，却把笔记本从客人盘子底下抽出来，皱着眉、伸直胳膊对准它，邀谢廖沙凑在他肩后同看。帕维尔看惯了英文报纸，细心的话，能认出威尔弗雷德写下的东西。写的是这个：

===Original===

**This indenture** made the twenty-seventh day of September One Thousand nine hundred and twenty-eight **between Sergei Sergeievitch Malinin** of Chi-tao-kou in the Province of Kirin Manchuria timber worker (hereinafter called the husband which expression shall unless the context otherwise determine herein include his heirs executors administrators and assigns) of the first part **Tatiana Pavlovna Ostapenko** of Mi-san Korea Spinster (hereinafter called the wife which expression shall unless the context otherwise determine herein include her heirs executors administrators and assigns) of the second part **Wilfred Chew** (**Ch’u Wei-Fu**) of the Middle Temple London England Barrister-at-Law (hereinafter called the Agent which expression shall unless the context otherwise determine include his heirs executors administrators and assigns) of the third part and **Pavel Nicholaievitch Ostapenko** of Mi-san in Korea Equitable Merchant (hereinafter described as the settlor which expression shall unless the context otherwise determine herein include his heirs executors administrators and assigns) of the fourth part **whereas** a marriage is intended shortly to be consummated between the husband and the wife **and whereas** the said marriage has been arranged so to be consummated as aforesaid at the suggestion and with the assistance of the agent **now** in consideration of the services rendered and to be rendered (at the desire express or implied of the parties hereto other than the agent) in the premises by the agent **this indenture witnesseth** that the settlor agrees to pay to the agent on the consummation of such marriage as aforesaid the following sums (that is to say) ¥100 (one hundred Yen) in consideration of his services in arranging the said marriage and ¥50 (fifty Yen) in consideration of professional services in the negotiation preparation and execution of these presents **and** in consideration of the natural love and affection of the husband for the wife and of divers other good and valuable considerations him hereunto moving and enabling **this indenture further witnesseth and it is hereby agreed and declared as follows**

-

===Chinese===

**兹立契约**一份，立于一千九百二十八年九月二十七日，立约各方为：满洲吉林省七道沟伐木工人**谢尔盖·谢尔盖耶维奇·马利宁**（下称“丈夫”，此称谓除文义另有所指外，包括其继承人、遗嘱执行人、遗产管理人及受让人）为第一造；朝鲜米桑**塔季扬娜·帕夫洛夫娜·奥斯塔片科**，未婚（下称“妻子”，此称谓除文义另有所指外，包括其继承人、遗嘱执行人、遗产管理人及受让人）为第二造；英格兰伦敦中殿律师学院出庭律师**威尔弗雷德·赵**（**赵伟富**）（下称“代理人”，此称谓除文义另有所指外，包括其继承人、遗嘱执行人、遗产管理人及受让人）为第三造；朝鲜米桑公平商人**帕维尔·尼古拉耶维奇·奥斯塔片科**（下称“财产授与人”，此称谓除文义另有所指外，包括其继承人、遗嘱执行人、遗产管理人及受让人）为第四造。**鉴于**丈夫与妻子之间婚事拟于近期缔结；**又鉴于**上述婚姻系经代理人建议并借助其助力而如前述安排；**兹**为酬谢代理人应本契约他方当事人（明示或默示）之意愿，就上述事项已经提供及将行提供之服务，**本契约见证如下**：财产授与人同意，于上述婚姻缔结之时，向代理人支付下列款项（即）：酬其安排上述婚姻之服务，日元一百元（¥100）；酬其就本契约之磋商、拟备及执行所提供之专业服务，日元五十元（¥50）；**又**为丈夫对妻子之天然情爱、及促动并授权于他的其他种种良善而有价之对价，**本契约进一步见证并约定声明如下**：

- 

上述丈夫同意接受上述妻子为其妻子，并按上帝所召其身处之生活境况扶养之，拥有且/或持有，爱且/或珍重，至死方休（**注**：关于妻子之正式条款待补）

===Original===

The said husband agrees to accept the said wife as his wife and to support her as such in that state of life to which it has pleased God to call her to have and/or to hold to love and/or to cherish until death do her part (**note** fill in formal parts as respects wife)

-

The husband further agrees in consideration as aforesaid to settle on the said wife by will or otherwise within a reasonable period of time the sum of ¥250 (two hundred and fifty Yen) at present at his disposal and/or under his control in the Bank of Chosen Seoul.

-

And in consideration of the love and affection of the said Settlor for the said wife and in further consideration of the natural gratitude of the said settlor to the said husband for offering the said marriage in spite of the 7 (seven) previous unfortunate circumstances the said settlor agrees on the consummation as aforesaid to pay as dowry the sum of ¥2,000 (two thousand Yen) to the said husband

Nothing in this Deed shall disentitle the said agent from receiving any sum of money thereunder or from charging his reasonable professional fees in connection therewith **in witness** whereof the parties hereto have hereunto set their respective hands and seals the day and year first above written (**Note** Query add clause re agent performing Wesleyan marriage ceremony query ¥25 fee)

Wilfred Chew, Barrister-at-law.

“But I don’t understand,” said Pavel, suddenly realizing that he was drunk. “Have we talked about this before?”

“Not we, but he,” said Seryozha, sheepishly. “He talked of it several times﻿—being a friend of both of us. I took little notice of him, as at that time I didn’t consider myself a marrying man. He’s a good chap, Chew,” added Seryozha, sentimentally. “Of course being a Chinese and a lawyer, he must drive a bargain. But he is a good honest chap. Twice he lent me his blanket, when I was cold after swimming. I shall sign this. I have sworn not to eat until I have signed something, and I am very hungry.” He wrote his name on the paper, his tongue-tip writing in the air above the pen.

“We will not sign it till tomorrow,” said Varvara. She could not read the agreement, but she was dubiously watching her husband’s glazed red face.

===Chinese===

- 

丈夫另同意，基于上述对价，于合理期限内，以遗嘱或他法，将现存于汉城朝鲜银行其支配之下且/或其控制之下之日元二百五十元（¥250）授予上述妻子。

- 

而为酬财产授与人对上述妻子之情爱，并为酬财产授与人感激上述丈夫不顾前此七（7）起不幸情事而提出上述婚姻之天然谢忱，财产授与人同意，于前述缔结之时，作为嫁资支付上述丈夫日元二千元（¥2,000）。

本契约任何条款，均不得剥夺上述代理人收取其项下任何款项、或就其收取合理专业费用之权利。**特此为证**，立约各方已于前述年月日于此签署姓名并盖印（**注**：可否增补“代理人主持卫斯理式婚礼”条款？费用¥25可否？）

威尔弗雷德·赵，出庭律师。

“可我不明白，”帕维尔说，忽然发现自己醉了。“这个咱们从前谈过吗？”

“不是咱们，是他，”谢廖沙讪讪地说。“他讲过好几回——说他是咱们两边的朋友。我没大理他，因为那会儿我没把自己当要结婚的人。他是个好人，这位赵，”谢廖沙动了感情，添上一句。“当然，又是中国人又是律师，总要讲讲价。可他是个正派老实的好人。有两回我游完泳发冷，他把毯子借给我。我签。我发过誓，不签点什么就不吃，我又饿得很。”他签上名，笔尖上头的空中，舌尖也跟着写。

“明天再签吧，”瓦尔瓦拉说。契约她读不了，可她正狐疑地盯着丈夫那张上了釉的红脸。

“塔尼娅同我这会儿就签，”帕维尔说，脸上那些游移的线条忽然一齐绷成固执。“能有位英雄做女婿的日子，可不是天天有的。”

他泼墨似的在谢廖沙那一大团污渍名字底下签上自己的名。“塔尼娅，”他添一句，把笔放进女儿手里。

===Original===

“Tanya and I will sign it now,” said Pavel, the uncertain lines of his face stiffening all at once to obstinacy. “It’s not every day one gets the chance to have a hero for a son-in-law.”

He splashed his signature under Seryozha’s large blotched name. “Tanya,” he added, putting the pen into his daughter’s hand.

For a moment Tanya wrote with it on the air, as though in a dream. Her father and mother watched her. Seryozha paused childishly, with his mouth full, to watch her. Her lips were set in a little smile. She leaned back in her chair and looked down her cheeks at the paper. Then she wrote her name in tiny letters﻿—Tatiana Pavlovna Ostapenko, and under her name she suddenly drew a little alert picture of a sparrow taking flight.

Her father looked guiltily at the signatures and the drawing. He folded up the paper and put it in his pocket. “Ah, *tschah*!” he said, as he seized a new bottle of champagne. “It means nothing. We still have tomorrow.﻿ ﻿… Varitchka, let’s eat your goose.”

===Chinese===

有一霎，塔尼娅拿它在空中写着，像在梦里。父亲母亲望着她。谢廖沙满嘴食物，孩子气地停下来望她。她唇边嚷着一小朵笑。她靠回椅背，顺着腮帮俯视那张纸。随后她写下自己的名字——小小的字母——塔季扬娜·帕夫洛夫娜·奥斯塔片科；又在名字底下，忽然添了一笔灵动的小画：一只振翅欲飞的麻雀。

她父亲心怀鬼胎地看看签名同那幅画。他把纸折起，放进口袋。“啊，嚓！”他抓起一瓶新香槟说。“这不算数。咱们还有明天呢。……瓦里奇卡，来吃你的鹅。”

## IX / 九
===Original===

From the moment of Seryozha’s departure from Chi-tao-kou, Anna’s world seemed filled with an entirely new air. One would have said that Seryozha must have filled the house as completely as a snail fills its shell﻿—so convoluted, so entire was the emptiness he left, from floor to ceiling, from wall to wall. Out of doors, looking through Anna’s eyes, one would have guessed her son a bright obscuring light﻿—so bleakly new and hard and shadowless was every leaf, every hill, every silly angle of the street, lacking even the possibility of his presence. It was a world lit, as it were, by indirect lighting under low clouds, instead of by the honest bland sun.

Anna’s son, ever since his birth, had always been within a few miles of her, and now those few miles, empty of him﻿—a cube of featureless summer air﻿—boxed her in, a prisoner. She could not spontaneously imagine his return. With a mental effort she could invent elaborate scenes of his homecoming, but she knew them to be artificial, knew them to be constructed with ingenuity rather than with faith and hope. Such scenes were always shattered by her conviction of premonition. “Absurd!﻿ ﻿… He will never come back.﻿ ﻿…” Every inch of the earth is, after all, so dangerous. Here, where we stand, a minute or a million years ago, some heart failed. There, at that point to which our dear love is hurrying, the lightning struck, the germ of plague was born, the tree will fall, the flood will surge, the murderer will stab﻿—a minute or a million years hence﻿—a minute or a million years ago. Living is a matter of missing death by a hair’s-breadth or an aeon﻿—it doesn’t matter which﻿—and dying is a matter of coincidence. If we knew the past and the future of every yard of every path we tread, or of every stone our dear love’s foot turns over as he goes, where should we turn for peace? Once we have realized the billions of deaths and horrors that have been, the billions that will be, every inch of the world seems soaked in blood. Every inch of the world, it seemed to Anna, was haunted by the ghost of a son whose mother had let him go. “Even at this very second, perhaps, his foot is lifted for his last stride.”

===Chinese===

从谢廖沙动身离开七道沟的那一刻起，安娜的世界仿佛灌满了一种全然新鲜的空气。简直可以说，谢廖沙从前准是把这座房子填得满满的，像蜗牛填满它的壳——他留下的这片空才这样曲折、这样完整，从地板到天花板，从这面墙到那面墙。走到屋外，透过安娜的眼睛看，人家准会猜她儿子原是一道亮亮的、遮蔽一切的光——如今每一片叶子、每一座小山、街上每一个傻气的转角，都那样荒凉地新鲜、坚硬、没有影子，连他在场的可能也不剩一点了。那是一个仿佛由低压云底下的间接照明照亮的世界，而不是由那坦荡温厚的太阳照亮的世界。

安娜的儿子，自落地起，从来离她不过几里地；如今这几里，空了他——一大块没有眉目的夏日空气——把她装在里头，成了囚徒。她没法自然而然地想象他回来。费一把心力，她能编出他归家的详尽场面，可她知道那是造出来的，知道那是拿心机搭的，不是拿信念和盼望。这样的场面总被她那预感的确信击碎。“荒唐！……他是不会回来的了。……”毕竟，大地上每一寸都这样危险。在这儿，我们站脚的地方，一分钟或一百万年前，某颗心衰竭过。在那儿，我们心爱的人正赶去的那一点上，雷劈下来过，瘟疫的芽胞生出来过，树会倒，洪水会涌，凶手会捅——一分钟或一百万年之后——一分钟或一百万年之前。活着，不过是以一发之差或一劫之差躲开死——哪一样都无所谓——死，则是巧合的事。倘若我们知道我们脚下每条路每一码的过去与未来，知道心爱的人一路踢转的每一块石头，我们还上哪儿去讨安宁？一旦明白了曾有过的亿万次死与灾殃、将有的亿万次，世界每一寸仿佛都浸透了血。在安娜看来，世界的每一寸，都闹着一个鬼——一个被母亲放走了的儿子的鬼。“说不定就在这一秒，他的脚已经抬起来，要迈最后一步了。”

===Original===

“Why should you be so pessimistic, Annitchka?” asked Old Sergei, who had rather relaxed his attitudes during this period of acute domestic discomfort, and lived now in an emotional deshabille, content to be seen as Old Sergei Malinin at last. “Hundreds of young men have made the very journey that he is making and have come back safely. Indeed, our Seryozha is doubly safe, since he has a reliable man with him.” He spoke to reassure himself as well as his wife, since her chronic conviction of a fatal presentiment affected him, too. He kept secretly in his pocket a knotted string, making a new knot for every day that brought no news of his son, in order that he might not have to irritate his wife by asking her to verify his calculations by the calendar. Apart from this tiny effort of ingenuity, Old Sergei had become, in a few days, very much more helpless than ever before. The necessity for posing as a father being now past, he had shrunk and withered into immobility, as flowers, dry and forgotten in the empty vase, hardly respond, except by an unlovely rattle, to the moving air that used to swing their bright heads. Old Sergei expressed by his wistful avoidance of authority his craving to be at last old, to be nursed, to be pitied, to have nothing more expected of him.

“Ah, why did you send him away?” said Anna, turning on the old man. “He was our life﻿—our crutch; we’re nothing but a couple of old bags of bones without him. You know what a dangerous, lawless country this is for a Russian, yet you were so greedy to add money to money.﻿ ﻿… Why, money’s dirt compared with the safety of our Seryozha. The money we had was enough; we lived very well; we were happy enough.”

“Don’t worry yourself so, Annitchka. It’s folly to worry so﻿—besides, it worries me. The boy’s not gone far; he’ll be back again in no time; we shall see him one of these days coming in at that door as usual﻿—or rather, you’ll see him, since I am so afflicted.”

“I shall never see him again,” said Anna, looking at the door, trying to force her imagination to reconstruct the prow of his long shadow, coming in at the door, like a ship into harbor.

“Oh, very well, then, worry﻿—worry. You take pleasure in worrying both yourself and me. Cry yourself sick if you like.”

===Chinese===

“你干吗这样悲观，安尼奇卡？”老谢尔盖问。这段剧烈的家常忧患里，他倒把架子松了下来，如今住在一身情感的便服里，甘愿让人看见他终于只是老谢尔盖·马利宁本相。“成百的年轻人走过他此刻走的这一趟，都平平安安回来了。何况咱们谢廖沙是双份的保险，身边带了个可靠的人。”他这话一半也是宽自己的心，因为妻子那慢性的凶兆确信也感染了他。他背地里在口袋里揣着一根打结的细绳，儿子没有音信的每一天就打上一个新结，省得去烦妻子、请她对着日历替他核账。除了这一小点用心，几天之间，老谢尔盖变得比从前任何时候都不中用得多。如今摆父亲的架子已无必要，他缩了，枯了，枯成一动不动，像空瓶里干透又被忘掉的花，除了瑟瑟一声不甚悦耳的轻响，几乎不再应答那曾经摇得它们明亮的花头摆动的活风。老谢尔盖用他对权威的哀哀躲闪，表明他一心只想终于老下去——受人服侍，受人怜惜，再没有人对他抱指望。

“唉，你干吗把他打发走？”安娜转过身冲老头子说。“他是咱们的命——咱们的拐棍；没了他，咱们不过是两把老骨头。你知道这对俄国人是个多危险、多没王法的国，可你偏那样贪，钱上还要摞钱。……唉，比起咱们谢廖沙的平安，钱是泥。原先那些钱尽够了；日子过得很好；咱们也算够幸福的了。”

“别这样愁，安尼奇卡。这样愁是糊涂——再说，愁得我也不安。孩子没走远；一转眼就回来了；早晚有一天，咱们会看见他照旧从那扇门进来——或者说，你会看见他，我这受罪的人是看不成了。”

“我再也见不着他了，”安娜说，眼睛望着那扇门，拼命逼自己的想象搭出他长长的影子探进门的模样，像船进港。

“哦，很好，那就愁吧——愁吧。你拿愁你自己、愁我当乐子。要哭就哭个病出来吧。”

===Original===

But Anna was not crying. She never cried. Her eyebrows were hitched up, her forehead strained into wrinkles, there was a little taut pain in the top of her head; these things, with her, took the place of tears. Sometimes she could almost have prayed to her muscles. Let go! Let go! Let go! Her eyes, her brow, and the little sore tiptoe yawning feeling inside the top of her skull seemed to be caught, hooked, seemed to have forgotten how to relax.

Poor Old Sergei was certainly a most uncomfortable old man at that time. His wife could hardly bear him, and yet she was not so cruel to him as she would have liked to be. Kindness was, as it were, at each extremity of her behavior to him; the core of her feeling was kindness and she tossed an exasperated kindness from her finger tips. This surface kindness made her buy him things he liked to eat and serve them with muffled curses, which, being misheard by him, she would change to words of half-ironic gentleness. But between the core and the surface of her mood there was a dark, tortuous area of weariness and hatred of his plaintiveness, his meanness, the contradiction of life that he was. In this intervening confusion of her nature she suffered a sort of contrariness, a doubling back, that made her challenge herself unconsciously to be cruel﻿—to try him a little more﻿—a little more﻿—a little more (will he stand it?)﻿—a little more still (almost like a murderess daring herself to press a trigger)﻿—till he would suddenly feel the prick of her insult, and lose his temper and his dignity. Then she would feel acutely guilty, talk to him gently with elaborate harmlessness, answer his meandering talk for a little while, until the obscurely revengeful impulse came back to hurt him again﻿—a little﻿—a little more﻿—a little more still.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

可安娜没有哭。她从不哭。她的眉毛吊着，额头绷出皱纹，头顶里有一点绷紧的疼；这些，在她身上，就顶了眼泪。有时她几乎要向自己的肌肉祷告。松开！松开！松开！她的眼睛、她的额头、颅顶里那小而酸痛的、踮脚打呵欠似的感觉，仿佛被钩住了，挂住了，仿佛忘了怎样放松。

可怜的老谢尔盖那时候实在是个最难受的老头儿。妻子几乎容不下他，可她对他又不像她想的那样狠。可以说，善意悬在她待他每一个举动的两端；她感情的核心是善意，从指尖上抛出去的却是一种带恼的善意。这表层的善意使她买他爱吃的东西端给他，端的时候压着嗓子咒骂；他听岔了，她便把咒骂改作半带讥讽的温存话。可在她心境的核心与表层之间，横着一片黑暗曲折的地带，是对他的哀哀诉苦、他的小气、他这个人生的自相矛盾的厌倦与憎恶。在她天性的这片夹缝的混乱里，她受着一种别扭，一种倒卷，使她不自觉地挑战自己去残忍——再试他一下——再一下——再一下（他受得住么？）——再再一下（几乎像一个女凶手给自己壮胆去扣扳机）——直到他忽然觉出她那句侮辱的刺，发起脾气，失了体面。这时她便尖锐地负疚，装出万无一失的和气跟他细声说话，应答他那些漫无头绪的絮叨一阵子，直到那阴暗的报复冲动又回来伤他——一点——再多一点——再多一点。……

===Original===

She would wrench his rheumatic fingers with a half-deliberate pinch as she guided his hand to his food, and then, when he cried out, impulsively and genuinely beg his pardon, pretending, even to herself, that it had been a clumsy accident. She would sit and look at him, grinding her teeth because he was not his son, and all the time make wounding or humiliating retorts to his plaintive prattle. He was not very acute and did not often perceive that he was being worse treated than usual; he only thought that Anna seemed clumsier and stupider than usual, more misunderstanding in her talk and more abrupt in her movements. And, seeing his obtuseness, that strange contrary cruelty in the soft Anna would gloat over its opportunity﻿—the tormenting of a creature too silly to recognize the instrument of torment﻿—“how safe﻿ ﻿… no one will ever know of this﻿ ﻿… no one but me﻿ ﻿…” Then, in the night, she would suddenly wake up, frozen with self-disgust, beat her head with her palm, and throw herself upon her husband, crying: “My darling, I’m sorry, I’m sorry! Forgive me. What a beast I am!” To Old Sergei these violent night-scenes of remorse were much more disturbing than the subtle discomfort of his days.

===Chinese===

她引他的手去够吃食时，会用半故意的拧劲去拧他那风湿的手指；他一喊痛，她又冲动地、真心地求他饶恕，连对自己也装作那不过是笨手笨脚的失手。她会坐着端详他，因为坐在那儿的不是他儿子而咬牙切齿，嘴上却一直拿伤人的、羞人的话回他那哀哀的絮叨。他不算敏锐，不常觉出自己受了比平常更坏的待遇；他只以为安娜比平常笨了、蠢了，说话更不接榫，动作更毛躁。而软心肠的安娜瞧着他这份迟钝，那股奇怪的、别扭的残忍便暗自得意于这个机会——折磨一个蠢得认不出刑具的活物——“多稳妥……谁也不会知道……除了我，谁也不会……”随后，夜里她会猛地醒来，恶心得浑身冰凉，用手掌打自己的头，扑到丈夫身上哭喊：“亲爱的，对不起，对不起！饶了我吧。我是头畜生！”对老谢尔盖来说，这些夜里狂风暴雨似的悔恨场面，比白天那种细磨细碾的不自在，搅扰得厉害得多。

===Original===

In order partly to be away as much as possible from her lonely house, and partly to save her old husband from herself, Anna pretended that she had a lot of work to do for Mrs. Butters during the first week of Seryozha’s absence. Really the preparations for the Butters baby were finished now, and Anna was only needed at the mission once a week to help with the darning and mending. Yet every morning, after breakfast, she would murmur a vague word or two about Mrs. Butters’s sewing and disappear, leaving bread, cheese, and beer ready on the table for her husband’s noon meal. Old Sergei would sit drooping alone all day in the street doorway. He had charge of the sale of a few packages of cheap Russian and Japanese cigarettes, matches, sweets, biscuits, bottles of lemonade and clay pipes that were arranged on a couple of trestles outside the living-room door﻿—the last rigor mortis of his dead shop. But only two or three customers a day spent a few sen on his goods and all day he would sit, half in and half out of his door, listening to the shouts of Korean and Chinese children playing, listening to the thin whine of the Japanese photographer next door singing over his work, listening to the *clop-ker-clop* of the senseless facetious gamboling of the mission kid in the yard behind the house, listening to the unfailing accompaniment of wails, cries, and squeals of thwarted and hurt animals that is always in the background of the hearing in every Chinese town. Sometimes the lonely old man would spend hours trying to lure within reach a dog that he could hear panting and snuffling and snapping at flies and ticks across the street. With a bait of crusts or show of imaginary food, he would patiently fish for the animal, only for the pleasure of touching its rough neck and shoulders when at last it trusted him enough to approach him﻿—touching its mangy ribs, its furtive tail, feeling the drip of sweat from its hanging tongue, assuring himself morbidly of the presence of another prisoner like himself, another life within another lean, sad, and elusive body.

===Chinese===

一半为了尽量躲开她那寂寞的家，一半为了救老丈夫躲开她自己，谢廖沙走后的头一个星期，安娜装出有好多活计要替巴特斯太太做。其实巴特斯家婴儿的预备如今都齐了，传教团那边一星期只要安娜去一回，帮着缝缝补补。可每天早晨，早饭后，她咕哝一两句含糊的巴特斯太太的针线，人就不见了，桌上给丈夫留好面包、干酪和啤酒，当他的午饭。老谢尔盖便整日独自蔫蔫地坐在临街的门洞里。他管着卖几包便宜的俄国香烟、日本香烟、火柴、糖果、饼干、几瓶柠檬水和黏土烟斗，摆在起居室门外两张条凳搭的架子上——他那死铺子最后的尸僵。可一天只有两三个主顾，在他货上花几个钱；他整天半进半出坐在门口，听朝鲜孩子中国孩子玩耍的喊叫，听隔壁日本照相师一边做活一边唱歌的细声呜咽，听屋后院里传教团的娃娃没心没肺、嬉皮笑脸蹦跳的啪嗒——咔啦——啪嗒，听那从不缺席的背景伴奏——在中国每一座城子里永远垫在人耳朵后头的——受挫的、受伤的牲口的哀号、哭叫、尖叫。有时这寂寞的老人会花上几个钟头，去诱一只能听得见的狗——在街对面喘气、嗅鼻子、扑咬苍蝇和壁虱的狗——诱到够得着的地方。拿面包皮作饵，或者装出有吃食的样子，他耐心地钓那活物，只为了等它终于信得过他、肯走近他时，摸一摸它粗糙的脖颈和肩膀——摸摸它生癞的肋骨，它贼溜溜的尾巴，觉出它垂着的舌头滴下的汗，病态地向自己证明：还有一个像他一样的囚徒，还有一条性命，包在另一副瘦削、悲哀、抓不住的身子里。

===Original===

The first day of Seryozha’s absence, Anna’s only impulse in leaving her home was to walk a little way along the path that he had trodden. Perhaps she might see the print of his big shoes or find something that he had dropped﻿—the stub of a cigarette or the paper that had wrapped *pirozhki*. Perhaps she might learn something about him from a Korean peasant or Chinese peddler who had seen him passing by. At any rate, she could see things that he had seen﻿—notice the patched crops upon the hills, the sharp rocks that slit with a short gash of foam the smooth-running surface of the river, the thin shade under which he had perhaps rested, the bloomy dazzle of reeds in the shallows, the fantastic duplication of crags﻿—reared in groups, as nearly alike as the chimneys of one house﻿—halfway round the horizon, the farm dogs that must have barked at him, that great scrawny sow, dragging her unbeautiful dugs through the dust, that must have made him laugh yesterday. Probably he remembered, when he saw that, that his mother had once said that the fat pink mission school on the hill, flanked at right angles by a neat row of little pink mission houses, looked like a sow suckling its young. She felt for a moment as if she were actually sharing a laugh with her Seryozha, and she stood staring hungrily at the sow until the poor beast looked almost embarrassed.

Then she noticed, sitting on a stone near the river, that young Russian who had lately walked up from Seoul﻿—Alexander Petrovitch Weber. This young man, a plain, sad, gawky creature enough, radiated beauty in Anna’s eyes, because she knew that he and Seryozha had met. Seryozha had even brought him to the house the day before yesterday when she had been busy over the washing. Seryozha had, she believed, liked him, and this boy had doubtless been delighted by Seryozha. It seemed as if some scrap of Seryozha’s darling personality had been grafted upon this young man.

===Chinese===

谢廖沙不在的头一天，安娜出门唯一的冲动，是沿他走过的那条小路走上一小段。说不定她能看见他那双大鞋的印子，或者拾着他掉下的什么——半截烟卷，或者包过油煎包的纸。说不定她还能从见过他走过的朝鲜庄稼人或中国货郎嘴里，打听出他一点什么。至少，她能看见他看见过的东西——看山坡上打补丁的庄稼，看那些尖利的石头用一道短促的白色裂口豁开平滑奔流的河面，看他也许歇过脚的薄薄荫凉，看浅水里芦苇开得毛茸茸的白亮，看奇异的群峰的重影——一丛丛拔起，像一座房子的几根烟囱那样彼此相像——围了半圈地平线，看那些准朝他吠过的庄户狗，看那头干瘦的大母猪，拖着它不体面的奶头从尘土里蹭过——昨天准逗他笑过的。他见到那猪时，多半想起母亲说过的话：山上那座粉红的、胖胖的传教团学校，直角旁边齐齐整整陪着一排粉红的小传教团房子，看上去活像一头奶着崽子的母猪。有一霎，她仿佛当真同她的谢廖沙共享了一个笑，她站在那儿，饿溜溜地盯着那头母猪，盯得那可怜的活物几乎显出难为情来。

随后她看见，河边的石头上坐着一个年轻的俄国人——新近从汉城步行上来的那位——亚历山大·彼得罗维奇·韦伯。这个年轻人，本来生得平常、愁苦、笨手笨脚，在安娜眼里却放着光，因为她知道他同谢廖沙见过面。前天她忙着洗衣裳，谢廖沙还把他带到家里来过。她相信谢廖沙喜欢过他，这孩子呢，无疑也领受过谢廖沙的欢喜。仿佛谢廖沙那可爱的性情的什么一小片，嫁接到了这个年轻人身上。

===Original===

She therefore walked towards him, feeling fat and humble and ungainly, as she had felt ever since she had lost sight of Seryozha. She must be tentative and a little self-conscious with everyone now, since there was no one in Chi-tao-kou to justify her existence﻿—no son before whom she could feel, “Well, I mayn’t be beautiful, but this splendid creature calls me Mother.” She approached Alexander Weber, conscious of her waddle and of her splay shoes, one of which was slit over one toe to accommodate a corn.

Alexander Weber was very tall and lanky, black-haired and sallow, with a big nose, abrupt cheekbones and generally prominent features﻿—among which an assertive Adam’s apple seemed to hold its own, almost as though it were a second attempt at a chin. He had a very gentle look in his dark eyes﻿—a look which he withdrew from the river and focused, as though with difficulty, on the approaching Anna. “My son﻿—Sergei Sergeievitch Malinin, you know﻿—started for Seoul yesterday.”

“I know,” said Alexander, rising politely, though indifferently, from his boulder. “I thought of going with him.”

“Going with him? Why, you have only just come from there, surely!” said Anna.

“Yes,” said the young man, dreamily.
