Until at last you had to admit, “Well﻿—yes﻿—if you assure me of that, and that, and that, and swear to it so solemnly, of course I must believe you that *this* is so,” and only then did his round eyes relax, blink, look safe again. You were convinced; that was all that mattered; he had obliged you to admit that he was right. Whether he was right did not matter﻿—that he had lied from the beginning did not matter﻿—you had said that he was right, you accepted his truth, though it was no truth.

===Chinese===

"还告诉他，"威尔弗雷德又添一句，"就是在英国，我也从没见过一只'嚎'——枭——形状的芥末罐——不过有一回我亲眼见过这鸟儿，那晚坐公共马车——马夫——马车去阿斯科特，它正扑腾着穿过一片林子。扑腾——穿——林子——坐——马车。"

"我娶瓦里娅，可以说，纵不算是出于怜悯，也实在是出于骑士风——可怜的人儿。我知道她不大可能再有什么人来求亲——一个穷挤奶工的女儿，又那么瘦，还有那块胎记。可是主啊，她从前——如今也还是——一心一意向着我。她知道她的福气。"

帕维尔的脸，是一个从不曾受过挫折的人的脸。他的五官定在一种快活的笃定里；他那张嘴对付起自我怀疑来，全然是一副勇士相；他的舌头不知道什么叫打绊。只有他的眼睛，焦灼而滚圆地瞪着，像是怕挨打——仿佛他非得把它们睁得大大的，好收集证据，证明他说的一切都叫人信了、叫人赏识了。他那双眼睛挑你来挑刺，说你撒谎、说你错了，你但凡露一点疑，他就要证据摺证据、假证摞假证、谎上摞谎、赌咒上摞赌咒，全然忘了那么一座摇摇欲坠的断言之塔，最稳当还是打实情的地基上盖。直到末了你只好认："唔——是——你既然这样那样再这样向我担保，又赌得这么郑重的咒，我当然只好信你这个是这样，"只有到那时，他那双圆眼睛才松弛下来，眨一眨，重新显得安全。你被说服了；这才是顶要紧的；他叫你不得不认他对。他究竟对不对，不要紧——他从一开头就在撒谎，也不要紧——你说了他对，你收下了他的真相，虽然那根本不是真相。

===Original===

“I must say,” went on Pavel, “except in one matter, I’ve never had to regret my impulsive and chivalrous decision. Varya’s been a wonderful wife to me (I can see it now looking back) except in one particular. The exception I leave you to imagine, my dear boy. You’re young, but you know life, and you know as well as I do there are certain things a decent man doesn’t give away when talking of his wife. Nature﻿—and a man’s nature especially, mind you﻿—shocks her, seems to her improper. She has tried to adapt herself, poor soul; she sees quite well that such freakishness and prudishness make a woman an unworthy mate for a manly man. But fundamentally, it’s still there.﻿ ﻿… It accounts for much of the oddity in our Tanya’s nature. Tanya, our only child, has inherited her mother’s nature, though her coldness has a different aspect.﻿ ﻿… Tanya doesn’t think anything improper﻿—indeed, her mother and I have often had to check the freedom with which she describes her observations of animals. She doesn’t examine realities enough to be shocked by them﻿—she just feels far away from physical realities. She is, you see, of an unhuman nature. (I’m not concealing anything from you, my boy, I don’t think it would be fair.)”

He fixed his round eyes on Seryozha all the time, as if, in spite of the complacent set of his mouth and carriage of his head, he was expecting Seryozha to say: “O Lord! Pavel Nicholaievitch, you are a liar!” But of course Seryozha said nothing of the sort. He sat in a dream, half listening to his host, half remembering the lost face of Tatiana. He rubbed the bridge of his broad shiny nose wisely. Of course he knew that parents never know anything about their children.

“Saggay Saggayitch,” said Wilfred, “you say nothing. As a friend you will ’llow me to suggest, would it not be better to show a little more vivivacity with this kind gentleman, Mr. Ozz. You don’t talk enough, my dear chap, you don’t make the bezz of yourself. Your future may hang on making bezz of self.”

===Chinese===

"我得说，"帕维尔接着讲，"除了在一件事上，我从没懊悔过我那冲动而讲骑士风的决断。瓦里娅一直是我的好妻子（如今回头看，我看得分明）——只除了一样。那一样例外，留给你自己去想，我亲爱的孩子。你年轻，可你懂生活，你跟我一样明白，一个体面人谈起身子上的妻子来，有些事是不能外传的。天性——尤其是男人的天性，你记着——吓着她了，在她看来不像话。她也试过迁就，可怜的人儿；她看得很清楚，这种怪脾气、这股子假正经，叫一个女人配不上一个男子汉。可根子上，那东西还在。…这就说明了咱们塔尼娅天性里好些古怪。塔尼娅，我们的独养女儿，承了她母亲的天性，虽说她的冷淡另是一副面目。…塔尼娅不觉得什么不像话——真的，她母亲同我还常常不得不拦着她，拦她描述她那些对动物的观察时那份随便。她不把实相看得够重，所以不会叫实相吓着——她只是觉得离肉体的一切远远的。她是，你明白，一副非人的天性。（我什么都不瞒你，我的孩子，我觉得瞒了不公道。）"

他一直把圆眼睛定在谢廖沙身上，仿佛尽管他嘴巴上得意、头颅摆得神气，他正等着谢廖沙说："主啊！帕维尔·尼古拉耶维奇，你是个骗子！"可谢廖沙当然没说这种话。他坐在梦里，一半听主人说，一半回想塔季扬娜那张失去了的脸。他聪明地搓着自己那根宽宽发亮的鼻梁。他当然知道，做父母的对子女从来什么都不知道。

"萨盖·萨盖伊奇，"威尔弗雷德说，"你什么也不说。作为朋友，你许我提一句：对这位和气的先生、奥兹先生，多显一点活活泼——活泼劲儿，岂不更好？你说话不够，亲爱的伙计，你没把你自己最大的好处——最最大的好处使出来。你的前程，也许就悬在把自个儿的最大好处使出来上头呢。"

===Original===

“When I say unhuman,” went on Pavel, “I don’t mean anything against the child’s temper. She has the sweetest temper, the tenderest heart.﻿ ﻿… She gets her sweetness of nature from my side, of course. She’s a dear girl and perhaps love will teach her a great deal. I used to call her a fairy when she was little, and now sometimes I’m tempted to call her a fairy still, but I’d say it in a different voice now. It’s been a great pain to me. I counted so on a family of robust sons and daughters to hand on my good old name to.﻿ ﻿… They’re a man’s immortality, his children and his children’s children.﻿ ﻿… However, my dear boy, it may very well be that the right husband for Tanya may get the right results with her. One never can tell. Even icebergs melt under some sun or other, and Tanya, as I say, isn’t an iceberg emotionally﻿ ﻿… she has *my* blood in her as well as her mother’s, and therefore she has her own brand of passionate feelings, like all her breed. She signed that paper with a very good grace﻿—allowing for the natural shrinking of a young girl﻿—and in a sense she is already your wife, with her own eager consent, mark you, which is further than any of the other lads ever got. That paper was drawn up by a professional lawyer, and declares you man and wife. You couldn’t ask for a better paper than that.” He was silent suddenly, pouring himself out some more wine.

Nobody spoke for several minutes, not even Wilfred. Wilfred’s head was nodding; his eyelids and chin were dropping. The clock pointed to half past twelve and Seryozha was proud to find that he was not sleepy.

===Chinese===

"我说非人，"帕维尔接着说，"并不是说孩子脾气坏。她的脾气再好没有，心肠再软没有。…她那份性子的甜，当然是从我这一边得去的。她是个可爱姑娘，兴许爱情会教会她许多。她小时候我常叫她小仙子，如今有时候我还想仍旧叫她小仙子，只是如今我喊出口的腔调不同了。这一直是我的大痛。我那么指望一屋子结结实实的儿女，好把我的好姓氏传下去。…儿女是一个人的不朽，儿女的儿女也是。…不过，我亲爱的孩子，也很可能，配得上塔尼娅的那个丈夫，就能从她身上得出配得上的结果。谁也说不准。冰山也总有叫这个太阳那个太阳晒化的，塔尼娅呢，我说过，在感情上不是冰山…她的血管里有我的血，也有她母亲的血，所以她有她自己那一路的热情，同她全族一样。她签那张纸签得非常大方——就算上一个年轻姑娘自然的畏缩吧——从某种意义上说，她已经是你妻子了，出于她本人的甘愿，你记着，这比先前那些小伙子哪一个到过的地步都远。那张纸是一位职业律师起草的，宣告你们是夫妻。再好的纸你也要不来了。"他忽然住了口，给自己又斟了些酒。

好几分钟谁也不说话，连威尔弗雷德也不。威尔弗雷德的头点着；他的眼皮和下巴往下坠。钟指着十二点半，谢廖沙得意地发现，自己不困。

===Original===

Pavel began speaking again in a voice of rather defiant surprise. “Just now I said ‘*In a sense* Tanya is your legal wife.’ But of course really there’s no *in a sense* about it. She is your wife. That document was drawn up by a professional lawyer; we have all signed it in the presence of Varya and Katya as witnesses. What more could we do to make a marriage legal? There is no priest of our Church available﻿—besides, you and I are too modern to feel the need of the Church’s interference in practical matters like this. In Paris﻿—London﻿—New York﻿—a legal paper, duly drawn up and signed, is enough for a legal marriage. Why should we expect more in Mi-san? My boy, you’re a married man. Congratulations!” Doubt came into his strained eyes again. “Really I don’t know what else one could want to make the thing correct and in order﻿—lacking a priest. It’s not as if there was any unwillingness on either side﻿—you both signed of your own free will, at your own suggestion. She gets a good dowry. You’ll find me a generous father-in-law. All we Ostapenkos are generous to a fault. Your father, Sergei Dmitrivitch, will be delighted at such a good match for you﻿—a dowry of two thousand yen is not to be sniffed at, and there’ll be more later﻿—and he gets a lovely daughter-in-law into the bargain.”

“The paper is legal all right,” said Seryozha solemnly, speaking to the shades of his father and mother, and pursing his face into a man-of-the-worldly look. “As legal as possible. Mr. Chew is a famous lawyer in London. He never would write an illegal paper.”

===Chinese===

帕维尔又开口了，声音里带着一点挑衅的惊讶。"方才我说'从某种意义上'塔尼娅是你合法的妻子。可其实当然，这里头没有什么'从某种意义上'。她就是你的妻子。那份文书是一位职业律师起草的；咱们当着瓦里娅和卡佳（Katya）作证，全都签了字。要叫一桩婚事合法，咱们还能再做什么？咱们教会的神父一个也请不着——再说，你跟我都太摩登，不会觉得这类实际的事务需要教会来插手。在巴黎——伦敦——纽约——一张正经起草、签了字的合法文书，就够一桩合法婚姻了。凭什么咱们在米山倒要指望更多？我的孩子，你是个成了婚的人了。恭喜！"疑虑又爬进他那双紧张的眼睛。"我真不知道，缺一位神父，还能要什么把这桩事办得妥当——齐整。又不是两头有半点不愿意——你们俩都是自愿签的，还是你自己提的。她得一份好嫁妆。你会发现我是个大方的岳父。咱们奥斯塔片科家的人，大方得过了头。你父亲，谢尔盖·德米特里耶维奇，见了这样一门好亲只会有多欢喜——两千日元的嫁妆不是小数，往后还有——外带他还白得一个可爱的儿媳妇。"

"那张纸完全合法，"谢廖沙郑重地说，是对着他父母亲的亡灵说的，把脸皱成一副深谙世故的神气。"合法得不能再合法。赵先生是伦敦有名的律师。他绝不会写一张不合法的纸。"

===Original===

Wilfred, opening his eyes for a moment, found the eyes of both Russians fixed upon him. He was one who always put the best construction on any ambiguity, and that two Russians should look at him implied some pleasing compliment just uttered in their outlandish tongue. He bowed pleasantly in answer to their look. But bowing, which he always did thoroughly and briskly, being always quite sure of his occasion, now started a ball rolling in his head. Something went on bowing by itself inside his brain; the room bowed round him﻿—or rather curtsied, in silly acknowledgment of this something inside his head that went on bowing. Was his outer skull really wagging? he asked himself in some consternation. Could this uncertain ball be the head of Wilfred Chew, Esquire? A slight bump as his forehead hit the table showed him that his head, though unusually independent, was still attached to his body, but, since the forehead was on the table, he might as well let it stay there. “Then they will think I am simply asleep,” he thought, cunningly. “Any gentleman may go to sleep without being thought morally affected.” It was quite outside his plan actually to go to sleep. His pretense of sleep took on an aspect of revenge, almost, in his own mind as he shut his eyes. “Well, if people *will* talk Russian for six hours without a break, they can’t be surprised if their superiors seem to go to sleep.” He listened upward, as it were, feeling that he was turning his ears like a dog﻿—listened to the sound of Pavel’s talk. It sounded like *shyok-shyeh-shyok-shyeh*, and in Wilfred’s ears it swelled and dwindled like the humming one hears when under chloroform, or like the wowing of an airplane engine in the sky.

While he was still marveling at the fact that this contagious bowing craze seemed to be affecting his hearing and making sound bow too, a sudden startling silence fell in the room. The light inside Wilfred’s eyelids changed from scarlet to deep purple. Someone must be taking the lamp away. Heavy feet trampled cautiously out of the room, the rhythm of their tread adjusting itself to the rhythm of Wilfred’s throbbing attention. *Tramp-ti﻿—tramp-ti﻿—tramp-ti*﻿—and then a check, and a woman’s voice “Ah *Pavlik*!”﻿—a voice of almost unbearable disappointment.

===Chinese===

威尔弗雷德睁眼一霎，发现两个俄国人的眼睛都盯着他。他是那种对任何含糊都往好里解释的人，两个俄国人望他，想必是刚才用他们那蛮语说了什么中听的恭维。他愉快地躬身作答。可他这一躬——他躬身向来彻底、干脆，永远笃定自己的场合——这一躬却在脑袋里滚起了一个球。有个什么东西在他脑壳里自顾自一躬接一躬；房间围着他躬——不如说行屈膝礼，傻乎乎地答谢他脑袋里那个一躬接一躬的东西。他外头这个脑壳真在晃么？他有点惊惶地问自己。这个拿不稳的球，难道真会是威尔弗雷德·赵老爷的头？额头磕在桌上轻轻一撞，告诉他头虽然异常独立，还连在身子上；可既然额头已经搁在桌上了，不妨就让它搁着。"那他们只当我睡着了，"他狡猾地想。"哪位绅士都可以睡着，不致被人看作德行有亏。"真睡着原不在他的计划之内。他闭上眼，这睡的假装在他自己心里几乎带上了报复的味道。"好啊，要是这些人非要用俄语连说六个钟头不停，他们的上等人看着睡着了，他们可别奇怪。"他竖起耳朵听，只觉得自己像狗一样转着耳朵——听帕维尔谈话的声响。那声响像咻克—谢—咻克—谢，在威尔弗雷德耳朵里涨了又缩，像人上氯仿时听见的那阵嗡嗡，又像天顶飞机引擎的呜呜。

他还在惊奇，这场传染的躬身热怎么好像连他的听觉也传染了，连声音也在打躬，忽然屋里落下一阵吓人的寂静。威尔弗雷德眼皮里那点光从猩红变成深紫。准是有人把灯端走了。沉重的脚步小心地踏出屋去，那踏拍的节奏，正调着威尔弗雷德突突跳的注意的节奏。咚嗒——咚嗒——咚嗒——随后一顿，一个女人声音："啊，帕夫利克！"——一副几乎受不住的失望的嗓音。

===Original===

Wilfred lifted his pendulum head only enough to be able to lean his chin instead of his brow upon his hands. His head might actually roll off if he should rear it unsupported into the swinging air again. Across a glimmering silver plain of lampless tablecloth, he saw a perfectly still golden scene framed in the doorway. Pavel Ostapenko, holding the lamp a little crooked but not dangerously so, stood facing his wife in the passage, his eyes stretched to an alert glare, like a squirrel’s eyes. Varvara, in a blue cotton kimono, stood defiantly across the passage, watching her husband with a defiant dark look. Her wine-colored birthmark looked like an eccentric shadow thrown by a more ominous flame than the lamp’s light. Seryozha, against the opposite wall, was drawing a little parcel from his pack. “The fish’s liver and heart,” thought Wilfred. “He is going to face the devil in that red and white Tanya woman. She sleeps behind that door at the top of the little matted slope out of the kitchen. I saw her go in when I was washing at the pump.” “*Pavlik!*” cried Varvara again with a low violence of voice, but her husband took one step towards her, took Seryozha’s﻿—arm and pointed over his wife’s shoulder﻿—“At the devil’s door,” thought Wilfred. Varvara put both her hands in her mouth, cramming all her fingers between her lips as though frantically trying to tear them to pieces. She looked like a witch, grinning fixedly through the fringe of her fingers. Pavel began talking again. *Wow﻿—wow﻿—wow﻿—*, heard Wilfred. Wilfred’s heavy eyelids dropped like a curtain on the bright scene, and when next he half lifted them there was no light except the light of the kitchen fire, blurring and quickening on the passage walls, like breath dimming a windowpane. There were distant voices in the kitchen. Wilfred’s perceptive senses became mixed in a drowsiness, and the voices became a cloud, a curious menacing cloud like a wing, each spasm of sound a feather in the wing﻿—the whole flaked cloud coming nearer and nearer on a wind that blew in regular throbbing gusts. Before this wind all the trees in the world bowed, and all the leaves on all the trees were blown so as to show their white linings﻿—white flecks showing silver in a moonlight that streamed from no visible moon. The moonlight had a breathless quality, in spite of the wind.

===Chinese===

威尔弗雷德把他那颗钟摆似的头只抬起一点点，刚好把下巴而不是额头搁在手上。若是没人扶着，再把头竖回那晃荡的空气里，它兴许真会滚落下来。隔着一片没了灯的桌布的微微发亮的银白平原，他望见门框里嵌着一幅纹丝不动的金色画面。帕维尔·奥斯塔片科端着灯，歪了一点，但不至于危险，在过道里同他妻子对面站着，眼睛张成一个警戒的瞪视，像松鼠的眼。瓦尔瓦拉穿着蓝布和服，横在过道上，用一股反抗的黑劲望着丈夫。她那块酒色的胎记，像一道比灯光更凶的火焰投下的古怪影子。谢廖沙在对面墙根，正从他的背包里往外掏一个小包。"鱼的肝和心，"威尔弗雷德想。"他要拿这个去会那个红白脸的塔尼娅女人背后的魔鬼了。她睡在那扇门后头，在厨房出去那小片铺草垫的坡道顶上。我在水泵边洗脸时看见她进去的。""帕夫利克！"瓦尔瓦拉又喊，声音低而暴，可她丈夫朝她迈了一步，拉住谢廖沙的胳膊，越过他妻子的肩头一指——"到魔鬼门口了，"威尔弗雷德想。瓦尔瓦拉把两只手塞进嘴里，把手指头全都塞到嘴唇中间，像发了狂要把它们撕碎。她像个女巫，从手指的流苏后面僵僵地龇着牙笑。帕维尔又说起话来。呜哇——呜哇——呜哇，威尔弗雷德听见的就这个。威尔弗雷德沉重的眼皮像幕布一样落在那幅明亮的画面上，等他再把它们半掀起来时，屋里已没有灯，只剩厨房炉火的光，在过道墙上模模糊糊地又亮又跳，像呵在窗玻璃上的气。厨房里有远远的人声。威尔弗雷德的知觉搅进一片睡意里，那些人声成了一朵云，一朵古怪的、带威胁的、翅膀似的云，每一阵声音的抽缩是翅膀上的一根羽毛——整片剥剥落落的云，乘着一阵一阵规律搏动的风，越逼越近。这风吹得世上所有的树都弯了腰，所有的树上所有的叶子都翻出叶背的白——白斑点在一片月光里闪着银，那月光却不见月亮。月光有一种叫人屏息的性质，尽管刮着风。

===Original===

Everything in the world was flawed and burnt with sparks of white; all the stars of the sky, fleeing from that stupendous winged dragon of a cloud, were lying dying in agony in the dust, like fish thrown up by a great departed wave. Trees, houses, deserts, and mountains were caked, clogged, with dying, gasping stars. The pursuing cloud strode across the sky; one could hear now the approaching boom﻿—boom﻿—boom of its wings. And Wilfred, sweating with excitement, addressed the jury, though his voice was a little thick. “I know how you bind dragons, gentlemen,” said Wilfred. “With one hand you take them behind the gills and with the other behind the wingpits﻿—(wingpits? Well, armpits. Why not wingpits?) Yes, with the other firmly under the wingpits and thus they are perfectly helpless.” He said this bravely and the jury was obviously impressed. The jury numbered thousands﻿—their little silver-white faces upturned, all over the twilit limitless hall. One must be brave before such a crowd of puny dying white faces﻿—yet, as the wide earthshaking booming of wings drew closer and closer, Wilfred wondered whether he could really master so frightful an enemy single-handed. Boom! *boom!* **boom!** **boom!**﻿—the monster was upon him; it had only a little blank disc for a face﻿—how much more terrible than gnashing teeth and flashing eyes! Wilfred suddenly shouted in such a great voice that the strong wind was checked and the galloping cloud thrown back on its haunches. *Asmodeus. Asmodeus. Asmodeus.* Now what? thought Wilfred, mad to show his own monstrous power. One hand pinching the gills and the other the soft bending ribs (a lizard’s ribs), a twirl﻿ ﻿… round and round the sequin-scaled body whipped the rope, flipped skillfully by Wilfred’s heroic hand. “I am bound﻿—I am bound﻿—I am bound,” piped the little fading disc that was the demon’s face. But, oh, the scales were cold. They burned like naked ice. The great icy body leaped, squirmed, and writhed like a hooked fish. Here, a limb struggled loose﻿—Wilfred lashed it with another loop; there, the tail began sweeping free﻿—it was bound before more than a paltry million stars had been annihilated; here, a claw reached out and was instantly netted. The vast faceless thing heaved, powerless, agonized.﻿ ﻿… Yet, what was that﻿—boom! *boom!* **boom!** **boom!** again? Was some outlying coil breaking free?

===Chinese===

世上样样东西都带上了瑕疵，烧出白色的火星；天上所有的星，逃着那朵巨大带翼的云龙，正在尘土里奄奄一息地挣命，像一场大浪退去后擞在滩上的鱼。树、房屋、沙漠和山，都叫那些垂死的、喘息的星糊满了、堵满了。追来的云跨着大步横过天空；如今听得见它翅膀逼近的隆——隆——隆。威尔弗雷德兴奋得冒汗，对陪审团陈起词来，虽然嗓音有点含糊。"我知道怎么绑龙，先生们，"威尔弗雷德说。"一只手抓它的鳃后头，另一只手抓翅膀根底下——（翅膀根？唔，胳肢窝。为什么不能叫翅膀根？）对，另一只手牢牢地抓在翅膀根底下，这样它就完全没奈何了。"他说得勇敢，陪审团显然受了感动。陪审团有几千人——他们小小的银白脸仰着，铺满这整个黄昏里无边的大厅。面对这么一大群奄奄一息的小白脸，人得勇敢——可是，那翅膀震天动地的隆隆声越逼越近，威尔弗雷德拿不准，他单枪匹马到底制不制得了这么可怕的敌人。隆！隆！隆！隆！——那怪物扑到他身上了；它只有一小片空白的圆盘当脸——比獠牙利目可怕多少倍！威尔弗雷德忽然大喊一声，嗓门那么大，狂风给遇住了，奔跑的云给掼得跪坐了回去。阿斯莫德。阿斯莫德。阿斯莫德。现在怎么着？威尔弗雷德想，疯了似的要显示他自己的神力。一只手掐住鳃，另一只手掐住那软软弯着的肋骨（蜥蜴的肋骨），一旋…亮片鳞的身子叫绳子抽得团团转，绳子由威尔弗雷德英雄的手腕巧妙地甩着。"我给捆住了——我给捆住了——我给捆住了，"那张越来越弱的小圆盘——恶魔的脸——尖声叫。可是，哦，那些鳞那么冷。它们烫得像赤裸的冰。那庞大的冰冷身子跳跃、扭动、翻滚，像上了钩的鱼。这边，一条腿挣脱了——威尔弗雷德又甩一圈套住它；那边，尾巴开始扫开——不等那区区一百万颗星再给毁灭多少，它已经捆上了；这边，一只爪子探出来，立刻落进网里。那庞大的没脸的东西挺动着，无能为力，痛得要命。……可是，那是什么——又是隆！隆！隆！隆？莫非哪一段外国的身子又挣开了？

===Original===

A scarlet air was blinding the world, a scarlet pain was riveted like a helmet on the hero’s brow. Walls, a table, lamplight, a smell of roast goose﻿—little dazzling homely things imprisoned him. Here was the room again, safe now from its terrific menace. Pavel Ostapenko was coming in with the lamp. He would want to hear the news of Wilfred’s victory, of course, yet it was difficult to talk through such a taste in the mouth﻿—such a band of pain round the skull.

“I bound Asmodeus! I bound him!” cried Wilfred. “Ah﻿—my head! my head!﻿ ﻿… Yet I bound him﻿—Asmodeus.﻿ ﻿…”

Ostapenko sat down at the table and buried his face in his hands.

“Where is Saggay Saggayitch?” asked Wilfred, thickly, feeling the need of an interpreter.

“Sergei Sergeievitch? Oi! where﻿—where?” groaned Pavel in English.

This unexpected proof of linguistic sympathy encouraged Wilfred to begin to tell Ostapenko about his dream in detail. He never could quite believe that people really didn’t understand English. Incomprehension was a sort of coyness on their part, which brisk treatment might help them to overcome.

But Ostapenko only replied by groans; only when Varvara came in did the talk begin again, and then, of course, in no more intelligent language than that outlandish Russian. *Shyeh-shyeh shyok-shyok wow-wow*﻿—as though by its pulsing it was measuring off the seconds of this endless night.

“Don’t Russians ever go to bed?” thought Wilfred. Clasping his aching head without disguise, he got up and stumbled over to the sofa. None of them cared that he had bound Asmodeus for their sake. By the way﻿—why Asmodeus? He lay down on the sofa and was instantly drowned in profound dark sleep.

“You needn’t look at me like that, Varya,” said Pavel, raising his tired face. “It seems to me you have lost your senses. Here we have a charming and eligible young man﻿—a kinsman of my own﻿—whom our daughter has chosen willingly as her husband﻿—”

“Pavlik﻿—*think*.”

===Chinese===

一片猩红的空气瞎了整个世界，一道猩红的疼痛像头盔钉在英雄的额头上。墙壁、桌子、灯光、一股烤鹅的香味——一样样耀眼的家常小东西关住了他。房间又回来了，从那场可怕的威胁底下安全回来了。帕维尔·奥斯塔片科端着灯进来。他当然想听威尔弗雷德得胜的消息，可是嘴里这么一股味道、脑壳上这么一道箍着痛，说话真难。

"我捆住了阿斯莫德！我把他捆住了！"威尔弗雷德喊。"啊——我的头！我的头！…可我把他捆住了——阿斯莫德。…"

奥斯塔片科在桌边坐下，把脸埋进双手。

"萨盖·萨盖伊奇在哪儿？"威尔弗雷德含糊地问，觉得需要一位翻译。

"谢尔盖·谢尔盖耶维奇？哎！哪儿——哪儿去了？"帕维尔用英语哼哼。

这意外的语言上的同情，鼓励威尔弗雷德开始向奥斯塔片科详细讲他的梦。他从来不能完全相信，人们竟然真听不懂英语。听不懂是他们的一种拗拗而已，来点爽利的对待兴许就能治好。

可奥斯塔片科只报以哼哼；直到瓦尔瓦拉进来，谈话才重新开始，而那时，说的当然还是那蛮子俄国话，不见得更能听懂。谢—谢咻克—咻克呜哇—呜哇——仿佛靠它一阵一阵的搏动，量着这无尽长夜的一秒一秒。

"俄国人难道从来不睡觉？"威尔弗雷德想。他不再遮掩，捧着作痛的头起身，跌跌撞撞奔沙发去。谁也不在乎他为了他们捆住了阿斯莫德。对了——为什么偏偏是阿斯莫德？他往沙发上一躺，立时淹进深沉漆黑的睡眠里。

"你用不着那样望着我，瓦里娅，"帕维尔抬起疲惫的脸说。"我看你是丢了魂了。咱们这儿有的是一位叫人喜欢的、合格的年轻人——我自家的亲戚——咱们的女儿心甘情愿挑他做了丈夫——"

"帕夫利克——想想。"

===Original===

“Well, my dear, I’m not jumping to any conclusions. I’m only asking you not to be so sure that your daughter shared your own ignorance as to the actual significance of that paper she so eagerly signed. She has plenty of sense, and she must have realized that one doesn’t sign lawyers’ papers without committing oneself to something. She realized from the first, I think, that it made a married woman of her. She was probably waiting and wondering, in her room﻿—a neglected bride﻿—”

He paused to listen for that urgent, “Pavlik﻿—*think*” again, but it did not come. Hearing no protest from his wife, he allowed his always self-sufficient mind to nurse the illusion that he had prevailed. Varya believed him﻿—and why not? He had told her the truth. The paper was binding, the marriage sanctified by lawyers’ law, according to Western European custom if not Russian custom. He was not accustomed to imagining what lay behind other people’s silences, nor had he any ears to hear things that were not uttered. His roving telltale eyes, however, refused to look at his wife.

===Chinese===

"唔，亲爱的，我不急于下断语。我只请你别那么拿得稳，以为你女儿对那张她热切签了字的纸的真实含义，同你一样懵懂。她有的是脑子，她一定明白，人不会平白签律师的文书，签字总是把自己交给什么事。我从一开头就觉得，她明白了这会使她成为一个成了婚的女人。她多半正在她屋里等着、纳闷着呢——一个受了冷落的新娘——"

他顿住，等那声紧急的"帕夫利克——想想"再来，可它没有来。听不见妻子抗议，他便容许他那颗永远自足的心，滋养起一个他已说服她的错觉。瓦里娅信了他——为什么不呢？他同她讲的是实情。那张纸有约束力，这桩婚事得了律师法的圣化，就算不合俄国规矩，也合西欧的规矩。他不惯于想象别人沉默背后的事，也没有耳朵去听没有说出口的事。只是他那双乱转的、泄密的眼，不肯朝妻子看。

===Original===

His voice began again with a challenging confident sound. “Tanya’s consent means the more because she is by no means easy to please.﻿ ﻿… Seven betrothals behind her, all broken by her own overfastidiousness and whims. Here we have the pair, obviously falling in love at first sight, like the story books. Here we have Sergei Sergeievitch only too willing to overlook the unfortunate chops and changes of the past (which we have not concealed from him), proposing for our daughter most ardently, and Tanya at once showing an equal eagerness to be his wife. Here we have, most fortunately, on the spot, an English-trained lawyer of a high degree of learning, kindly arranging for us a legal marriage paper such as is daily drawn up in London or Paris or New York or any other sophisticated city﻿—a form of marriage which, in the absence of a Russian priest, is the best we can possibly hope for. How very very much more fortunate we are than those Russian families﻿—we know many of them﻿—who in these hard times, for lack of any religious marriage facilities and too ignorant to obtain legal help, have to dispense with any ceremony at all, however naturally moral, modest, and respectable they may be. We, on the other hand, have a paper, properly signed and in my safe keeping, and if things go wrong I now have a means of protecting my daughter﻿—”

“I can’t hear their voices in the kitchen now,” said Varvara.

===Chinese===

他的声音又响起来，带着挑战的自信。"塔尼娅的同意格外作数，因为她决不是容易取悦的人。…她背后有七回订婚，全会叫她自己的过分挑剔和任性拆散。如今这对儿，分明是一见钟情，同故事书里一样。如今这位谢尔盖·谢尔盖耶维奇，只有太乐意，不计较过去那些不幸的变迁挫折（咱们没瞒他），火热地向咱们女儿求婚，塔尼娅呢，立刻显出同等的急切要做他的妻子。如今又最凑巧，当场有一位英国培养的律师，学问极高，好心地替咱们安排下一纸合法婚姻文书，同伦敦、巴黎、纽约或随便哪座时兴城市里天天起草的那种一模一样——在请不到俄国神父的情况下，这是咱们所能指望的最好形式。咱们比那些俄国人家庭——咱们认识许多这样的——不知多走了多少运：他们在这艰难时世里，因为没有任何宗教婚姻的设施，又太无知，不懂得求法律的帮助，只好什么仪式都免了，不管他们为人多么自然地端正、贞静、体面。咱们呢，手里有一张纸，签得正正经经，归我稳妥保管，万一出了岔子，我如今有了一个保护我女儿的办法——"

"这会儿厨房里听不见他们说话了，"瓦尔瓦拉说。

===Original===

“Well then, we must suppose they’ve done all the talking they want﻿—in the kitchen,” said Pavel with a blustering uneasiness. “But, Varya, listen﻿—what other marriage ceremony can you hope for? What would satisfy you? True, in old Russia nothing short of a ceremony in church would have contented us (little as I, a modernist, believe in these superstitions). But we are not in Russia now. We are exiles. We must no longer feel provincial and Russian in our prejudices. We are citizens of the world now. What is good enough for sophisticated modern people in the great cities of Europe must be good enough for us. We must move with the times. There’s nothing else for it. Angels aren’t likely to come down and conduct an orthodox Russian marriage service for us. That paper, I assure you, satisfied me, a business man, as being comprehensive and legal. You can’t read English, but I can, and I read every word of it. In it Sergei Sergeievitch swears to be a faithful husband to our Tanya, to support her as long as they both live, to provide for her in case of his death, to take her to his home and treat her as what she is﻿—his legal wife. What more can he do? He has signed the paper﻿—you saw him do it. The lawyer had signed it already. I have signed it to show my approval and my willingness to do my part﻿—give our girl the dowry that we always meant to give her, and pay for the valuable services of the lawyer. Tanya signed it with her eyes open. I feel perfectly satisfied about it.”

“Then why were you groaning just now as I came into the room?” asked Varvara.

===Chinese===

"那么，咱们只好认为，他们想说的话——在厨房里——都说完了，"帕维尔说，带着虚张声势的不安。"可是瓦里娅，听着——你还指望什么婚礼？什么才能叫你满足？真的，在旧俄国，不到教堂行礼，什么也不能叫咱们心满意足（尽管我这个摩登人多么不信这些迷信）。可咱们如今不在俄国。咱们是流亡的人。咱们不能再抱着外省的、俄国式的偏见。咱们如今是世界公民。欧洲大城里时兴摩登人觉得够好的，咱们也该觉得够好。咱们得跟上时代。没有别的路。天使不大会下来给咱们主持一场俄罗斯正教婚礼。那张纸，我向你保证，叫我这生意人满意，又全面又合法。你不懂英文，我懂，我一个字一个字读过的。文书里，谢尔盖·谢尔盖耶维奇起誓做咱们塔尼娅忠实的丈夫，两人活着一天就养她一天，他若死了要给她留保障，带她回他家，按她的本分待她——他合法的妻子。他还能做什么？他签了那张纸——你亲眼见的。律师先签了。我签了，表示我的赞同、我愿意尽我的一份——把咱们一贯打算给女儿的嫁妆给她，再付律师先生的宝贵服务的报酬。塔尼娅睁着眼签的。我心里完全踏实。"

"那你方才我进屋时哼哼什么？"瓦尔瓦拉问。

===Original===

“Why﻿ ﻿… well, I’m tired, of course, and overwrought. One has one’s feelings as a father, and one’s only child’s marriage night is an emotional experience for any parent. Even though I am perfectly satisfied that the children are man and wife, and that﻿—Well, Varya, what’s the use of arguing? I gave in to you on every point you mentioned. I let you have a talk alone with the child﻿—and I don’t believe you did anything more helpful than cry over her. I admitted the sense of your suggestion that young Sergei shouldn’t immediately take her away from her home, and he willingly promised to stay here for a fortnight, so as to allow the little creature to adapt herself to marriage in the comfort of familiar surroundings, with you and me at hand. And you saw how Tanya accepted what I said. She was listening attentively to me, I could see that, though she never has a closely attentive look; and if she was staring at her husband all the time, well, isn’t that natural, in a young bride? And then, when I had explained to her gently that by signing that lawyer’s paper the marriage had become a legal thing and that her husband was now entitled to some proof of her love and duty, did she protest? My dear Varya, you irritate me inexpressibly when you look like that. I ask you, did she? Compare her behavior tonight with what she did when Boris kissed her at the kissing game﻿—with the fuss she kicked up when Sasha Weber pressed her to marry him before the end of the summer﻿—with her screaming hysterical fit when Petya Isaev kissed her in the garden on her birthday. Well, I ask you﻿—what did she do tonight when she came back from her weep with you? She walked across the kitchen to her husband and just said his name, Seryozha﻿—just like that, in a soft voice﻿—*Seryozha*”﻿—(Ostapenko wheezed a restrained falsetto). “Why, Varya, what’s the matter with you? You yourself were convinced at the time. You said to me, ‘Come away, Pavlik, it’s their affair, after all. Let’s leave them to work it out together, whichever way they wish.’ ”

Varvara was shaken. “Can it be really all right?” she asked of the witnessing air. She was so well accustomed to thinking that everything had an odd contrary rightness of its own, if an Ostapenko thought it right. “Oh, Pavlik﻿—if you would only come free of these words and *tell me* out of your own heart if it’s all right.”

===Chinese===

"为什么……唉，我累了，当然，又劳神。人做父亲的有做父亲的情感，独养女儿成婚的夜晚，做父母的心里哪能不翻腾。尽管我完全相信，孩子们已是夫妻，而且——唉，瓦里娅，争有什么用？你提的每一点我都依了你。我让你单独同孩子谈了一场——我看你除了对她哭，也没干出什么更有用的来。我也认了，你那个建议有道理：小谢尔盖不该立刻把她从家里带走，他也满口答应在这里住上半个月，好叫这小生命在她熟悉的环境里舒舒服服地适应婚姻，有你我在身边。你也看见塔尼娅怎么接受我的话了。她在专心听我讲，这我看得出来，虽然她从来没有一副紧紧盯着你的专心相；她要是眼睛一直望着丈夫，那不也自然么，一个年轻新娘？后来，等我柔声细语向她解释清楚，签了那张律师的纸，婚事就成了合法的事，她的丈夫如今有权利得一点她爱情和义务的凭据——她抗议了么？我亲爱的瓦里娅，你那副样子叫我说不出的恼火。我问你，她抗议了么？把她今晚的行为，同比一下她那回亲嘴游戏里鲍里斯吻她时的行为——比一下萨沙·韦伯逼她赶在夏末前嫁给他时她闹的那一场——比一下彼佳·伊萨耶夫（Petya Isaev）在她生日那天在花园里吻她时她那尖叫的癔症。喏，我问你——今晚她同你哭完了回来，做了什么？她穿过厨房走到她丈夫跟前，只叫了他的名字，谢廖沙——就这样，声音软软的——谢廖沙"——（奥斯塔片科用压住的假嗓子学了一声）。"嘿，瓦里娅，你怎么了？你当时自己也是信了的。你跟我说：'走吧，帕夫利克，这到底是他们自己的事。让他们自己去解决，爱怎么解决怎么解决。'"

瓦尔瓦拉动摇了。"真能没事么？"她问着空气里的证人。她太惯于认为：只要一个奥斯塔片科认为对，件事就有它自己的一套古怪的反理。"哦，帕夫利克——你要是能跳出这些话，从你自己心里告诉我，到底没事不没事。"

===Original===

He went on talking. She had known he would never come free of words. He could not. She knew the expression of his mouth﻿—the set of lips determined to convince by words alone. Even when she asked him, as she often did, to *think*﻿—Pavlik﻿—*think*﻿—she knew it was impossible for him to think as well as talk﻿—to go behind his own plausible words, to compare them with the facts in his own brain, and trim his argument to the shape of his inmost conviction. She knew that words were in themselves the stuff of his conviction﻿—he believed what he said, instead of saying what he believed. Yet this was Ostapenkoism; she did not criticize it; words were his genius, she thought. Outsiders might say that he quibbled, but his wife shaped his quibbling to fit into her pride in him. Only Pavlik could quibble as superbly as he quibbled. Only Pavlik could make a thing actually true by proving ingeniously that it was true. He could mold truth. She began to see that this elaborate quibble about Mr. Chew’s paper was sealing the paper itself with an actuality, a significance, that it had not seemed to possess. The paper had floated as negligibly as a dead leaf into Varvara’s notice, and now here it was, transmuted by Ostapenko argument into a heavy lawyer’s parchment, a thing to be kept in the safe, a thing solid enough for the future to be built on. It blew in like a leaf and settled like a stone. Pavlik’s talk and magic had turned the blowing fancy to stone﻿—to the cornerstone of the house.

===Chinese===

他还讲下去。她早知道，他永远跳不出话去。他跳不出。她认得他那张嘴的表情——一双嘴唇，拿定了主意单凭话叫人信。就连她时常求他的那样——想想——帕夫利克——想想——她也知道，他不可能一边想一边说——不可能绕到自己那套言之成理的话背后去，拿它们同他自己脑子里的实情对一对，把论点修成他心底信念的样子。她知道，话本身就是他信念的料——他信他所说的话，而不是说他所信的话。可这就是奥斯塔片科主义；她不批评它；话是他的天才，她想。外人兴许说他诡辩，可他妻子把他的诡辩修削成形，嵌进她对他的骄傲里。只有帕夫利克，能把诡辩诡得这么堂皇。只有帕夫利克，能靠巧妙地证明一件事真，把这件事真真弄成真的。他能捏得动真相。她开始看出，这一篇围着赵先生那张纸绕的精心的诡辩，正在给那张纸本身封上一层实际的、要紧的性质，一层它先前仿佛不曾有的性质。那张纸起初飘进瓦尔瓦拉的眼角，轻得同一片死叶一样，如今呢，叫奥斯塔片科的论证点化成了沉重的一大张律师羊皮纸，一件该收进保险柜的东西，一件结实得可以拿将来往上盖的东西。它像叶一样飘进来，像石头一样落了定。帕夫利克的话和法术，把那阵飘着的空想点成了石头——点成了这座屋子的基石。

===Original===

Varvara was in the grip of reaction from her first moment of furious anger with Pavlik and her suspicion of him. When she had first seen him walking toward the kitchen threshold, pointing out Tanya’s door to young Sergei Malinin, all her faith in him had seemed for a moment to be cracking. At that moment she could have torn him to pieces; he was something namelessly hideous﻿—naked of words at last. Now that he had clothed his intentions with their usual fine wrappings of explanation, she felt like a child who, in the dark, thinks it sees in a moving shadow at the door a bogey arriving frightfully to devour it﻿—and then in the next moment identifies the thing as the sweet familiar shadow of its mother and feels incredibly safe again. Varvara felt the remorse such a child might feel﻿—remorse and sheepishness, for having so mistaken that loving shadow, misheard that dear step, repulsed with panic a friend so tried and so trusted. Must she not make up for her first panic of misunderstanding by understanding with double intensity?

It was the more possible for Pavel to convince Varvara, because she felt herself, in this matter, on his ground, not her own. She had no illusions about the incompleteness of her own nature, though she would have resented bitterly any suggestion that an outsider could see her lack of life. Living with the ebullient Pavel had taught her secret humility. She therefore attached no moral superiority to her fear of love﻿—no *you ought not*﻿—only an alas, *I am not*. Her natural instinct, even if Tatiana had been married with every possible rite of orthodoxy, would have been to lie awake with tears of horror throughout her daughter’s wedding night. This she recognized as morbidness, and was therefore the more inclined to distrust her own almost unbearable instinct of reluctance to believe in the sudden significance of this lawyer’s paper. Paper blessing﻿—church’s blessing﻿—archimandrite’s blessing﻿—God’s blessing﻿—her answer to each or all would have been﻿—*No﻿—no﻿—no﻿—not for my Tanya.﻿ ﻿…*

===Chinese===

瓦尔瓦拉正从起初那一阵对帕夫利克的狂怒和疑心里缓过来。她头一眼看见他朝厨房门槛走去、把塔尼娅的门指给小谢廖沙·马利宁看时，她对他的全部信任，有一霎仿佛都在开裂。那一刻她恨不得把他撕成碎片；他成了件说不出名目的丑东西——终于脱尽了话的裸体。如今他把他的意图重新裹上惯常的、体面的解释的包装，她就像一个在黑地里的小孩子，以为在门口晃动的影子里看见一个妖怪凶恶地走来要吃它——可下一刻认出那东西是母亲的亲爱的影子，于是重新觉得不可思议地安全。瓦尔瓦拉感到那样一个孩子会感到的悔——悔，外加害臊，为那么错认了那亲爱的影子，为听错了那亲爱的脚步，为拿惊惶击退了这样一位久经考验、久经信托的朋友。她难道不该拿加倍的体谅，去弥补她起初那场误解的惊惶么？

帕维尔这回所以更能说服瓦尔瓦拉，也因为她觉得，在这件事上，她站在他的地界上，不是自己的地界上。她对自身天性的欠缺没有幻想，虽然谁要提一句外人看得出她缺着活气，她会恨透了。同沸騙腾騙的帕维尔过日子，教会了她一种秘密的谦卑。所以她不给自己对爱情的恐惧安上任何道德的高——不觉得"你不应该"——只有一声唉，"我不是。"就算塔季扬娜行了正教一切可能的仪节成的婚，她的天性也会叫她在女儿新婚之夜瞪着眼流泪到天亮。这一点她认作病态，于是也就越发信不过她自己那个几乎受不住的本能——那个不肯相信这张律师的纸突然有了重大意义的本能。纸的祝福——教会的祝福——大司祭的祝福——上帝的祝福——对这一样或全部，她的回答都会是——不——不——不——不为我的塔尼娅。…

===Original===

Among Ostapenkos, she was accustomed to being more sober but less inspired. She had felt essentially right as she barred her husband’s path to her daughter’s door, but she was used to feeling right, yet knowing herself wrong. The strange rather than the reasonable must be her accepted standard. The breath of sobriety she introduced was an irrelevance, in this strange family she loved.

Yet﻿—in this case﻿—*Tanya*﻿—that white baffling door of Tanya’s fortress to be knocked upon﻿—to be assailed with such abrupt news from the strong thick world of men.﻿ ﻿…

“I’ll just stand in the passage for a minute to listen if I hear voices,” she said. “He may be arguing with her, trying to persuade her against her will. He’s just a boy﻿—it’s difficult for him. Supposing she’s crying.﻿ ﻿… She cries so quietly.﻿ ﻿…”

“A funny thing,” said Pavel, “to be expecting to hear the bride crying on a night like this.﻿ ﻿…”

Varvara stood in the passage for a minute or two. There was no sound. She turned. Through the front door, left open for coolness this hot night, she saw the houses of Mi-san roofed with silver; she heard the distant barking of a dog sounding like a beating on copper. The alchemist stillness transmuted to bright metal the polished leaves of the zinnias and shrubs in the garden. The sky shone like a spangled dragon’s wing. There were even stars in the dust of the street﻿—a dust of stars, too, over the brick material of the frame of the door, and stars in every spider’s-web. After all, she’s just another flower in another garden, thought Varya dreamily, another starry seed on the wind, blowing home to earth. She was thinking wordlessly; she was seeing a vision of the breaking of dear loneliness, the breaking of the virgin round world by the forces of fire and water and wind, the breaking open of strong remote mountains, breaking into chasms and seas, craters, valleys and peaks﻿—the dumb world breaking with a song of thunder, and broken, being clothed with intention at last﻿—clothed with snows and flowers and blue veils of ice and deserts of corn-colored sand and feathers of fire﻿—a world the richer for its broken integrity. To make a statue was to break a stone. Being alive was a breaking of death. To become something was to shatter the peace of being nothing.

*Come home﻿—come home﻿—come home, Tanya.﻿ ﻿…*

===Chinese===

在奥斯塔片科家的人中间，她惯于做一个更清醒、却更缺少灵感的。她拦住丈夫去女儿门前的路时，心里觉得本质地对，可她也惯于觉得对、又知道自己错。古怪，而不是有理，必得是她认下的标准。她带进这家的这一口清醒的气，在这家她所爱的古怪人家中间，原是一样不相干的东西。

然而——这一回——塔尼娅——塔尼娅那座堡垒的白得叫人困惑的门，要被敲了——要拿男人那又厚又强的世界里这么突兀的消息去攻打。…

"我就到过道里站一分钟，听听有没有声音，"她说。"他兴许在同她争，想违背她的意劝她。他不过是个孩子——他为难。万一她在哭呢。…她哭得那么静。…"

"有趣，"帕维尔说，"这样的夜里，居然等着听新娘哭。…"

瓦尔瓦拉在过道里站了一两分钟。没有声音。她转过身。前门为着这闷热的夜敞着，透过它，她看见米山的房子顶着一层银；她听见远远的狗吠，响得像敲铜。炼金术士一般的寂静，把园子里百日菊和灌木的亮叶子点化成闪光的金属。天空亮得像一面撒满亮片的龙翼。连街上的尘土里都是星——门框的砖上也是一层星尘，每一张蛛网里也是星。说到底，她不过是另一座花园里的另一朵花，瓦里娅迷迷糊糊地想，风里另一粒带星的种子，吹着，吹回地上的家。她无语地想着；她看见一幅幻象：亲爱的孤独破裂了，浑圆的处女世界叫火、水、风的力量破裂了，坚强而遥远的大山裂开了，裂成深渊和海洋、火山口、峡谷和峰峦——哑默的世界带着一声雷霆的歌破裂了，破裂了，终于穿上了意图——穿上雪和花、冰的蓝面纱和谷色沙的沙漠、火的羽毛——一个世界，因为它的完整破了，反而更丰富。雕像，是拿石头的一破换来的。活着，是死亡的一破。成为什么，是把什么都不是的那份安静打碎。

回家来——回家来——回家来，塔尼娅。…

===Original===

Varvara roused herself with a start, finding her lips parted to utter a call. She went back into the living-room and found her husband groaning again, his head in his hands.

“What is distressing you so, Pavlik? You are so sure it is all right, and yet you moan and mourn.”

===Chinese===

瓦尔瓦拉猛地回过神，发现自己的嘴唇已张开，就要唤出声来。她回到起居室，见丈夫又哼哼起来，头埋在手里。

"什么事叫你这么难受，帕夫利克？你既然拿得稳没事，却这样呻吟、这样哀声。"

===Original===

“*Tschah!* I don’t know. Leave me alone. No, I’ll tell you. I’m not worrying about our Tanya﻿—she’s all right. She has to grow up sooner or later, and she showed more feeling about this boy than she did about any of the others. I’m sure we were right to strike while the iron was hot. No, I worry about the boy﻿—a decent bright boy﻿—and my cousin’s son, too. What if it were really true that there’s a curse on any lover who comes near our Tanya? We couldn’t say we hadn’t had full warning﻿—she really has sucked the life out of every one of the lads who were attracted by her good looks. Supposing it really is a fact that there is something deadly in her icy pretty looks. Supposing we find this nice boy dead by her side in the morning.﻿ ﻿… What should we say﻿—how should we explain it? The neighbors would﻿—all say ‘well, you’ve had fair warning, haven’t you? It’s as good as murder.’ One can’t disregard such a possibility. There’s something so very strange in the perfectly consistent string of disasters that has followed each of her affairs﻿—disasters to the lads themselves, I mean. I’ve heard of such curses” (Pavel always had precedents ready). “I knew of a nice old woman﻿—very rich, very quiet, very good-natured and considerate﻿—whose servants always died before they had been in her service a year. She was obliged at last to go and live in a hotel at Yalta, and even there, they say, the chambermaids kept on developing mysterious diseases. There are such cases﻿—it’s no good shutting one’s eyes to them. There certainly is something fatal about our Tanya’s effect on men﻿—there isn’t a single one of her admirers that hasn’t reason to regret ever having set eyes on her. Sasha’s dead; Isaev, in the Chinese army, is as good as dead; young Stepan Soloviev is a hopeless sot; Boris threw up a respectable job to go and be a pimp in Shanghai; Vanya seems to have gone entirely off his head. One can’t ignore all that, Varya, one can’t say it’s coincidence﻿—it does look like the work of some kind of devil that possesses our girl. Have we thrown away the life of this nice boy, Sergei Sergeievitch? His affair has gone further than any of the others﻿—the curse would affect him more immediately and fatally.﻿ ﻿… Oh, Varya, I have a dreadful premonition﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

"嘛！我不知道。别理我。不，我告诉你。我不是担心咱们的塔尼娅——她没事。她早晚要长大，何况她对这孩子比对先前哪一个都多情。我敢保咱们趁热打铁打对了。不，我担心的是这孩子——一个体面的、聪明的孩子——又是我表哥的儿子。要是靠近咱们塔尼娅的情人都要遭咒，这话竟是真的呢？咱们不能说没受过充分的警告——叫她那副好容貌引来的小伙子，哪一个的命不叫她吸于了。要是她那副冰冷的俏模样里真有什么致命的东西呢？要是明早晨咱们发现这好孩子死在她身边呢。…咱们说什么——怎么解释？邻居们会——个个说：'喏，你们受够了警告，不是么？这跟谋杀一样。'这种可能性不能不防。她那一件接一件的裙事，每一件后头都紧跟着一场灾——我说的是那些小伙子本人遭的灾——这一连串毫无例外的灾，太古怪了。我听说过这样的咒"（帕维尔永远备着先例）。"我认得一位挺好的老太太——很有钱，很安静，很和气、很休贴——她家的佣人总是一年不到就死。她末了只好上雅尔塔一家旅馆去住，据说就在那儿，客房女招待还是一个接一个地生怪病。这种事例是有的——闭眼不看没有用。咱们塔尼娅对男人的效力里，确实有致命的东西——她的爱慕者里，没有一个不该后悔见了她。萨沙死了；伊萨耶夫（Isaev），在中国军队里，跟死了差不多；小斯捷潘·索洛维约夫成了个没救的酒棍；鲍里斯丢了体面的差事，跑到上海拉皮条去了；瓦尼亚看样子整个儿疯了。这些不能不看，瓦里娅，不能说是碰巧——看着倒真像附在咱们姑娘身上的什么恶魔干的。咱们是不是把这好孩子、这谢尔盖·谢尔盖耶维奇的命也扔了？他的事比谁都走得远——咒会在他身上发得更快、更致命。…哦，瓦里娅，我有一个可怕的预感…

===Original===

I have a terrible feeling that in the morning we shall find him dead﻿—and Tanya icy and quiet as she always is, no sorrier than if she had crushed a spider by mistake. What could we say? How could we explain it to his father and mother?”

“It’s the night makes you have such fancies, Pavlik,” said Varvara. “Two or three o’clock in the morning is always the time when people who can’t sleep have their dreadful fancies.”

“Two or three o’clock in the morning is when people die,” said Pavel Ostapenko, shuddering. “O God! what have I done that my only child is so cursed that I have to fear so for everyone who comes near her? What a silence is in this house, Varya! One can hear no sound of life at all.”

“One couldn’t, anyway, from here.”

Ostapenko looked at the clock. “I wonder why people die in the small hours. I wonder if it’s because all the demons of the night have gathered strength out of the dark by then.”

“No, certainly it isn’t. It is because the pulse beats most weakly then.”

“Yes, but the demons of the night perhaps seize that opportunity. Perhaps people always die then for that reason. The dead never come back to explain to us the deadly wiles of demons. O God! perhaps the boy, even as we speak, feels something freezing his heart, and thinks: ‘They told me the truth about this haunted girl. Why wasn’t I wise in time?’ ”

“Perhaps you are a little tired, Pavlik,” said Varvara. “Or even a little﻿—Well, you know, Pavlik, you drank a great deal of champagne.”

Pavel groaned again. His sense of guilt about his insistence on the consummation of the marriage had strangely disguised itself, transferred itself to a fear for Seryozha’s safety. His imagination was very strong when dealing with the inhuman and mysterious, although it could never penetrate a fellow mortal’s skin and show him a glimpse of the reasonable and sensitive hearts of his neighbors.

“Why don’t you go to bed?” suggested Varvara. “You are doing no good by sitting up﻿—only tormenting yourself.”

Pavel rose to his feet and left the room so promptly that Varvara looked after him in surprise. She was not accustomed to having her suggestions so instantly followed.

===Chinese===

我有一种可怕的感觉，早晨咱们会发现他死了——而塔尼娅呢，冰冷的、静静的，同她一向一样，不见得比错踩了一只蜘蛛更抱歉。咱们能说什么？咱们怎么向他父亲母亲交代？"

"是夜里叫人起这些怪念头，帕夫利克，"瓦尔瓦拉说。"后半夜两三点钟，睡不着的人总在这个时辰起那些可怕的念头。"

"后半夜两三点钟，是人死的时候，"帕维尔·奥斯塔片科说，打了个寒噤。"上帝！我造了什么孽，我的独生孩子这样遭咒，叫我为每一个靠近她的人这样害怕？这屋里多静哪，瓦里娅！一点活气的声音也听不见。"

"从这儿，反正也听不见。"

奥斯塔片科看看钟。"我不明白，人为什么偏在小半夜里死。我不明白，是不是因为黑夜的群魔到那时都从黑暗里攒足了力气。"

"不，当然不是。是因为那时候脉博跳得最弱。"

"是啊，可黑夜的群魔兴许正抓这个机会。人总在那个时辰死，兴许就为的这个。死人从不回来，把群魔那些致命的诡计讲给咱们听。上帝！这孩子，兴许这会儿正说着话，就觉得有什么东西冻住了他的心，想：'他们跟我讲的是实话，这闹鬼的姑娘。我怎么不及时学乖？'"

"你许是有点累了，帕夫利克，"瓦尔瓦拉说。"或者还有点——喏，你知道的，帕夫利克，你喝了好多香槟。"

帕维尔又哼哼起来。他那坚持要圆房的罪感，古怪地化了装、挪了窝，变成了一股对谢廖沙安危的担忧。他那想象力，对付非人的、神秘的东西时非常强大，却永远钻不进一个同类凡人的皮里，让他看一眼邻居们那副有理性的、敏感的心。

"你怎么不上床去？"瓦尔瓦拉劝道。"坐着没有用——只是折磨你自己。"

帕维尔站起身，那么干脆地离开了屋子，瓦尔瓦拉诧异地目送他。她不习惯自己的建议被这么立刻照办。

===Original===

After a moment she rose, too. She looked down for a minute at the sleeping Wilfred. His mouth was wide open; his gold tooth looked ashamed with so much uncovered pink and cavernous mouth round it; as a rule, its glory was enhanced by the discreetness of its glimpsed glitter. “How hospitable we are to our visitors,” thought Varvara with a faint sneer, looking down at Wilfred’s helpless sagging face. Then she went to her bedroom, pausing a moment in the passage to listen to the silence.

Her husband was not in their bedroom. She lay down on the bed in her kimono, and shut her eyes. In the glimmering steel light of early dawn, the dark mark on her cheek looked like a third sunken eye.

She opened her eyes an hour later to find that her husband was still not by her side. As she went back along the passage to look for him, he came in at the front door, looking exhausted and wretched leaning on an earthy spade.

“Why, Pavlik, where have you been? You are all earthy﻿—look at your hands. You look so tired.﻿ ﻿… What have you been doing?”

“Oh leave me alone﻿—don’t nag at me!﻿ ﻿… Oh, Varya, I have such a terrible presentiment of evil to that boy. I’m sure she breathes death﻿ ﻿… the more I think of it, the more sure I am.﻿ ﻿… We are as good as murderers﻿ ﻿…”

Varvara sighed. “Oh, Pavlik, how obstinately fanciful you are when you have been drinking. Now *think*﻿—think of the ordinary everyday world; think of our dear little Tanya learning to knit socks for you, learning to ride a horse, helping me to make the beds and to shell peas.﻿ ﻿… Demons don’t live in such simple things. Here is Katya﻿—she shall make us a cup of tea each.”

For the first beam of the sun, aiming like a wary archer across the red plain, always woke Katya to her work. The energetic old woman had no alarm clock; she relied on the sharp tip of the first ray to pry her little red eyelids apart.

“A cup of tea,” she repeated after Varvara, opening that kitchen door which had been such a tiptoe secret all night, and stumping briskly into the kitchen.

Varvara stood in the front doorway, watching the sun rise﻿—watching the sun climb surefooted up straight rails of red cloud.

Pavel followed Katya into the kitchen. “Katya, you know our little Tanya was married last night,” said Pavel in an uneasy whisper, pinning the old woman’s attention with his wide sharp eyes.

“Married!” exclaimed Katya shrilly.

===Chinese===

过了一霎她也起身了。她低头看了一分钟睡着的威尔弗雷德。他嘴张得老大；他那颗金牙，叫这么一大片没遮没盖的粉红的洞穴嘴围着，显出害臊的样子；平常，它的光荣全靠那一闪一闪、若隐若现的分寸衬托着。"咱们待客待得多殷勤啊，"瓦尔瓦拉望着威尔弗雷德那张没设防的、下垂的脸，淡淡冷笑着想。随后她进了卧室，在过道里停了一霎，听听那寂静。

丈夫不在他们的卧室里。她穿着和服在床上躺下，闭上眼。拂晓那微微发亮的钢色光里，她腮上的黑记，像第三只凹进去的眼睛。

一个钟头后她睁开眼，丈夫还是不在身边。她顺着过道回去找他，正碰上他从前门进来，拄着一把沾土的铁锹，一脸的疲惫和凄惶。

"哎，帕夫利克，你上哪儿去了？你一身土——瞧你的手。你看上去累坏了。……你干什么去了？"

"哦别理我——别数落我！…哦，瓦里娅，我对那孩子有一股可怕的凶兆。我拿得准，她呼出来的是死……我越想越拿得准。……咱们跟杀人犯差不离……"

瓦尔瓦拉叹口气。"哎，帕夫利克，你一喝了酒，就犟着起这些怪念头。现在想想——想想平平常常、天天过日子的人世；想想咱们亲爱的小塔尼娅学着给你织袜子，学骑马，帮我铺床、剥豆子。……魔鬼不住在这些简简单单的东西里。卡佳来了——叫她给咱们一人沏杯茶。"

原来太阳的头一束光，像一名谨慎的弓箭手瞄着红平原射过来，总能把卡佳唤醒去做活。这个精神抖擞的老太太不用闹钟；她全指望头一道光的尖，把她那两片小红眼皮撬开。

"一杯茶，"她跟着瓦尔瓦拉重复一遍，推开那扇整夜蹑手蹑脚当作秘密的厨房门，咚咚地走进厨房去。

瓦尔瓦拉站在前门口，看太阳升上来——看太阳脚步稳稳地，顺着一条条红云的直轨往上爬。

帕维尔跟着卡佳进了厨房。"卡佳，你知道咱们的小塔尼娅昨晚上结婚了，"帕维尔说，不安地打着耳语，用他那双又大又尖的眼睛钉住老太太的注意。

"结婚了！"卡佳尖声喊。

===Original===

“*Pssst!* Yes, married﻿—by this Chinese guest who is a lawyer and knows the London way of marrying without a priest.”

“There is no such thing as marriage without a priest,” began Katya loudly.

“Devil take you, old woman, I tell you there *is*!” said Pavel, convulsed for a second with fury by this bluff attack on his edifice. “Tanya *is* married. She is a wife, legally. She is in that room there with her husband﻿—and with my blessing, I tell you. Do you think I should not be the first to make my only daughter’s honor safe? Don’t be such a fool, old woman, but listen to me. You know Tanya﻿—you’ve known her all her life. You know she’s not like other girls, quite﻿—easy in love, accustomed to kissing and flirting. I want to know if they’re all right﻿—I must know﻿—and I daren’t look in﻿—I daren’t.﻿ ﻿…”

“Pavel Nicholaievitch, you’ve been drinking. Why should you want to spy on a man and his wife﻿—if man and wife they are?﻿ ﻿…”

“They are﻿—they are!” cried Pavel frantically. “I swear they are legally man and wife.﻿ ﻿… It’s only that I have a presentiment.﻿ ﻿… Katya, be a good woman and just open that door a crack﻿—just peep in.﻿ ﻿… I must know the worst now.﻿ ﻿… I daren’t look myself.﻿ ﻿…”

“Ah, *tschah*!” said Katya. She waddled up the little matted slope and listened at Tanya’s door. “There’s no sound,” she said.

“No sound!” echoed Pavel, terrified. “Ah, dear Katya, just open the door a crack, very quietly, tell me how they look.”

Alarmed and shocked by his wild manner, Katya cautiously opened the door a very little, and put her fat red face to the crack. When she turned her face it was creased into a sentimental smile. “I must say they are a pretty pair,” she said, as pleased as though she had had a hand in the creation of their beauty. “As pretty as fairies, asleep in each other’s arms, his lips to her cheek﻿ ﻿…”

“O my God! Safe! Safe!” cried Pavel, and rushed down the passage, calling: “Tea﻿—tea! Varitchka, where’s my tea?” Then he ran like a boy back into the kitchen. “Katya, you might tell Yi to fill up that deep trench he will find in the garden to the east of the poppy bed.”

“I can see it from here,” said Katya. “It looks like a grave.”

===Chinese===

"咐！是，结婚了——用的这位中国客人，他是律师，懂伦敦那一套不用神父成婚的法子。"

"没有神父就不算结婚，"卡佳大声开了腔。

"魔鬼带你走，老太太，我告诉你有就是有！"帕维尔说，这一下冲撞他那座建筑，叫他有一秒钟火得抽搐。"塔尼娅就是结了婚。她是妻子，合法的。她就在那间屋里，同她丈夫——还带着我的祝福，我告诉你。你想我会不头一个把我独养女儿的名分弄稳当？别犯傻了，老太太，听我说。你了解塔尼娅——她生下来你就了解她。你知道她跟别的姑娘不大一样——不惯谈恋爱，不惯亲嘴调情那一套。我想知道他们好不好——我必须知道——可我不敢看——不敢。……"

"帕维尔·尼古拉耶维奇，你喝了酒。人家两口子——就算真是两口子——你凭什么去偷看？…"

"是——是！"帕维尔狂喊。"我赌咒他们是合法的夫妻。……只不过我有个预感。……卡佳，做个好心的女人，把那门开一条缝——就往里瞅一眼。……我现在必须知道个最坏的底。……我自己不敢看。……"

"啊，嚓！"卡佳说。她摇摇摆摆爬上那小片草垫坡道，在塔尼娅门边听了听。"没有声音，"她说。

"没有声音！"帕维尔恐怖地应声。"啊，亲爱的卡佳，就把门开一条缝，轻轻地，告诉我他们什么样子。"

叫他这副狂态惊着了、骇着了，卡佳小心地把门开了小小一条缝，把她那张肥肨的红脸凑到缝上。她转回脸时，脸上皱起一团感伤的笑。"我得说，真是一对漂亮人儿，"她说，欢喜得仿佛他们的美貌里有她一份功劳。"漂亮得跟仙子似的，睡在彼此怀里，他嘴唇贴着她腮帮……"

"哦，我的上帝！安全了！安全了！"帕维尔喊，冲下过道，一路叫："茶——茶！瓦里奇卡，我的茶呢？"随后他像个孩子似的跑回厨房。"卡佳，你叫老伊把园子里那道深沟填上，他会在罂粟花畦东边找着的。"

"我从这儿就看得见，"卡佳说。"看着像座坟。"

===Original===

“Yes﻿—yes. I thought I might want to bury some rubbish today.﻿ ﻿… But I don’t want it now. Tell Yi to set to work on it at once.﻿ ﻿… O God!” he cried, running back to Varvara. “Varitchka, can’t we praise God or something﻿ ﻿… ?”

===Chinese===

"是——是。我想着我今天兴许要埋点废东西。……现在用不着了。叫老伊这就去动手。……哦，上帝！"他喊着，跑回瓦尔瓦拉那边去。"瓦里奇卡，咱们能不能赞美一下上帝，还是怎么着……？"

## XI / 十一
===Original===

Seryozha’s dog, all netted with spiders’ webs and glazed with dew, stood in the doorway, collecting with a high nose the too faint traces of Seryozha’s vicinity. The smell of putrefying fish clinging to Seryozha’s pack in the passageway cheered the dog a good deal; it knew that smell by now as part of the family. The house seemed to be empty of everything but air, and this happy patch of smelly air was a sort of ghost of Seryozha﻿—the next best thing to the presence of that solid and glorious being. The dog stood for a long time with its nose pressed very hard to a spot on the floor on which Seryozha had dropped, hours before, a small shred of fish liver. For quite a minute, the bereaved dog licked this spot slowly, romantically, like a melancholy epicure.

Slanting from the windows were shallow barred slides of sunlight, down and across which raced and blew and eddied the little light glittering typhoons of dust stirred up by Katya’s recent sweeping. The pale floor mats were a rectangular maze of sunlight and shadow; and flies seemed to browse in these square fields of sunlight like cattle seen from an airplane. Now and then this restful illusion was broken by one fly rising and flying petulantly away as another one alighted. A sequin-blue butterfly with a long tail flew uncertainly in from the garden, its shadow dodging across the fields of sun and dikes of shade, and settled on Tanya’s blue cloak which hung on a peg. It opened and closed its wings slowly before its shadow, like a queen trying on a new dress before the glass. Summer morning danced in the house, but, to the dog, human bustle alone spelled morning﻿—and human bustle was disturbingly lacking here. There were so many strangers in the house﻿—the blue butterfly, a couple of thistledowns riding high across the ceiling, a muddy spade with a rather dead section of worm on it, leaning against the wall, a riding-whip smelling of horse, a noisy bee that hovered about the dog’s shrinking ear, an insolent brown hen on the threshold﻿—all strangers﻿—no friends﻿—and the dog loathed strangers. Strangers so often have stings, like bees, or sticks, like beggars, or kicks, like horses; they never smell right.

===Chinese===

谢廖沙的狗，缠了一身蛛网、挂了一层露水，站在门口，高高地举着鼻子，收拢谢廖沙在场的痕迹——那痕迹已经太淡了。过道里谢廖沙包袱上那股腐鱼的气味叫狗大为振奋；如今它已经认得这气味，算作家里的东西了。这房子仿佛除了空气什么都空了，而这一块快活的臭空气，算是谢廖沙的一个鬼魂——仅次于那个结结实实、光彩夺目的人物亲自在场。狗站了很久，鼻子死死抵住地板上的一小块地方——几个钟头前，谢廖沙在那儿掉过一小片鱼肝。足有一分钟，这失了主的狗慢慢地、缠绵地舔那块地方，像一个伤感的美食家。

从窗口斜进来几道浅浅的、带栅栏的日光，卡佳方才扫地搅起来的小小的、亮晶晶的尘土台风，就在这几道光里赛跑、翻涌、打旋。浅色的席子上，日影织成一方一方的迷宫；苍蝇仿佛在这些方方正正的日光田里吃草，像从飞机上望下去的牛群。这安详的幻象时不时被打破：一只苍蝇赌气飞起，另一只落下。一只拖长尾的亮片蓝蝴蝶从园子里飘忽飞进，它的影子闪过一块块日光的田、一道道荫凉的堤，落在挂钉上塔尼娅的蓝斗篷上。它在自己的影子跟前慢慢开合翅膀，像王后对着镜子试一件新衣裳。夏天的早晨在屋里舞蹈；可对狗来说，只有人的忙乱才拼得出“早晨”两个字——而这里的忙乱，偏偏少得叫它不安。屋里生人太多了——蓝蝴蝶，两团高高地飘过天花板的蓟花冠毛，一把靠着墙的沾泥的铁锹，上头还带着半截死蚯蚓，一根有马味的马鞭，一只在狗缩着的耳朵边嗡嗡的蜜蜂，门槛上一只目中无人的褐毛母鸡——全是生人——没有朋友——而狗厌恶生人。生人动不动就带刺，像蜜蜂；动不动就带棍子，像叫花子；动不动就踢人，像马；他们的气味永远不对。

===Original===

A smell casts no shadow before; dogs, therefore, who are led through life by the nose, have to be intensely conservative. They can tolerate no new departures because they can know no destinations. Seryozha’s dog, though trying to be brave, felt as far away from its own tried and trusted circumstances as a man might feel who found himself the first to set foot on the moon. To the dog this house seemed as shimmering and appalling and silent as the moon; this bright gloss of sunlit air, speckled with smell-less strangers, lacking the immediate familiar smell of known gods, was as lifeless as the brittle bodiless glare that lays gray shadows at the feet of the moon’s starved peaks. Traveling from place to place, one carried one’s own exciting, flying world along with one; somewhere close in front of one’s thrilled nose was home, or something like home; somewhere close behind one’s tail was one’s own tramping homespun god. But arrival at Mi-san had meant to the dog an elaborate homelessness. That little spot of concentrated rotten fish smell on the floor was all that was left of home.

Tiptoeing on stiff suspicious toes into the living-room, the dog was much pleased to find Wilfred Chew lying asleep on the sofa. As a rule, the dog thought nothing of Wilfred, but this morning the man could certainly be promoted to the rank of an encouraging smell. The dog pushed its nose into Wilfred’s eye, savoring the blessed tang of something known before. Wilfred, with a loud groan, turned over, flinging his arms across his frowning shut eyes, and the dog whipped its precious self away to the other side of the room, fearing a kick.

A very small piece of goose skin, which, under the table, had escaped Katya’s active brush, comforted the dog for a moment, and this snack inspired the lost animal to go to the kitchen. It knew where the kitchen was; any dog could find a kitchen, even in the moon. Katya had gone to market. The kitchen door at the end of the passage was ajar, and the dog pushed in. In a bucket by the pump it found a priceless treasure of goose bones and gravy-splashed scraps.

===Chinese===

气味不会在前头留下影子；所以，靠鼻子领着一辈子走的狗，只好极端保守。它们容不得任何新花样，因为它们认不得任何目的地。谢廖沙的狗虽然努力勇敢，却觉得自己离那些试惯了、靠得住的环境远得很，好比一个人发现自己头一个踏上月球。在这狗看来，这座房子像月亮一样闪着光、吓人、寂静；这一片亮亮的、满是阳光的空气，缀满了没有气味的生人，缺着熟神当面的熟气味，死气沉沉，就像那层又脆又没有身子的高光，在月亮那些饿瘪的山峰脚边投下灰色的影子。从一处走到另一处，狗随身带着自己那套兴奋的、飞着的世界；激动的鼻子跟前不远，就是家，或者像家的东西；尾巴后头不远，就是自己那个走南闯北的土布神。可到了米桑，对这条狗等于一场精心安排的无家可归。地板上那一小点浓缩的烂鱼气味，是家剩下的全部了。

狗踮着僵硬的、起疑的脚尖进了起居室，看见威尔弗雷德·赵在沙发上睡着，不由大喜。按说，狗从不把威尔弗雷德放在眼里，可今天早晨，这人无疑可以擢升为“一个鼓舞人心的气味”。狗把鼻子拱进威尔弗雷德的眼睛，咂摸那点宝贵的、从前认得的味道。威尔弗雷德大声哼了一声，翻过身去，胳膊横在皱着的闭眼上，狗赶紧把它那条金贵的命甩到屋子另一头，怕挨踢。

桌子底下一小片鹅皮，逃脱了卡佳勤快的扫帚，安慰了狗一小阵；这点小吃又鼓动这迷路的畜生上厨房去。它认得厨房在哪儿；随便哪条狗都找得着厨房，哪怕在月亮上。卡佳赶集去了。过道尽头的厨房门虚掩着，狗拱了进去。在水泵旁的一只桶里，它找到一宗无价之宝——鹅骨头和溅了卤汁的碎屑。

===Original===

Seryozha, coming with a dazed, quiet step into the kitchen, saw his dog and loved it as he had never loved it before. That dusty brindled back seemed to shine with a light of blessed familiarity. Seryozha’s tired eyes, looking at it, seemed to be stroked with a kind of home balm. He stood still watching the dog, his consciousness numbed﻿—as it always was﻿—by the thrill of seeing an animal enjoying itself by itself, unaware of his presence. To watch his dog smiling and snorting into its bucket of ambrosia almost made him feel as if this old worried Seryozha were standing here watching his young self, careless and apart﻿—his young lost self, enjoying something as this old watching Seryozha would never enjoy anything again.

The dog looked up and saw Seryozha. It cringed and crawled toward him, expecting a kick for thieving, and Seryozha, beaming at it, gave it a little soft kick to oblige it. While he focused his eyes exclusively upon it, blurring from his sight the strange surroundings, he could imagine himself that young happy Seryozha again at home in his mother’s kitchen. He could pretend that he would look up in a minute from the dog’s delighted writhings and see the white pansy face of that old clock in the marbled case on the dresser﻿—that kind foolish dial with the six rubbed out﻿—look up and see that it was time to go to work again﻿—to begin another safe known day.

Seryozha, with the dog hurrying triumphantly at his calf﻿—almost pressing its front teeth against his calf in its anxiety not to lose him again﻿—went out through the kitchen door. He crossed the walled Japanese garden and went out into the acre or so of Ostapenko estate, half of which was a vegetable garden and half a railed enclosure for horses. A Korean in his white puffed clothes﻿—looking like a cream cracker﻿—was filling up a trench in the earth of the garden, and Seryozha, loathing the man because he was the only man in sight﻿—and a stranger﻿—put as much distance as possible between them and sat down on the edge of a little stream that ran at the foot of the fence. The high grass, tufted with wild blue geraniums and scarlet lilies, hid Seryozha from the world as he sat down, dangling his feet over the stream. His dog, pressing its seat as near as possible to his, sat down, too, and blew great hot loving breaths into his ear.

===Chinese===

谢廖沙迈着发懵的、轻轻的步子进厨房来，一眼看见他的狗，起了从未有过的疼爱。那张灰扑扑的、带斑纹的背，仿佛泛出一层熟稔的、有福的光。谢廖沙疲倦的眼睛看着它，像被一种家常的香膏抚过。他站着不动，看狗吃，意识麻了——每逢看见牲口自顾自受用、不觉得他在场，他的意识总是这样麻。看他的狗对着满桶的琼浆微笑、哼哧，几乎叫他觉得，仿佛这个满腹心事的谢廖沙站在这儿，看着他自己那个年轻的、没心没肺的身影——他那年轻的、丢失了的自己，正受用着什么；而这个在旁看着的谢廖沙，再也不会受用任何东西了。

狗抬头看见了谢廖沙。它缩着、爬着迎上去，等着为偷嘴挨一脚；谢廖沙笑眯眯的，轻轻给了它一小脚，满足它的指望。只要他把眼睛全聚在狗身上，把生疏的四周从视线里糊掉，他就能想象自己又是那个年轻的、快活的谢廖沙，正待在母亲家的厨房里。他可以假装，过一分钟就从狗那欢天喜地的打滚里抬起头来，看见碗柜上大理石纹壳子里那张三色堇般的老钟脸——那张又和善又犯傻的钟面，六字磨没了——抬起头看见又该上工了——又该开始一个稳当的、熟门熟路的日子了。

谢廖沙往外走，狗得意洋洋地紧贴着他的小腿——急得几乎拿门牙抵住他的小腿肚，生怕再把他丢了——穿过厨房门。他走过带围墙的日式园子，走进那一英亩上下的奥斯塔片科家庄产：一半是菜园，一半是围了栏杆的马圈。一个朝鲜人，裹在他那身鼓鼓的白衣裳里——活像一块奶油苏打饼干——正在菜地里填一条沟。谢廖沙厌恶这个人，因为他是眼前唯一的活人——又是个生人——便尽量躲他远远的，在栅栏脚下流过的一条小溪边坐下来。高草里一丛丛野蓝老鹳草、红百合，把坐下的谢廖沙同世界隔开了；他把脚悬在水面上荡。他的狗把屁股尽量贴着他的坐处，也坐下了，朝他耳朵里喷着滚烫的、热爱的鼻息。

===Original===

Seryozha threw his arms round the dog’s neck and cried into its shoulder.

For the first time in his life, Seryozha was shaken﻿—shaken in his stalwart anonymity﻿—called home to self-consciousness by a sort of earthquake of the heart. He had been invisible, he had been a matter of course, he had been too close to see, he had been a hollow yet satisfactory person labeled Sergei Sergeievitch Malinin. He had no more known the creature that moved behind that name than he had known the shape of the bones that moved beneath his flesh and skin. The only mystery about the anonymous blank life that lay behind the name Sergei Malinin was found in the repeated utterance of the name itself, curiously enough. A delicious poised strangeness perched on the peak of the soul, when one said that name﻿—Sergei Malinin﻿—Sergei Malinin﻿—a hundred times over. This slippery transparent Seryozha, through whom, as it seemed, one could see the sky, was a hill of glass on which no bird but that winged mystery of his own mesmeric name could find foothold. As for love﻿—women’s love﻿—friend’s love﻿—self-love﻿—a hill of glass afforded no hospitality to such flying visitors.

Now, this earthquake intrusion of a trespasser had shaken him awake﻿—had forced him to turn and meet himself. He was recognized as a man, as he had longed to be recognized; he was traveling, far from his mother, as he had longed to travel; he was married, by his own expressed wish, to the most beautiful and gentle girl he had ever seen. And he felt the lonely fright of a chicken outside the egg, of a fledgeling outside the nest, of a weaned puppy refused its mother’s warm teats. He shook with a fevered longing to go back﻿—to go back into safety﻿—to be warm and careless in little yesterday again, instead of turned loose in this wide draughty today, with no guide but his own reluctant maturity.

===Chinese===

谢廖沙搂住狗脖子，对着它的肩头哭起来。

有生头一回，谢廖沙被摇撼了——他那一身的无名无我被摇撼了——被一场心里的地震唤回了自觉。他从前是无形的，是理所当然的，是近得看不见的，是一个空心却又叫人满意的、贴着“谢尔盖·谢尔盖耶维奇·马利宁”标签的人。他从来没有认得那个躲在这名字后面行动的活物，正如他不认得在他皮肉底下行动的那副骨头的形状。说来也怪，“谢尔盖·马利宁”这名字背后那段无名的、空白的生活，唯一的神秘，就在这名字本身的反复呼唤里。把这名字念上一遍——谢尔盖·马利宁——谢尔盖·马利宁——念上一百遍，灵魂的峰顶便栖着一种美妙的、悬着的陌生。这个滑溜溜、透明条的谢廖沙，仿佛人能透过他看见天，是一座玻璃山，除了他那有魔力的名字化成的带翅膀的谜，没有一只鸟找得到落脚的地方。至于爱情——女人的爱——朋友的爱——自己的爱——一座玻璃山，款待不了这样的飞客。

如今，一个闯入者这一场地震般的侵犯，把他摇醒了——逼他转过身来，同自己打照面。他被认作一个月人了，正是他巴望的；他正出着远门，远离母亲，正是他巴望的远行；他结了婚，凭他亲口的心愿，娶了他平生见过的最美最温柔的姑娘。可他觉着小鸡出壳那种孤单的怕，雏鸟离巢那种孤单的怕，断了奶的狗崽子被拒绝在母亲温热的奶头之外那种孤单的怕。他发着抖，一股热病似的渴望，要回去——退回安全里去——重新暖暖和和、无忧无虑地待在小小的“昨天”里，而不是这样被撒在四面透风的“今天”，除了自己那份勉强的成熟，没有一个向导。

===Original===

Seryozha tried to dislodge a stone in the bank with his heel; he kicked it spitefully, as though he were trying to demolish something menacing. Growing up, then, was a trap; he had been lured into it by an exquisite decoy and must spend the rest of his life pressing his face against iron bars. There was Tanya, the decoy, a prisoner with him﻿—still exquisite﻿—still his. But Seryozha had never felt the need of a friend or a comforter﻿—had never yet desired reinforcement against himself or anyone else; he had not known that even his mother’s affection was valuable to him﻿—he had thought it contemptible, quite negligible﻿—though now he saw it tenderly as part of the furniture of little safe yesterday. He had a mournful senseless vision of himself now, spinning a whipping-top in the street of Chi-tao-kou﻿—a game that needs no partner. And here was Tanya﻿—a spray of orchids handed to a person who needed both his hands for the whipping of his top.

What was that Mr. Chew had said about cold devils? There were no devils, really, but there were strangers. As a boy, yesterday, Seryozha had never noticed strangers. Now, it seemed, a stranger could trespass﻿—could lie in one’s arms all night and yet never be known, never be simple, forgotten, easy, taken for granted. He must live uneasy, now, he must come inside himself and think. This was the result of the cold presence of strangers; no magic smoke﻿—no heart and liver of an enchanted fish﻿—could exorcise that trespassing presence.

Seryozha’s body was not accustomed to being used by thought. It scarcely knew how to behave while thinking. His heel kicked and kicked at the stone, and when the stone at last fell with a splash into the stream, his body felt innocently triumphant, and his lips began to whistle by themselves﻿—a low, flat whistle.

===Chinese===

谢廖沙拿脚跟去蹬溪岸里的一块石头；他恶狠狠地踢它，仿佛要拆掉什么威胁他的东西。原来长大是个圈套；他被一个精巧的诱饵诱进来，往后一辈子，都得把脸贴在铁栏上过。塔尼娅就是那个诱饵，同他一起的囚徒——还是那样精巧——还是他的。可谢廖沙从来不需要朋友，不需要安慰他的人——从来不曾渴望过什么增援，来对付他自己或者任何人；他连母亲的爱对他多值钱都不知道——从前只当它可鄙，全不值一提——如今才温温地看见，它是小小的、安稳的昨天里的一件家具。这一刻他眼前浮起一幅哀哀的、没道理的图景：他自己正在七道沟街上抽陀螺——一种用不着伙伴的游戏。而塔尼娅在这儿——一枝兰花，送给了一个两只手都得用来抽陀螺的人。

那位赵先生说过什么冷魔鬼来着？魔鬼其实是没有的，有的是生人。昨天，还是个孩子的时候，谢廖沙从不留意生人。如今呢，生人竟能闯进来——竟能整夜躺在一个人怀里，却永远认不得，永远单纯不了，忘不掉，松不下心，理所当然不起来。如今他不得不提着心过活，不得不走进自己里面去想。这是生人冷冷的在场的后果；什么法术的烟——什么魔鱼的心肝——也驱不走那闯进来的在场。

谢廖沙的身子不惯叫思想使唤。思想的时候，它简直不知道该怎么安放自己。他的脚跟踢呀踢那块石头，石头终于扑通一声落进溪水里，他的身子天真地得意起来，嘴唇自顾自吹起口哨——低低的、平平的一声。

===Original===

His dog was greatly cheered by this sound, and still more delighted to be suddenly pushed off the bank into the water. Seryozha’s body, having achieved one splash, desired another. The dog laughed in the water, rolling its entranced eyes upward, and, finding that it could not jump up to the top of the high bank again from the hampering water, ran gayly a few yards down the current to a point at which the stream flattened out into strands of sand and shallow bubbling rapids over pebbles﻿—a perfect working model of a Manchurian river. Seryozha’s eyes﻿—always alert for miniature things﻿—noticed the fidelity to fact of this toy river. And his eyes remembered the spring in Manchuria﻿—that rolling golden lark-shrill Manchurian spring that was, to him, home and yesterday. He remembered tremendous auburn distances and wide tender curves of marbled colors in varied earth﻿—yellow deepening to orange﻿—orange to red﻿—red paling to sandy﻿—sandy to cream﻿—with here and there the faint green flush of pricking grass﻿—and on every rainbow hillside, a white spot of Korean moving slowly along the edge of a growing parallelogram of new-furrowed land behind a yellow blob of bullock.

===Chinese===

这声音叫狗大为振奋，更高兴的是忽然被从岸上推下水。谢廖沙的身子溅起一朵水花，还想再溅一朵。狗在水里笑，入迷的眼睛朝上翻；它发现从碍事的水里跳不上那么高的岸了，便欢欢喜喜顺流跑下几码，跑到溪水摊平成一股股沙、一片片卵石上咕嘟冒泡的浅滩的地方——一条满洲河的完美模型。谢廖沙的眼睛——永远在留神小东西——注意到这条玩具河的忠实。他的眼睛想起了满洲的春天——那个滚滚的、金色的、云雀尖叫的满洲之春，在他就是家，就是昨天。他想起了大片赫红色的远景，各色泥土织出的一道道宽厚温柔的纹理——黄得深了成橙——橙得深了成红——红得淡了成沙色——沙色淡成乳白——四处缀着草芽刺出的一点淡绿——每一面虹色的山坡上，一个小小的白点，一个朝鲜人，跟在一坨黄的犍牛后头，沿着一片越拉越大的新犁的地，慢慢移动。

===Original===

“Ah, *tschah*!” said Seryozha, throwing a stone at a bird. The stone rustled and ricocheted through the bush beneath which the bird sat, but the bird scarcely moved. “A funny thing,” thought Seryozha. “A bird that’s not afraid of a stone.﻿ ﻿…” He took off his shoes and splashed across the stream. The bird﻿—a sparrow﻿—looked at him with an anguished round eye, but it scarcely moved. Seryozha put his hand over it, loving it as soon as he saw it was too sad to be his prey. Caked round one of its claws was a ball of clay as big as a walnut. The bird must have alighted unwarily on some unusually wet adhesive surface﻿—it must have been involved in some combination of circumstances outside common bird-experience﻿—so now it went hopelessly encumbered, a crawling thing about the grass instead of a brown flash in a tree. Seryozha took out his pocket knife, and with his cautious clever blunt hands began flaking off the hard-baked mud, sliver by sliver, till first one little scaly knuckle appeared, then another. The anatomy of birds’ claws he studied by comparison with the free claw, so that the point of his knife knew where to be bold and where delicate. The bird’s eye, bright and silly as a sequin, remained fixed upon him; its little bones, wrapped thickly yet unsubstantially in a warm padding of feathers, seemed swooning in his hand. After half an hour’s delicious mincing work, the whole claw was free; the knife had made no mistake. “Stand up, bird,” said Setyozha impatiently, as the bird reeled on his wrist. Its freed claw remembered, hesitantly, how to grip; it tautened its body, threw a glance of sharp loathing at its savior, and flew a few yards. “Oh, get away now, bird,” said Seryozha, and threw a stone at it.

“The darling thing,” said Tatiana’s voice behind him. “It thinks itself *so* clever.” She stood in the long grass, biting a blade of grass, her head bold and vivid against the faint blue morning sky. “It thinks it used your knife so neatly﻿—made a fool of you. It thinks you were trying to eat it or something, and freed it by mistake because it was so clever. It’s laughing up its wing at you.”

===Chinese===

“啊，嚓！”谢廖沙说，朝一只鸟扔了块石头。石头簌簌地擦过鸟栖的灌木丛，弹开了，那鸟却几乎不动。“怪事，”谢廖沙想。“一只不怕石头的鸟。……”他脱了鞋，蹚水过了溪。那鸟——一只麻雀——拿一只痛苦的圆眼睛看他，还是几乎不动。谢廖沙把手罩上去，一见它伤心得够不上当他的猎物，就爱上了它。它一只爪子上结着一团泥，核桃那么大。这鸟准是没留神落在了什么特别湿、特别黏的东西上——准是撞上了什么鸟的常理之外的巧合——如今只好带着累赘绝望地走，在草里爬，再不能在树上一道棕色的闪光。谢廖沙掏出折刀，用他那双谨慎、灵巧、粗短的手，一片一片削那烤硬的泥，直到先露出一个小小的鳞壳的关节，又露出一个。鸟爪的构造，他拿那只自由爪子对照着研究，好让刀尖知道哪儿该胆大，哪儿该手轻。鸟的眼睛，亮得傻，像一片亮片，定定地望他；它那小小的骨头，厚厚地却又虚虚地裹在一层暖绒毛里，在他手心里仿佛晕了过去。半个钟头津津有味的细工之后，整只爪子自由了；刀子没有一下走失。“站起来，鸟，”谢廖沙不耐烦地说，那鸟在他腕上打晃。它那只解放的爪子迟疑地记起了怎么抓握；它绷紧身子，朝它的救命恩人狠狠剜了一眼，飞出几码远。“哦，快走吧，鸟，”谢廖沙说，朝它扔了块石头。

“多可爱的东西，”塔季扬娜的声音在他背后说。“它自以为聪明极了。”她站在深草里，咬着一根草叶，头迎着淡淡的蓝早晨的天，又大胆，又鲜明。“它以为它把你的刀使得那样利落——把你耍了。它以为你要吃它什么的，因为它太聪明，倒被错放了。它正躲在翅膀底下笑你呢。”

===Original===

“It was very kind of me to take so much trouble about a little idiot of a bird,” said Seryozha, throwing stone after stone at the bird as it fluttered from branch to branch of a low tree. “You praise these silly beasts so, but, say what you will, we’re better friends to them than they to us.”

“Oh, of course﻿ ﻿… but what are friends, after all? Just messy things.﻿ ﻿…”

She wondered why he looked so sulky. Had she grieved him? Ought a wife to be somehow different? She thought alternately. “Well﻿—it’s what I am﻿—he must take it or leave it﻿ ﻿…” and, “But was I wrong? ought I to have been different?” She was in two worlds﻿—her heart away in its solitary and exciting wilderness, her body watching for a signal from her lover, and desperately ignorant of how to obey the signal when it should come.

Seryozha was sullenly thinking, who were these two persons discussing nature? Sergei Sergeievitch Malinin, a married man, and Tatiana Pavlovna, his wife, two grown-up people with interesting thoughts. What if he sprang to his feet now, and ran home? He thought of Sonia Matvievna﻿—that easy squeaking creature in Chi-tao-kou. Her conversation was all giggle﻿—one giggle for yes﻿—another giggle for no﻿—no giggle ever meant anything so complicated as a discussion of a sparrow’s reaction to an act of human helpfulness. Then he remembered the consenting Tanya of last night. Sonia Matvievna would have had only a giggle for that, too.

“Ah, Tanya﻿—Tanya﻿—come closer.﻿ ﻿…”

She knelt by his side at once and kissed him lightly on one eye. His dog, inspired by this, rushed upon him and kissed him, much more clumsily and ardently, on the other. They all laughed.

Seryozha stopped laughing rather soon. In fact only the dog went on. It was insensitive to atmosphere, and knew nothing about the domestic changes in divine Valhalla since yesterday. Tatiana stopped laughing and watched Seryozha’s face. Even the kiss hadn’t been right, she could see.

===Chinese===

“为一只小鸟傻子费这么大劲，我真是太好心了，”谢廖沙说，一边朝那鸟一块接一块地扔石头，看它在矮树的枝子间扑腾。“你们这样夸这些蠢畜生；可随你怎么说，我们待它们，比它们待我们够朋友。”

“哦，那当然……可朋友到底是什么呢？不过是些乱糟糟的东西。……”

她纳闷他怎么一脸的不高兴。莫非她伤了他？做妻子的，是不是该换一副样子？她翻来覆去地想：“呃——我就是这个样——他要就要，不要拉倒……”又想：“可我错了么？我是不是该换一副样子？”她人在两个世界里——心在那片孤零零、兴奋的荒野里，身子却在等她的情人发信号，信号真来了，又全不知道该怎样听命。

谢廖沙阴着脸想：这两个谈天说地的人是谁？谢尔盖·谢尔盖耶维奇·马利宁，一个成了家的男人；塔季扬娜·帕夫洛夫娜，他的妻子；两个有有趣的思想的成年人。要是他这会儿一跃而起，跑回家去呢？他想起索尼娅·马特维耶夫娜——七道沟那个轻易就咯咯笑的家伙。她的话全是笑——一声笑是“是”——一声笑是“不是”——哪一声笑也不会复杂到像讨论麻雀对一桩人类善举的反应。随后他想起昨夜里依从的塔尼娅。索尼娅·马特维耶夫娜对那个，怕也只有一声笑。

“啊，塔尼娅——塔尼娅——再靠近些。……”

她立刻跪到他身边，在他一只眼睛上轻轻亲了一下。狗受了启发，扑上来，在他另一只眼睛上笨拙得多、热烈得多地亲了一口。三个人都笑了。

谢廖沙的笑收得很快。其实只有狗还在笑。它对气氛不敏感，也不知道神圣的瓦尔哈拉打昨天起发生了什么家变。塔季扬娜止住笑，端详谢廖沙的脸。连那个吻都不对，她看得出来。

===Original===

Seryozha blinked the eye that she had kissed. It felt shocked by that light, quiet kiss; the eyelid fluttered by itself like the skin of a horse’s shoulder. He supposed that he was hungry, though he wasn’t quite sure. The skin of his face felt disappointed. His ears listened for some loud vulgar shout or laugh. Girls were fun, and one was fun to girls. Yet Tanya, though so lovely, so darling, was no fun﻿—nor did she find him fun. What, then, was between them﻿—something more exciting than fun﻿—or nothing? Now, for instance, she suddenly seized his hand. Why should she do this, if it were no joke to her? Why should she want to hold his not very clean hand﻿—so quietly, so seriously? Her thin hand seemed to need no pinching﻿—no activity at all. He held it, with a surprise that gradually changed to pleasure. Vaguely, with no recognition more articulate than a faint prevision of serenity, he began to know that this quiet taking for granted was at least as fit a sequel to last night’s strange joy as the restlessness and roguishness of Sonia Matvievna would have been. Something was born between Seryozha and Tanya at that moment﻿—a taking-for-granted for *two*﻿—a doubling, instead of a halving, of anonymity and unconsciousness.

“Do people ever have breakfast, in Korea?” asked Seryozha. The sun was quite high. This visit seemed to lack landmarks, somehow. And he had promised to stay for a fortnight. Was it only perhaps hunger that made him feel that he would never be at home in life again?

They walked toward the house. Seryozha’s dog led the way. It hoped that their destination was the bucket full of goose scraps, and writhed winningly at the back door. But Seryozha and Tatiana walked to the front door and into the living-room.

===Chinese===

谢廖沙眨了眨被她吻过的那只眼睛。那轻轻的、安静的一吻叫它震了一下；眼皮自个儿扑闪，像马肩上的皮。他猜想自己是饿了，虽然拿不准。他脸上的皮肤觉出失望。他的耳朵等着一声响亮的、野气的喊或笑。姑娘们有趣，人对姑娘们也有趣。可塔尼娅虽然这样可爱，这样亲，却不好玩——她也不觉得他好玩。那么他们中间是什么——一样比好玩更叫人心跳的东西——还是什么都没有？就说这会儿，她忽然抓住他的手。这对她要不是玩笑，她干吗这样？她干吗想牵住他这只不算太干净的手——这样静，这样认真？她那只瘦手好像不需要捏一把——什么花样都不要。他握着它，惊讶渐渐变成欢喜。模模糊糊地——说不出什么，只隐隐预见一种安稳——他开始明白：这种安安静静的视作当然，至少同索尼娅·马特维耶夫娜的坐不定、使坏一样，配得上接在昨夜那场奇怪的欢喜后头。就在那一刻，谢廖沙同塔尼娅之间诞生了一样东西——一份属于两个人的视作当然——无名与无心不是减半，而是翻倍。

“在朝鲜，人们到底吃不吃早饭？”谢廖沙问。太阳已经很高了。这一场做客，不知怎么，好像缺少里程碑。何况他答应住满两星期。难道只是饿，才叫他觉得这辈子再也不会自在了吗？

他们朝屋子走去。谢廖沙的狗领路。它巴望目的地是那桶鹅肉碎，在后门口讨好地打滚。可谢廖沙同塔季扬娜偏走向前门，进了起居室。

===Original===

Pavel Ostapenko sat there, leaning over the still sleeping Wilfred, looking rather like the Doctor in Sir Luke Fildes’ picture, but not feeling like that at all. Pavel was haggard and cross. At the moment of his waking, the thought had broken on his brain like a clap of thunder: “What will happen when Chew wakes up﻿—sober? What if he says the paper is not legal?” Pavel, of course, had not for a moment entertained the thought, “What if I were wrong?”﻿—but only the much more torturing doubt﻿—“What if he says I was wrong, and tells the others so?” He had spent an uncomfortable hour wondering whether the impossible could happen﻿—whether Pavel Ostapenko could be accused of having made a mistake. Every voice in the house, except one, could now be depended on to say, “You were perfectly right, Pavel Nicholaievitch,” and that, of course, was just the same as *being* right. But, unfortunately, the one voice that remained, so far, silent in this necessary chorus was an indispensable one. And still more unfortunately, the lips that should form these vital words were sealed by intemperate sleep, and the understanding that should evolve the utterance could not be reached by Ostapenko logic, however eloquently expressed, even had it been awake. What on earth was the Almighty about, thought Pavel irritably, to create such a superfluity of human beings who were unfamiliar with the language that Pavel Ostapenko could use so skillfully. Unless something was done, an unthinkable emergency would arise﻿—Pavel Ostapenko would be humiliated in the eyes of his inferiors. It followed that Pavel must somehow, in some language not yet invented, have a talk with Wilfred before anyone else had a chance to do so. Pavel must be ready with a sufficiently persuasive argument to induce Wilfred, the moment he awoke, to admit﻿—“You were perfectly right, Pavel Nicholaievitch, in the course you took,” and to confirm this opinion triumphantly before the family.

Pavel sat with one elbow on his knee, glaring and breathing hotly at the innocent blank face of Wilfred. Beside him on a table were one hundred and fifty yen in notes, a Russian-English dictionary, an elementary English grammar, an illustrated history of the Russian Empire, several large sheets of paper covered with English words in wavering block letters, and the deed drawn up by Wilfred the night before.

===Chinese===

帕维尔·奥斯塔片科坐在那儿，俯身对着还睡着的威尔弗雷德，样子颇像卢克·菲尔茨爵士那幅画里的医生，心里却全然不是那么回事。帕维尔形容枯槁，火气很大。醒来的那一刻，一个念头像炸雷劈进他脑子：“赵醒了——酒醒了——会怎么样？要是他说那张纸不合法呢？”当然，帕维尔一刻也不曾有过“要是我错了呢”的念头——有的只是折磨得多的疑惧——“要是他说我错了，还告诉大家呢？”他坐立不安地过了一个钟头，琢磨不可能的事会不会发生——帕维尔·奥斯塔片科会不会被人指为办错了一件事。如今这家里每一张嘴，除一张，都保险会说：“您全对，帕维尔·尼古拉耶维奇。”这当然就等于真的对。可惜，这必需的大合唱里至今沉默的那一张，偏偏少不了。更可惜的是，该吐出这几句要紧话的嘴唇，叫酗酒般的酣睡封住了；那份该酝酿出这句话的理解力，任凭奥斯塔片科式的逻辑讲得天花乱坠，也够不着——哪怕它是醒着的。全能的上帝到底忙什么，帕维尔烦躁地想，造出这么多不熟悉帕维尔·奥斯塔片科运用得那样娴熟的语言的人。不赶紧想办法，就要出一件不堪设想的急事——帕维尔·奥斯塔片科要在下属的眼里丢人了。因此，帕维尔必须抢在任何人前头，用一种还没发明出来的语言，同威尔弗雷德谈一谈。帕维尔必须备好一套足够有说服力的道理，叫威尔弗雷德一睁眼就承认——“您走的这步棋全对，帕维尔·尼古拉耶维奇”——并且当着全家洋洋得意地把这意见坐实。

帕维尔坐着，一肘支在膝上，瞪着威尔弗雷德那张无事的、空白的脸，朝他喷着热气。他身边一张桌上，摆着一百五十日元钞票、一本俄英词典、一本初级英语语法、一册插图本俄罗斯帝国史、几大张写满歪歪扭扭大块英文字母的纸，还有威尔弗雷德昨夜拟的那份契据。

===Original===

“*Tschah!* Go away,” whispered Pavel, irascibly, to Seryozha and Tatiana as they came hungrily in. The foolish interfering creatures might awaken Wilfred prematurely, and Seryozha﻿—the interpreter﻿—would then have the dangerous first word.

Varvara, hearing the young people come in, joined them at the living-room door, carrying a tray. “Breakfast, Pavlik,” said Varvara. “It is eleven o’clock.”

“Devil take you all!” said Pavel in a furious whisper that scraped his throat. “Who in the world wants breakfast at this time of day? Can’t you leave the poor devil to finish his sleep in peace?”

Varvara, carried forward by the slow impetus of her grave assumption that breakfast was needed, put the tray on the table before she turned to her husband in surprise. “Who wants breakfast, you say!” she echoed. “Why, Pavlik, everyone wants﻿—”

Pavel rose to his feet in a shivering paroxysm of anger, and waved his clenched fists in the air. Varvara snatched the loaded tray up just in time to prevent him from seizing it and throwing it through the door. With the tray in her hands, she recovered her hard calm manner. “Pavel Nicholaievitch is rather tired this morning,” she said to Seryozha and, squaring her angular shoulders, she led the retreat from the room.

Pavel was left with the prostrate Wilfred, whose plaintive expression suggested that he was locked in a prison of disquieting dreams. One could almost see his teased brain beating at the closed shutters of his eyes.

“Should I not wake him?” Pavel asked himself. “He isn’t enjoying himself, wherever he is.”

But just as he leaned forward to say some urgent awakening word, the door opened and Katya, the servant, came in, with an expression of﻿—“Leave him to me; I’ll manage him.” Seryozha’s dog bustled after her. It thought the stout kitchen-smelling Katya a most delightful woman, and innocently mistook her for its hostess.

“Pavel Nicholaievitch,” said Katya, “you must allow me to lay the table for breakfast. It is﻿—”

Pavel rushed upon her with a roar, all his teeth showing, arms and legs sprawling across the air. The outraged old woman fled a few paces, then tried to make a stand in the passage. But Pavel slammed the door in her face with such a bang that the dog squeaked as it fled, believing itself shot.

===Chinese===

“嚓！走开，”帕维尔压着嗓子、恶狠狠地对进屋来觅食的谢廖沙同塔季扬娜说。这些蠢货、这些碍事的活物，说不定会把威尔弗雷德提前吵醒，而谢廖沙——那个翻译——就会抢先说出危险的头一句话。

瓦尔瓦拉听见年轻人进来，端着托盘到起居室门口会他们。“早饭，帕夫利克，”瓦尔瓦拉说。“十一点了。”

“叫鬼抓了你们这一家！”帕维尔怒气冲冲地耳语，嗓子都刮哑了。“大天白日，谁这时候吃早饭？就不能叫那可怜鬼安安稳稳睡完？”

瓦尔瓦拉被她那套“早饭总是要吃的”的庄严惯性推着，先把托盘放在桌上，才吃惊地转向丈夫。“谁吃早饭，你说！”她应道。“哟，帕夫利克，人人都——”

帕维尔一阵发抖的狂怒，站起身，抡起攥紧的双拳在空中挥。瓦尔瓦拉一把端起满满一托盘，赶在他夺过去往门外掼之前。托盘在手，她又恢复了她那一副冷硬的安详。“帕维尔·尼古拉耶维奇今儿早晨有点累，”她对谢廖沙说，端起她那副瘦棱棱的肩膀，领着众人退出了房间。

帕维尔同摊着的威尔弗雷德留在屋里；威尔弗雷德那副委屈的表情，像被锁在一座不安的梦的监狱里。几乎看得见他受折磨的脑子，在闭着的百叶窗似的眼睛上乱撞。

“要不要我叫醒他？”帕维尔问自己。“他在哪儿待着，反正不痛快。”

可他刚俯身要喊一句要紧的唤醒话，门开了，女仆卡佳进来，一脸“交给我，我来对付他”的神气。谢廖沙的狗跟在她后头乱转。它把这位一身厨房气味的胖卡佳当成顶可爱的女人，天真地把她认作了女主人。

“帕维尔·尼古拉耶维奇，”卡佳说，“您总得让我摆早饭的桌子。都——”

帕维尔咆哮着朝她扑去，牙全龇出来，胳膊腿在空中乱张。受辱的老太太退了几步，想在过道里站住。可帕维尔砰的一声当脸摔上门，狗吱的一声逃了，当自己中了枪。

===Original===

The noise half roused Wilfred, yet, in spite of the discomfort of his dreams, he was reluctant to wake. He rolled about on the sofa and buried his face in the cushion, trying to drive his struggling consciousness back into the safe imprisoning corridors of sleep again. Pavel stood over him. “Curse him!” he cried. “He must be doing it on purpose.” He seized the cushion and dragged it from under Wilfred’s head. Wilfred substituted an arm for it. Pavel dragged the arm away, too.

“Awaken, Mistah Chew, awaken!” he shouted. He had looked the English word up in his dictionary.

An obstinate sealed look came into Wilfred’s face, then a look of petulance, then a distortion of the most unspeakable agony, then a light of beautiful resignation, then a recollection of the Reverend Oswald Fawcett. Wilfred opened his eyes, himself again, though rather a melancholy self. “Hrrgh?” he inquired in a strangled snort, disappointed with the waking world.

“Is time of breekfast,” shouted Pavel, feverishly consulting his English notes. “Mistah﻿—quick﻿—awaken.”

Wilfred lay with his eyes wet and callow in their puffed sockets, like newborn kittens in their lair. His gaze was fixed with a tranced expression on a spittoon; one would have said it was his dearest treasure.

Pavel thrust a sheet of paper between Wilfred’s eyes and the spittoon. Wilfred took a minute to adjust his drowsy focus, and then he recognized his own writing on the paper. It was the indenture he had drawn up last night. So it had not been a dream, thought Wilfred, surprised.
